The envelope `size` for a bounded field is **runtime** (`val.encoded_len()`), not a compile-time constant — `emit_table`/`emit_union` branch on `IrFieldType::is_bounded_collection()`; a table with both a fixed and a bounded field is covered by the golden. **v0 deviations:** vector elements are the **inline subset** (prim/enum/bits/struct) — `vector<string>`/`vector<vector>` (nested out-of-line) stay deferred; a **nested `table`/`union` field** (not string/vector) is the only case the `compile_error!` still fires for; conformance-only, no kernel consumer, boot byte-identical. `echo_service`'s `protocol` remains a deferred comment (dispatch codegen is the next milestone). No new `unsafe` (`isl`/`isl-runtime` are `deny(unsafe_code)`). | Protocol dispatch codegen; nested `table`/`union` fields; `vector` of out-of-line elements (D10). |
| D69 | **ISL protocol dispatch codegen (M31).** The capstone of the ISL arc: a `protocol` now generates real code, so `echo_service.isl` — the reference service protocol — generates end to end (protocol + `string:256` tables + strings) from one schema. Closes the protocol part of D10. **What it needed:** the same lossy-IR fix the earlier slices had — `IrMethod` dropped the request/response payload types, so `IrMethodKind` now carries an `IrPayload` (`Empty` | `Type(name)`) per method, and an **inline `struct`/`table` payload is synthesized** into a named type `{Protocol}{Method}{Request\|Response\|Event}` (built by the existing `lower_struct`/`lower_table` and appended to the IR decls, so codegen emits it like any declared type — echo's `Log`/`OnHeartbeat` inline structs become `EchoLogRequest`/`EchoOnHeartbeatEvent`). **What it emits:** a namespace `struct P` with `INTERFACE_ID` (the D11 derivation, already computed) and one ordinal const per method; and three typed dispatch enums — `{P}Incoming` (call + one-way requests), `{P}Outgoing` (call responses), `{P}Event` — each with `method_id`/`encode`/`decode`, decode keyed by the method ordinal (an unknown one is new `WireError::UnknownMethod = 12`), an empty payload a unit variant framing zero bytes. **Boundary:** a protocol binding is the *payload* layer on top of the already-generated channel `MessageHeader` (interface_id/method_id, D54); the *transport* — txn-id matching, async, the channel itself — is the kernel's IPC, not the schema codec, and is intentionally out of scope. **v0 deviations:** dispatch is enum-based, not a server *trait* (a trait needs a caller impl; the enums are directly conformance-testable); conformance-only — the three hand-rolled `const IFACE_ID`/`METHOD_*` blocks in `main.rs` (the IPC-demo, pager, and perf protocols) and D54/D60's hand-shaped `MessageHeader` are now **generatable** but the rewire is a separate, byte-stable-sensitive change; a nested `table`/`union` *field* remains the one `compile_error!` deferral. Boot byte-identical. No new `unsafe`. | Rewire the `main.rs` hand-rolled protocols onto generated bindings; a trait-based server dispatch; nested `table`/`union` fields (the last wire-codegen gap); the non-Rust bindings / fuzzers / ABI-diff family (D10). |
| D70 | **AArch64 EL0 — minimal ring-3 bring-up (M32).** The AArch64 port gains an unprivileged execution level: a user program enters EL0, makes a syscall via `svc`, and its faults are contained — the design-validation the port exists for at the privilege boundary, and the payoff of the `UserContextOps` split M27 held open. `UserContextOps::init_user` (context.rs) seeds a context whose new `user_thread_trampoline` sets `SPSR_EL1`=EL0t (DAIF masked), `ELR_EL1`/`SP_EL0`, and `eret`s to EL0 — the AArch64 twin of the x86 ring-3 entry, reusing the M27 `context_switch` frame verbatim. | D71 | **AArch64 higher-half kernel — the TTBR1/TTBR0 split (M33).** The kernel moves to the high half and runs out of `TTBR1_EL1`; `TTBR0_EL1` now holds only the low half (devices + user). never exercised by any x86 blob); its instrumented `chan_*` arms stay local for the byte-identical gate but are now thin wrappers over the shared `resolve_endpoint`/`build_channel_message`. **Semantics note:** a malformed args struct now fails `Protocol` *before* any rights check on `MapDevice`/`DmaAlloc`; the documented D77/D78 negatives still produce `AccessDenied` (order after decode preserved) — both re-run → exit 65. Verified: aarch64 5/5 exit 33, every verdict line **byte-identical to the D78 baseline** including the dma phys; x86-64 exit 33, serial byte-identical modulo the known nondeterministic lines and one size-derived line (`memmap:` usable-frame count 129705→129700 — the kernel image grew by the dispatch module); 9 new kcore dispatch host tests + 4 decoder/wrapper tests; conformance + schema gate green. | An observer seam on `DispatchEnv` so x86's instrumented channel arms (sinks, switch counts) can migrate; migrating x86's `fs_/pager_/perf_b1_/wait_/user_` handlers onto the dispatcher; ISL args structs for the still-register-passed `PageServe`/`PageSupply`/`Port*`/`DeviceIo*`; `Backing::Device` tracked bookkeeping; retiring the bespoke aarch64 check harness. |

| D80 | **The ring-3 virtio-blk driver — a Rust EL0 program drives the disk (M42).** The driver leg of the ring-3 driver host: a real **`no_std` Rust user program** (`userspace/blk-driver` — not a hand-assembled blob) runs as an EL0 process, maps its device's MMIO window into its own space (`MapDevice`), allocates one DMA page (`DmaAlloc`), drives the **unchanged** memory-safe `tessera-virtio` core through the full modern handshake (reset → ACKNOWLEDGE/DRIVER → VERSION_1 → FEATURES_OK → queue setup → DRIVER_OK), reads sector 0, verifies `TESSERAV`, reports via `DebugWrite`, and exits (`ring3-blk: OK`, D58). This makes the layering claim real: the transport core's only device surface is the 2-method `Mmio` trait, so the EL0 impl is the kernel's `DeviceRegisters` twin (volatile access on the capability-granted window) and **zero lines of virtio logic moved or changed**. The whole working set — split-virtqueue rings for size 8 (`Layout::for_size`, 222 B), request header, status byte, 512-B sector — lives in the **one** DMA page, so the driver's device-visible addresses are `dma_phys + offset` throughout. Syscall args are ISL wire structs encoded with the generated `device_abi` bindings (`tessera_isl_runtime::encode`, buffers on the tracked user stack) — no hand-rolled serialization crosses the boundary. **Zero new syscalls, zero new hooks, zero kcore mechanism**: everything routes through the shared dispatcher (D79). Infrastructure firsts: `tessera_user_binary` generalized with an `arch` table (x86 roottask output verified bit-identical); the aarch64 user ELF is embedded via the root-task genrule pattern (`--cfg=has_blk_driver`, Bazel-only; the cargo inner loop prints an explicit `ring3-blk: skipped` line); and `kcore::elf::parse(Machine::AArch64)` gets its **first caller** — `ring3_blk_check` maps/populates/protects each PT_LOAD (map_anonymous rw → copy_in → protect_range to W^X-final rights → icache sync on exec segments) before `spawn_user` at the ELF entry. Negative (D58): granting the Device cap without `Rights::MAP` → `MapDevice` denied → the driver itself reports `0xdead…(stage 1, AccessDenied)` and exits → check fails → exit 65 (the FATAL line surfaces the driver's report for diagnosis). **v0 deviations:** polled completion with a bounded spin (no timer, no interrupts at EL0); single request; one queue; the driver's failure channel is one `DebugWrite` u64 (stage|cause codes); Bazel-only embed (mirrors the root task, D42); panics exit rather than unwind (panic handler reports + `ProcessExit` — the boot can never hang on the driver). No ABI, rights-catalog, or budget changes. AArch64 boot output gains the one verdict line + the kernel-image-size memmap shift; x86-64 byte-identical. | Serving clients over channel IPC — the driver **host** leg (a client process requests a sector read over the M38 channel, the driver performs it and replies); ring-3 interrupt delivery (D36); multi-request/multi-queue; a dedicated DMA/memory capability + IOMMU apertures; write requests; retiring the in-kernel aarch64 blk/net drivers once the ring-3 host serves; x86 parity for MapDevice/DmaAlloc. |

| D81 | **The ring-3 driver host — the EL0 blk driver serves a client over channel IPC (M43).** Completes the D78 vision: the D80 driver stays resident and serves a **separate client process**. The client (`userspace/blk-client`, a second `no_std` Rust EL0 program holding **only a channel endpoint** — no device or DMA capability) sends a `BlockReadRequest{sector}` with `ChannelCall`; the driver `ChannelRecv`s it, performs the virtio read of the requested sector as queue request #1 (after its sector-0 self-test as request #0), and `ChannelReply`s a `BlockReadReply` carrying the sector's first 64 bytes; the client verifies `TESSERAV` and reports (`ring3-host: OK`, D58 — the magic crossed device → DMA → driver → channel → client). The payload is **`tessera.driver.block` (`block_driver.isl`) — the first user↔user ISL protocol**: encoded/decoded only by the two EL0 programs; the kernel transports it opaquely and has no binding wired (deliberately no kcore SCHEMAS/BUILD entry). **Call-buffer semantics extended (kcore::dispatch)**: `ChannelCall`'s `ChannelMsgArgs` inline descriptor is now **symmetric** — request source before the call, reply destination after (copy-out clamped to `inline_len`), returning the reply length; the descriptor is decoded once, before blocking. M38's round-trip is bit-unaffected (empty reply → 0 copied, result 0) and x86's local chan arms don't route through it; three kcore host tests pin copy/truncate/empty. **Found and fixed a real kernel bug (karch-aarch64)**: `SP_EL0` was never saved/restored — the trap frame's `sp` slot recorded the *kernel* SP and `RESTORE_FRAME` never wrote `SP_EL0`, so an EL0 thread resuming after another EL0 thread ran would continue on the other thread's stack pointer. Every earlier EL0 program dodged it (blobs never touch SP after a resume; D80's driver ran alone); the first two interleaving stack-using Rust programs hit it — the driver resumed on the client's SP value (a *valid* VA in its own same-layout space, so no fault: it silently read zeroed memory and its frames overwrote the real buffer). Diagnosed by physical-frame forensics (the request bytes were present in the right frame at recv-return, replaced by frame-pointer spills by report time); fixed in `SAVE_FRAME`/`RESTORE_FRAME` (`mrs/msr sp_el0` through the frame's `sp` slot — for EL1→EL1 traps a harmless save/rewrite of dead state), and `TrapFrame::sp` now documents the field as `SP_EL0`. Negative (D58): the client's endpoint granted without `Rights::WRITE` → its `ChannelCall` denied → the client itself reports `0xdead…(stage 0x12, AccessDenied)` → exit 65. **v0 deviations:** the driver serves exactly one request then parks (a resident serve loop + service lifetime are deferred); `interface_id`/`method_id` ride as zeros (wire method discipline deferred); the 64-byte reply prefix stands in for full-sector transfer (needs a shared-memory grant); one queue, polled. No syscall-ABI ordinal changes; the `ChannelCall` return-value change is a documented behavioral extension of the v0 register ABI exercised only by in-tree programs. AArch64 boot output: the `ring3-blk:` verdict is subsumed by `ring3-host:` (the driver leg still fully exercised via its self-test) + the kernel-size memmap shift (second embedded ELF); x86-64 byte-identical modulo one size-derived memmap line (kcore dispatch grew). | A resident serve loop + multi-request/multi-client service lifetime; shared-memory grants for full-sector (and multi-sector) transfer; wire `interface_id`/`method_id` discipline — ~~`method_id`~~ (done, D129: a receive reports the method that arrived, so a server can dispatch), `interface_id` still unused; ring-3 interrupts (D36); ~~write requests~~ (done, D129); a net driver host; retiring the in-kernel aarch64 drivers once the host serves boot's own needs; x86 parity. |

| D82 | **The resident serve loop — the EL0 driver serves 2 clients × 2 reads (M44).** The driver host stops being a one-shot proof: after its self-test it loops on a new **`ChannelReplyRecv` (ordinal 25, append-only)** — reply to the current caller and receive the next request in one operation, the classic server-loop primitive — serving **four reads for two client processes** (the one client ELF spawned twice, distinguished by the spawn arg reaching `_start` in `x0`), each requesting sectors 0 and 1 and verifying per-sector magics (`TESSERAV`/`TESSERA2` — the test disk now stamps sector 1). Verdict `ring3-host: OK — resident EL0 driver served 4 reads for 2 clients over IPC (sectors 0+1 verified)`; after the final reply the driver parks inside the receive (the resident shape) and teardown reaps it. The syscall wires `Executive::reply_receive` — which existed since the executive landed but had **no syscall and no test** — into the shared dispatcher with the D81 symmetric-buffer convention (reply out of `inline_ptr`, next request back into it, descriptor decoded once before blocking); its first kcore tests land with it (immediate-dequeue, truncation, rights-denial). **Second real scheduler fix found by the milestone:** the exit path did `terminate` + `yield_to_boot`, ending the whole run at the FIRST thread exit — harmless while every check had one exiting thread, wrong for a service run (client 2 was abandoned Ready; the XOR sink held only client 1's report). New **`Scheduler::exit_current`** terminates the current thread and switches to the next ready thread, booting only when nothing is runnable (unit-tested; the aarch64 exit path uses it — byte-identical for every prior check since their remaining threads were Blocked, proven by the boot diff). **Multi-reporter sink:** the hook's `DebugWrite` arm now XOR-accumulates `EL0_SINK_LOG` (value-identical for single-writer checks); each client reports the disk magic rotated by its id, so the expected value needs BOTH reports and stays magic-dependent (identical reports would cancel). Confinement negative (D58): client 2's endpoint granted without `Rights::WRITE` → its calls denied → its `0xdead…(0x12)` report perturbs the XOR → exit 65 — the second client is provably load-bearing. **Documented limit:** a channel holds a **single outstanding caller** (`call` overwrites the one `pending_caller`; replies FIFO on the shared client end), so multi-client on one channel is sound only sequentially — which the cooperative direct-handoff regime guarantees here (client 1 completes before client 2 is scheduled). The full service model — per-client channels + port-based select, service shutdown/lifetime, shared-memory grants for full sectors, interrupts (D36) — remains the deferred column. x86-64 boot byte-identical (`ChannelReplyRecv` is `ENOSYS` on its pre-substrate demo handler; the D52 handler's catch-all covers it). | Per-client channels + port-based select (true concurrent multi-client); service shutdown/lifetime; shared-memory grants for full-sector transfer; wire interface/method-id discipline (`method_id` done, D129); ring-3 interrupts (D36); ~~write requests~~ (done, D129); the net driver host on the same scaffold; retiring the in-kernel drivers; x86 parity. |

| D83 | **One device host, both driver classes — ring-3 blk + net (M45).** Closes the Stage-0 scope sentence "one driver host running virtio-blk and virtio-net class drivers": the resident EL0 host (renamed `userspace/blk-driver` → **`userspace/device-host`**, cfg `has_blk_driver` → `has_ring3_host` — it now IS the device host) maps **both** devices' MMIO windows by capability (blk cap at handle 0, net cap at handle 1, endpoint at 2), allocates a DMA page per device, and self-tests both from ring 3: the sector-0 read AND a **real ARP round-trip with the SLIRP gateway** — the EL0 twin of the in-kernel `net_check` (same unchanged `tessera-virtio` core: `Net::init` two-queue modern handshake, config-space MAC, post-rx-first ordering, per-queue notify), packed into **one** DMA page (rx rings @0x000, tx rings @0x100, rx buffer, ARP frame — the in-kernel check uses four pages). On ARP success the host `DebugWrite`s the resolved gateway MAC under an "AR" tag — its own contribution to the XOR sink, so the expected value now requires the two clients' rotated disk magics AND the net report (`52:55:0a:00:02:02`, SLIRP-deterministic, enforced by the 5/5 boot gate); then it serves the blk clients exactly as before. Verdict: `ring3-host: OK — resident EL0 host drove blk+net (4 reads for 2 clients, ARP from EL0)`. **Gating:** the host check now needs both devices — kmain gates it on the new `net_device_base` beside the blk gate, with an explicit `ring3-host: skipped (no network device attached)` (the per-device boot tests hit it); a NEW `//tools/qemu:ring3_host_boot_aarch64_test` attaches disk + NIC and requires exit 33 + the host marker, so the full path is CI-covered. Negative (D58): the net Device capability granted without `Rights::MAP` → the host's net `MapDevice` denied → it reports stage `0x21`/AccessDenied and exits → the abandoned clients park, the sink mismatches → exit 65. **Deviations:** net is self-test only (no net service protocol to clients yet — a net user↔user ISL contract is the follow-on); single ARP exchange, polled (D36); the SLIRP-MAC expectation is a QEMU-environment constant (real hardware would verify differently). Zero ABI changes. x86-64 byte-identical. | A net service protocol (user↔user ISL) + serving net requests to clients; per-client channels + port select; service shutdown/lifetime; shared-memory grants; ring-3 interrupts (D36); ~~write requests~~ (done, D129); retiring the in-kernel drivers once the host serves boot's needs; x86 parity. |

| D84 | **Ring-3 interrupt delivery — the EL0 driver's read is woken by a real device IRQ (M46).** Closes the ring-3 leg of the interrupt deferral (D36): the device host's blk sector read no longer spins but **parks on its device interrupt**, woken by the virtio-blk completion IRQ routed GIC → kernel bridge → kcore port → the parked EL0 driver. The protocol is mask-on-deliver: the kernel's `virtio_irq_hook` (registered via the already-present `set_device_irq_hook`, wired only around the host `run()`) **disables the INTID** and `port_signal`s the driver's port; the woken driver **acks the device** through its own mapped window (`Blk::ack_interrupt`), then re-arms the line via a new **`IrqComplete` (ordinal 26, append-only)** syscall gated on `Rights::MAP` + a wired INTID — storm-safe for edge and level sources alike. `PortWait` (ordinal 18, long-defined) **joins the shared dispatcher** (v0 returns the coalesced pending count; a single binding per port needs no source/signal demux) — its block/wake ride kcore's existing `port_wait`/`port_signal`, which get their first end-to-end ring-3 exercise. Interrupt discovery is **parsed from the device tree**, no platform constants: `virtio_mmio_regions` now reads each node's GIC-SPI `interrupts` cell (INTID = SPI + 32), threaded onto the resource graph (`DeviceNode.intid`); a missing interrupt on the blk node is a FATAL (err 190), never a silent poll downgrade. Two supporting changes: EL0 now runs with **IRQ unmasked** (`SPSR` `0x340`, was `0x3c0` — a device interrupt must be takeable while the driver runs at EL0; every prior EL0 program was pure-cooperative and this is transparent, since the handler wakes waiters by queueing them Ready, preserving cooperative interleavings — proven byte-identical); and an **interrupt pump** (the boot idle loop `halt_until_interrupt`s and re-runs the scheduler until the check completes, so a completion that lands after every thread parked is not orphaned). **Found and fixed a real `reply_receive` bug:** its immediate-dequeue path (a next request already queued) returned the request but never **woke the just-replied caller**, who would sleep forever with its reply queued — unreachable while requests only arrived through direct handoffs, first hit when interrupt-driven serving let requests queue while the server waited on its device; unit-tested. Negative (D58): omitting the INTID wiring → the driver's `IrqComplete` is denied → it reports stage `0x31`/AccessDenied → exit 65. **Scope:** the interrupt-driven read is the driver's **self-test**; the client-serve path stays polled and single-client, because a server parking on a device interrupt mid-request breaks the one-shared-endpoint / single-outstanding-caller lock-step (D82) — concurrent interrupt-driven serving needs per-client channels + a port-based select, the deferred service model. x86-64 byte-identical (`IrqComplete` is `ENOSYS` on its pre-substrate handler; `PortWait` keeps its local x86 arm). | Per-client channels + port-based select so interrupt-driven serving scales to concurrent clients; the net device's interrupt (rx-driven receive); interrupt objects as first-class capabilities with ring-3 `PortCreate`/`PortBind`; per-INTID hooks (one hook demuxes today); x86 `IrqComplete`; retiring the in-kernel drivers. |

| D85 | **Per-client channels + a port-based select — concurrent interrupt-driven serving (M47).** Lands the deferred service model and lifts D84's single-client/polled-serve limit: the EL0 device host now serves **two clients concurrently, every read interrupt-driven**. Two changes make it work. (1) **Per-client channels**: `pending_caller` is a field of `Endpoint`, one slot each, so giving every client its own channel fixes D82's reply-crossing *structurally* rather than by scheduling luck. (2) **A port-based select**: a message arriving on an endpoint now `port_signal`s that endpoint's **object id** with a new `SIGNAL_MESSAGE`, so one port bound to N server endpoints is a working select; `Port::drain` already returns the matched binding's own source, which names *which* client has work. `PortWait` gains an out-record — `arg1 = 0` keeps D84's count-only shape byte-identical, non-zero writes an ISL **`PortEventRecord`** (`port_event.isl`, 32 B: source/signal/pending) into user memory, the value the server maps back to an endpoint handle. **Found and fixed the milestone's real bug — a reply that strands its server:** `Executive::reply` blocks the replier as part of its handoff, which is correct only when the next `call` on that same endpoint hands back; a server woken by a *port* instead is stranded forever. Added **`ChannelReplyContinue` (ordinal 27, append-only)** / `reply_and_continue`, which readies the caller and leaves the server runnable; unit-tested. **Also found and fixed a latent D84 defect**: the boot interrupt pump `wfi`'d with `DAIF.I` still set — the boot context masks IRQs at reset and only a thread trampoline or EL0's `SPSR` ever unmasks — so an interrupt arriving once every thread had parked could never be *taken*, and returning from a thread switch re-masks, so unmasking once before the loop is not enough. D84 passed only because a client thread happened to be running when its single interrupt landed; a driver parked with nothing else runnable was never woken. The pump now unmasks around each `halt_until_interrupt`. Negative (D58): omit client B's `port_bind` → B's requests never signal the service port → the host never selects B → B blocks forever and the sink carries only net ⊕ client A → exit 65. x86-64 byte-identical modulo the size-derived `memmap:` line (`ChannelReplyContinue` is `ENOSYS` on its pre-substrate handler). | Fairness in `drain` (lowest bound index wins today, so a saturating client can starve its peers); ring-3 `PortCreate`/`PortBind` (ports are still boot-granted) and handle discovery (numbers remain install-order constants); a net service protocol; x86 parity for the select. |
| D86 | **The third architecture — RISC-V 64 (RVA23), and the scope change that put it next (M48).** Two decisions land together. **(1) Scope.** `docs/hardware/01` and `docs/roadmap/01` now carry a five-family architecture matrix (x86-64, AArch64, RISC-V 64 targeting **RVA23**, ARM 32-bit, RISC-V 32-bit), an **emulation-first** rule that applies at *every* stage rather than only during core-bet validation, and a **modern-hardware-only** policy that puts legacy architectures, legacy firmware boot paths, and compatibility-only platform devices permanently out of scope. 32-bit moves from "a product decision rather than a core goal" to a supported target, which is a change to the *core and the ABI* (pointer width varies; byte order does not) and is therefore sequenced into Stage 1 rather than taken here. **(2) The port.** `kernel/karch-riscv64` + `kernel/kernel-riscv64` implement the porting layer for S-mode under SBI firmware on `qemu-system-riscv64 -M virt -cpu rva23s64`: NS16550A console, SiFive test finisher as `PlatformExit` (33/65, matching both siblings), a single `stvec` trap vector with `scause` decode, **Sstc** (`stimecmp`) as the tick — no fallback to the SBI timer path, so a machine without it is reported rather than silently degraded — the PLIC's claim/complete handshake, **Sv39** three-level paging with per-section W^X over the kernel image, and the context switch. It runs the **unchanged** `arch-conformance` battery: 9/9 pass, boot exits 33, five consecutive boots byte-identical. x86-64 and AArch64 are untouched — the x86 kernel ELF and the AArch64 flat image are byte-identical to the pre-change baseline (the ISO differs only in xorriso's embedded timestamps; its boot output is identical). **What the third port bought, concretely:** it found three places the abstraction had been described by two similar machines rather than defined — RISC-V has *no device-memory page attribute* (an I/O range is a PMA property the page table cannot override, so `PageFlags::device` carries no PTE bit and ordering comes from explicit `fence`s), *no architectural report of the timer frequency* (`counter_hz` answers `None`, the trait's documented calibrate-instead case, because RISC-V publishes it in the device tree), and *no serializing instruction* (`counter_serialized` is a `fence`, which orders memory but not retirement — recorded below, not papered over). It is also the first architecture where `sync_instruction_cache` is load-bearing rather than ceremonial: `fence.i` is architecturally required, which is precisely why that operation was put in the porting layer. Separately, wiring the port surfaced that `//:all_srcs` — the explicit list the tier-0 SPDX/license/unsafe gates walk — **silently omitted `//kernel/virtio`**, so that crate had never been gated; the list now includes it and both new packages, and the unsafe gate immediately did its job on the new code. **v0 deviations:** the kernel is **identity-mapped**, not higher-half (`USER_ADDRESS_MAX` still reports Sv39's real boundary, so nothing downstream encodes the temporary layout); **no ring 3** — `UserContextOps` is deliberately not implemented, so a user thread cannot be spawned and that is a compile error rather than a stub; **no drivers** (virtio-mmio transports are present on the machine and unused); **single hart** (D8) — `fence.i` and `sfence.vma` are local, and cross-hart publication needs the IPIs SMP brings; **no `hw_random`** (the Zkr `seed` CSR needs an M-mode grant this port does not negotiate, so the honest answer is `None`); the timer's `TIMEBASE_HZ` is the virtual platform profile's constant rather than the device tree's `timebase-frequency`, on the same footing as the hard-coded UART base; and **no budget measurement on this port**, because the counter read is not truly serialized. | Higher-half split (the AArch64 D71 shape); ring 3 (`UserContextOps`, `sscratch` as the per-hart kernel stack — and therefore as per-thread state the switch must preserve, per D81); the virtio driver stack on this machine; SMP with cross-hart `fence.i`/`sfence.vma` IPIs; read `timebase-frequency` and `seed`-CSR availability from the device tree; a serialized counter read before any budget is claimed here. |
| D87 | **The x86-64 port's legacy interrupt and timer path, and its BIOS boot, are now tracked debt (policy consequence of D86).** `docs/hardware/01` "Modern Hardware Only" names the 8259 PIC, the 8253/8254 PIT, and PC BIOS as permanently out of scope. `karch-x86_64/src/timer.rs` programs both the PIC and the PIT — it says so in its own header, as "the minimal honest timer tick" — and `kernel/image` builds a BIOS-bootable ISO (`limine-bios-cd.bin`). Neither was wrong when written: each was the smallest thing that worked, and the module confines itself to one file precisely so the swap stays local. What changes is their status: they are no longer unremarkable, they are a deviation with a due date, recorded here so the policy is not quietly contradicted by the oldest port. Nothing in the other two ports is affected — AArch64 uses the GIC and the generic timer, RISC-V the PLIC and Sstc, both of which are the modern mechanisms the policy names. | Replace the PIC with the local APIC + I/O APIC and the PIT with TSC-deadline (or the HPET where TSC-deadline is absent), keeping the tick behind `TimerControl` so no caller changes; make the UEFI boot path the tested one and drop the BIOS artifacts from the image. Both land with the SMP milestone, which needs the APIC anyway. |
| D88 | **The kernel core is word-size-generic, and a build proves it (M49).** The two 32-bit families in the D86 matrix are not a third instance of the porting work — they are the first time **pointer width varies**, which is a change to the core and the ABI rather than to a port. This does that work *before* either port, so the port bring-up is not also a core refactor. **What actually broke:** compiling every architecture-independent crate for `riscv32imac-unknown-none-elf` produced exactly **three** errors, all the same one — `core::sync::atomic::AtomicU64` **does not exist** below a 64-bit atomic width, and `console`/`trace`/`vm` all named it. Everything else compiled, which is the useful half of the finding: the core was already largely width-clean because it spells addresses `u64` rather than `usize`. **The fix** is `kcore::atomic::AtomicU64`, one type with two implementations: a `#[repr(transparent)]` inlining delegate where the target has a 64-bit atomic (so the three existing ports are unaffected — verified by boot output, below), and two `AtomicU32` halves with a **retry-on-high-half** protocol where it does not. That protocol is correct *here* specifically because every value it holds is monotonic or write-once, so the high half moves only on a carry; a seqlock would be the general answer and is the wrong one, because the writers are **reentrant** (`set_current` runs inside `Scheduler::switch_to`, which the timer tick reaches through `on_tick`) and a seqlock's odd/even sequence cannot survive being interrupted by another writer — the same reason `crate::trace`'s header rules out a `SpinLock`. The split path is **always compiled and host-tested** (5 new tests), so the 32-bit protocol is exercised from a 64-bit machine rather than trusted until a port runs it. What it deliberately does not provide is a linearizable 64-bit RMW: `fetch_add`'s carry is a second operation, recorded at the type rather than hidden. **The silent half.** Compiling is not correctness: `u64 as usize` compiles everywhere and truncates on half the matrix. Running clippy's `cast_possible_truncation` *against the 32-bit target* found the real ones, and three of them are bugs on **every** target, not just 32-bit — `Handle::from_raw(ep_handle as u32)` and its `port_handle` twin let `0x1_0000_0005` name handle 5 (now `handle_from_arg`, which rejects rather than truncates); `MessageHeader::from_wire` silently dropped flag bits above 32 (now returns `Result`); and the ELF loader truncated a 64-bit `e_phoff` from an untrusted image into a plausible in-range offset (now a checked conversion). The caller-supplied length casts became `saturating_len` + bound check, so an oversize request is *capped* rather than wrapped into a small one. **The gate.** `//kernel/width-conformance` is a build-only target — not a kernel, never booted — that compiles the architecture-independent crates for a 32-bit platform on every `bazel build //...`, plus `const` assertions that the ABI sizes and the 64-bit counter do not move with the word size. It needed a new `platform_library` transition rule, because a host-platform build *skips* incompatible targets and an unwrapped riscv32 library would have silently never been built. Negative-checked: reverting one `use` to `core`'s `AtomicU64` fails the gate while `//kernel/kernel-riscv64` still builds clean. **Verification:** 35/35 tests (214 kcore, +5), lint and fmt clean, all three ports boot exit 33 with **byte-identical verdict output**; the binaries themselves differ because real checks were added, and AArch64's size-derived `memmap:` line shifts by one page for the same reason. **v0 deviations:** the gate covers **RISC-V 32 only** — `armv7a-none-eabi` joins when its triple is added, and it has 64-bit atomics via `ldrexd`, so it exercises the *other* branch of the shim; `cast_possible_truncation` is run manually against the 32-bit target rather than denied at the crate root, so a new truncating cast is caught by the audit rather than by the build; and the remaining provably-bounded casts (`FRAME_SIZE as usize`, a percentile index) are left as-is rather than carrying an `allow` each. | Add the ARM 32-bit triple to the gate; promote `cast_possible_truncation` to a denied lint on the architecture-independent crates once the provably-safe sites carry justifications, so the audit becomes a build gate too; a linearizable 64-bit RMW (or a documented ban on needing one) when SMP makes the split `fetch_add`'s carry race reachable between harts rather than only between a thread and its own interrupt handler. |
| D89 | **RISC-V 32 — the fourth port, and the first at a 32-bit word size (M50).** Boots green on `qemu-system-riscv32 -M virt`: Sv32 paging, the S-mode trap vector, an Sstc tick, the PLIC, the context switch, 9/9 conformance cases, exit 33, five boots identical. Its value is not a fourth machine — it is the **first port where `usize` is not 64 bits**, so it is the first execution of the word-size work D88 did in the abstract. **What Sv32 actually changes** (it is not Sv39 with a level removed): a PTE carries a 22-bit physical page number, so the architecture addresses **34 bits of physical memory behind a 32-bit virtual address space** — physical is genuinely *wider than a pointer*, which is why `PhysAddr` is a fixed 64-bit type everywhere and why this port must state a limit rather than assume one. It does: the identity access window is a 32-bit pointer, so `build_kernel_space` **rejects a memory map reaching past 4 GiB**, once, where an error can still be returned, and every narrowing in the module is exact because of that single check. Also: a 10-bit index (1024-entry tables, 4 MiB superpages); `scause`'s interrupt bit at **31, not 63** — a vector that tested 63 would classify the timer tick as a fatal exception; a timer compare split across **two CSRs** (`stimecmp`/`stimecmph`) whose write order is load-bearing, since a new low half paired with an old high half can be a deadline already past and fire a spurious interrupt; a `time` counter that needs the same retry-on-high-half read the D88 split atomic uses, this time in hardware; and a `u64` return value split across the `a0`/`a1` register pair. **The bug it found immediately**, and the reason a port is worth more than an audit: the boot glue declared the firmware handoff `extern "C" fn kernel_main(dtb: u64)`. On a 32-bit ABI a `u64` parameter is passed in a **register pair**, so the kernel read a valid low half beside whatever `a1` held and carried a corrupt device-tree address into the reader. It surfaced as a garbage memory-map line and then as `KError::InvalidMapping` — because the 4 GiB window check caught the impossible `ram_end` before anything was mapped. Two mechanisms working: the gate that refused, and a port that ran. **Shared rather than copied:** the `virt` platform's PLIC, NS16550A and test finisher are identical at both word sizes, so they moved to a new `kernel/karch-riscv-common` before a second copy existed. The dividing line is stated in that crate: **a device is shared, a CSR is not** — unmasking `sie` stays per-port precisely because `in(reg) <u64>` does not compile on a 32-bit target, so a shared CSR helper would have to be generic over the thing that makes the two ports two ports. The 64-bit port's boot output is byte-identical across that extraction. **Verification:** 36/36 tests, lint and fmt clean, x86-64/AArch64/RISC-V 64 boot output byte-identical. Negative checks: masking `sie.STIE` → exit 65 at the timer check; returning a non-zero high half from the sentinel routine → the instruction-cache case FAILs → exit 65. A third negative **did not bite and is recorded as such**: deleting the sentinel's `li a1, 0` still passes, because `a1` already holds zero at that call site — the instruction is there because the ABI requires it, not because this test would catch its absence. **v0 deviations:** no ring 3 (`UserContextOps` deliberately unimplemented, so a user thread is a compile error rather than a stub); no higher-half split (identity-mapped, so the device range sits in the nominal user half); **`USER_ADDRESS_MAX` is a policy number, not an architectural one** — Sv32's address space is flat with no sign-extension hole, so 2 GiB is chosen to coincide with the base of RAM and is documented as a choice; no drivers; single hart (D8); the `timebase-frequency` is the platform constant rather than the device tree's; and no budget measurement, for the same unserialized-counter reason as the 64-bit port, made slightly worse by the two-CSR read. | Ring 3 (`sscratch` as the per-hart kernel stack, and therefore per-thread state the switch must preserve — D81); the higher-half split, which also moves the device range out of the user half; drivers on this machine; SMP; a sentinel with a non-zero high half so the instruction-cache case checks the return ABI as well as the cache. |
| D90 | **ARM 32-bit — the fifth port, and the architecture matrix is complete (M51).** Boots green on `qemu-system-arm -M virt -cpu cortex-a15`: LPAE paging, the eight-entry vector table, the generic timer through CP15, the GICv2, the context switch, 9/9 conformance cases, exit 33, five boots identical. Every family in `docs/hardware/01` now has a port that passes the same battery. **It needed no core work**, which was the point of doing D88 and D89 first: its word size is the one RISC-V 32 proved out, and it has 64-bit atomics, so it exercises the *delegating* branch of `tessera_karch::atomic::AtomicU64` rather than the split one — the first time that branch has been exercised at a 32-bit pointer width. **The bug that cost the milestone its debugging time, and the reason ports are worth building:** on AArch64 descriptor bit 54 is `UXN` — *unprivileged* execute never, which a kernel page sets to keep user mode out. On ARMv7-A LPAE **the same bit in the same position of the same format is plain `XN`**, applying to both privilege levels. Copying the AArch64 formula made the kernel's own text unexecutable; the first fetch after the MMU came on took a permission fault, which vectored to a table that was *also* unexecutable, and the machine spun on a recursive prefetch abort with nothing on the wire. `qemu -d int` named it in one run (`IFSR 0x20f IFAR 0x400202e4`, then an endless abort at PC 0xc). Two changes came out of it: the encoding is now `XN` iff not executable and `PXN` iff user, with the difference documented at the constant; and **`VBAR` is installed before the MMU is enabled**, not after, so a fault raised *by the enable itself* is a reported trap rather than a silent hang — the reset vector base is 0, which after the enable is device memory and unexecutable. **Boot protocol, third of three:** handed an ELF, QEMU treats an ARM image as bare metal and passes no device tree (`r2 = 0`, measured). Handed a *raw* binary it takes the Linux path and writes a stub that puts the tree in `r2`. So this port ships a flat image like AArch64 — but where AArch64 needs an `Image` header to signal it, here the file simply not being an ELF is the signal. **Shared rather than copied:** the `virt` board is the same board at both Arm widths, so the PL011, the GICv2 and the MMIO helpers moved to `kernel/karch-arm-common` before a second copy existed, on the same rule the RISC-V side uses — *a device is shared, a system register is not*. AArch64's boot output is byte-identical across that extraction. **Toolchain:** rules_rust 0.71.3 does not know the `armv7a` CPU architecture and fails the whole extension rather than the one triple when it is listed in `extra_target_triples`; `rust.repository_set` takes the platform constraints explicitly and is the supported way to register a triple the built-in mapping has not caught up with. **Verification:** 37/37 tests, lint and fmt clean, and all four existing ports byte-identical. Negative checks: leaving the timer interrupt masked at the GIC → exit 65 at the tick check; removing the W^X refusal → 2 conformance failures → exit 65. **v0 deviations:** no ring 3 (`UserContextOps` deliberately unimplemented); no higher-half split, so — as on Sv32 — `USER_ADDRESS_MAX` is a **policy** number rather than an architectural one and the device range sits in the nominal user half; no drivers; single core (D8); FIQ is routed nowhere; the instruction-cache maintenance steps by a hard-coded 32-byte line rather than reading `CTR`, which is correct on every ARMv7-A implementation but coarser than necessary; and no budget measurement on this port. | Ring 3 (banked-mode entry, and `SP_usr` joining the per-thread state the switch must preserve — D81 in its ARM 32-bit shape); the higher-half split, which LPAE supports through `TTBCR.T1SZ` exactly as AArch64 does through `TTBR1`; drivers on this machine; read the cache line size from `CTR`; SMP. |
| D91 | **The driver framework begins: drivers bind to devices they did not hardcode (M52).** Before this, a driver knew its device by a compiled-in handle number boot happened to install — `BLK_DEVICE_HANDLE = 0`. That is not binding, it is a constant shared between two files, and it fails the moment the machine has its devices in another order, two of a kind, or one fewer. Now a new ring-3 **device manager** (`userspace/device-manager`, the third EL0 program) holds a capability to *every* device boot discovered, and nothing else grants any driver anything; a driver names a **class** over an ISL channel protocol (`api/isl/examples/driver_bind.isl`) and is handed a capability to a device of that class, or told there is none. **Enumeration is why the manager is a program and not a table:** every virtio-mmio node on this machine carries the same device-tree `compatible` string, so the class exists only in a register — the manager maps each capability it holds, reads the magic and `device_id`, and records what it found. That is equally true of PCIe config space, so this is not a virtio quirk. **What deliberately does not cross the wire:** no MMIO base, no length, no interrupt. All three live inside the capability and the kernel reads them from there on `MapDevice`; a driver that could name its own window would not need a capability. The reply's real payload is the transferred handle. **Exclusivity needs no flag:** a transfer is a `take` from the sender's table, so a device can be handed out exactly once — one-driver-per-device is a property of capability conservation, and the manager could not violate it if it tried. **Two things the build taught, both of which the rights model was right about.** First, the initial boot failed `AccessDenied` because `HandleTable::take` requires `Rights::TRANSFER`: *handing a capability to another process is itself a right*, and boot now grants it to the manager and nowhere else. Second, the integrated boot then **deadlocked**, and the diagnosis is worth keeping because the symptom was nothing at all — no fault, no error return, no output, both parties parked. `Executive::reply` hands off to the caller and leaves the replier **Blocked**, which is correct only for a server whose next wake is the next call on that same endpoint, because `call` wakes a peer only if it registered itself via `receive`. A server that replies and loops back to `recv` is Blocked *without being a registered receiver*, so the second request finds no waiting receiver, blocks too, and neither side runs again — the first request succeeds silently, which is what makes it look like a hang rather than a bug. The manager uses **`ChannelReplyContinue` (D85)**, which was built for exactly this shape. This is the second time this trap has been hit (the first is recorded at D85); it is now recorded as a property of `reply` rather than of any one caller. **Verification:** 38/38 tests, lint and fmt clean, 5/5 AArch64 boots deterministic with every prior verdict intact, and the other four ports byte-identical. Negative check: making the manager misclassify block devices → the driver's Block bind returns "no such class" → it refuses to proceed → exit 65 with `0xdead…0040_0101`, proving the driver does not fall back to driving whatever handle 5 happens to be. **v0 deviations** (the manager keeping the MMIO mapping of devices it handed away was one of these and is **closed by D92**): **rights do not narrow on transfer**, so a driver inherits `TRANSFER` and could re-delegate; the *bootstrap* handle numbers are still install-order constants in both programs (what changed is that none of them says which device anything is); binding is by class only — no manifests, no `compatible`-string matching, no PCI IDs; **no restart** (a driver that dies does not re-bind); one class per request and no unbind; and the framework exists on **AArch64 only**, the one port with ring 3. | ~~Rights narrowing on transfer~~ (done, D113); ~~driver manifests as data (class + match keys + required resources) so binding is a match rather than a class lookup~~ (done, D130 — ten inputs, and a refusal that says which one); ~~restart and re-bind under the failure model~~ (done, D127/D128); handle discovery, so even the bootstrap numbers stop being constants; ~~PCIe/ECAM enumeration beside virtio~~ (done, D114); ~~ring 3 on the other ports so the framework is not AArch64-only~~ (done, D111). |
| D92 | **A transferred capability takes its register window with it (M53).** Closes the gap D91 opened. `MapDevice` grants register access *because* the caller holds a device capability; if the capability moves to another process and the window stays behind, the grant has been **copied rather than transferred** — the old holder keeps everything that mattered while the new one believes it has exclusive use. That is not hypothetical here: the device manager must map every device to classify it (the class lives in a register, on virtio-mmio and on PCIe alike), so without revocation the one component that hands out devices is also the one component that retains access to all of them. **What was missing was bookkeeping, not a mechanism.** `map_device_page` mapped straight through the arch layer and recorded nothing, so nothing could be revoked — the window was invisible to the kernel that installed it. A `Process` now carries a small `device_windows` table of `(object, VA)`; `MapDevice` records the window **before** installing the mapping and refuses the map outright if the table is full, because an unrecorded window is exactly the thing that would survive its capability's departure. `build_message_from_args` — the one place a capability moves between address spaces, and the only place holding both the handle table and the space — drains every window for the object it just `take`s and unmaps each. **The one detail that would have been a serious bug:** `unmap_device_page` deliberately **discards** the frame the unmap returns. It names MMIO, not RAM; it never came from the frame allocator, and returning it there would put a device's registers into the pool the kernel serves anonymous memory from. That asymmetry is why this is its own operation rather than a caller of the ordinary unmap path, where "return the frame so the caller can reclaim it" is the whole point. **Verification:** three new `kcore` tests (a window is recorded; a transfer takes the mapping with it; transferring *something else* leaves it alone — the revocation keys on the capability that moved, not on any transfer at all), 38/38 tests, lint and fmt clean. Runtime proof rather than unit-test-only: patching the manager to read a device register **after** the transfer completes faults and the boot exits 65, where the same read *before* the transfer succeeds — so the window is genuinely gone from the live address space, not merely absent from a table. The other four ports are unaffected; x86-64's boot output differs only in the size-derived `memmap:` line, the `Process` table having grown the kernel by five frames. **v0 deviations** (revocation firing on transfer only was one of these and is **closed by D93**): a device mapped more than once has every window revoked together, which is right for exclusivity but means a process cannot keep one mapping and give up another; and `MAX_DEVICE_WINDOWS` is a fixed 8 per process. | Decide whether partial revocation is ever wanted or whether "one capability, one window" should be enforced at `MapDevice`. |
| D93 | **Revocation follows the capability, not the route it left by (M54).** D92 revoked a device's register window when its capability was *transferred*. That made "handed to another process" safe and left the other exits open: a process could **close** its device handle and keep driving the device, which is the same hole reached by a different door. Both routes now revoke, and the rule they share is the correction of a real bug D92 shipped: revocation asks **"does this process still hold a capability naming the device?"**, not "did a handle just leave?". D92 revoked unconditionally on `take`, so a process that legitimately duplicated its capability and handed one copy on would have had the window pulled out from under it while still holding the authority — a driver punished for doing nothing wrong. `HandleTable::holds` answers the question and `Process::revoke_device_windows_unless_held` is the single place both paths go through; `sys_handle_close` reads the object *before* the close, because afterwards the handle names nothing and there would be no way to ask what left. **Process teardown deliberately gets no code**, and the reason is worth recording rather than leaving as an absence: the address space is destroyed with the process, and a device window is **untracked**, so `AddressSpace::teardown` — which walks only the tracked mapping table — cannot hand an MMIO frame to the frame allocator. The window dies with the space and the dangerous outcome (device registers entering the pool the kernel serves anonymous memory from) is structurally unreachable. A test pins that property rather than trusting the reading: it maps a device window, asserts the tracked mapping count is still zero, tears the space down, and asserts the allocator's free list did not grow. **Verification:** four new `kcore` tests (close revokes the last handle's window; close of one of two keeps it; transfer of one of two keeps it — the D92 bug, now a regression test; teardown returns no MMIO frame), 216 kcore tests, 38/38 targets, lint and fmt clean, AArch64 boots 3/3 at 33 and x86-64 verdicts identical. **v0 deviations:** revocation is per-object and all-or-nothing, so a process holding two windows on one device loses both together; `MAX_DEVICE_WINDOWS` is a fixed 8; and nothing revokes a window whose *object* is destroyed out from under a still-open handle, which cannot happen today because a live handle holds a reference. | Decide whether "one capability, one window" should simply be enforced at `MapDevice`, which would make the all-or-nothing question disappear rather than answering it. |
| D94 | **`ChannelMsgArgs` v2: the kernel reports which handles a receive installed (M55).** A receiver could not name the capabilities it had just been given. A handle is an index *and* a generation, and `take` bumps the generation of the slot it vacates — so a capability returning to a table it once lived in comes back at the same index with a **different value**, and any number the receiver remembered is stale by construction. That is the generation counter working as designed; what was missing was somewhere to tell the receiver the answer. Two appended fields, `installed_ptr`/`installed_cap`, are that answer: on a receive the kernel writes back the handle values it installed, and `installed_ptr = 0` opts out (what every send-only caller passes). **They are their own fields rather than a reuse of the send side's `handles_ptr`/`handle_count`** — the shape first tried — because a **call is a send and a receive at once**: that vector is already the request's input transfer list, so a caller that transfers nothing but expects a capability back cannot say so with one pair. Trying it that way made a bind attempt to hand away its own endpoint and fail `AccessDenied`, which is what settled the design. The version gate moves to **2**: a version-1 producer describes a shorter struct, and refusing beats reading one short and inventing the missing fields. **What the change actually cost, which is the part worth recording:** the descriptor is hand-built in **nine places outside Rust** — two AArch64 demo blobs written as raw machine-code bytes (needing new `movz`/`movk`/`str` encodings) and seven x86-64 blobs written as inline-assembly `.long`/`.quad` literals. Missing six of them on the first pass took x86-64's `m16`, `m17` and `driver-restart` demos to `saw_ping=false`, which is a loud and specific failure and found them immediately. A wire golden pins the 88-byte layout. **Verification:** 38/38 tests, all five ports boot with **byte-identical verdicts**. **v0 deviations:** the report is best-effort — a receiver naming an unwritable buffer loses the report, not the capability, because the handle is installed either way; and there is still no way for a *sender* to learn what its transfer became on the far side, which nothing needs yet. | Consider whether `handles_ptr` should have been in/out from the start and this pair is the migration; revisit if a third direction ever appears. |
| D95 | **A driver dies and its replacement takes the device (M56).** The framework's restart claim, proven on its own: one device manager and one minimal block driver (`userspace/blk-probe`) run twice. Incarnation 1 binds the block device, maps it, reads its identifying register and exits; the supervisor tears it down; the device is handed back to the manager; incarnation 2 — a fresh process with no memory of the first — binds and drives the **same transport**. Each incarnation folds its number into its report, so two successful runs cannot look like one counted twice. **The check is deliberately separate** from the resident host's, with no clients, no interrupts and no select loop: three earlier attempts bolted this onto that check and the handover was never the only thing in the picture, which is why they failed for reasons that had nothing to do with restart. **The kernel bug it found, which nothing else could have:** `Scheduler::reap` frees a thread's scheduler slot but leaves the index in its owning `Process`, and `ProcessTable::process_of_thread` answers with the *first* process claiming an index. So a supervisor that reaps a dead service and starts a replacement hands the replacement a **recycled index**, and its syscalls are resolved against the corpse — the dead process's handle table and address space. It surfaces as `AccessDenied` on a pointer the caller can see is perfectly valid, because the replacement's own stack is not mapped in the dead space. Reaping a thread and forgetting it are two halves of one operation, split only because the scheduler and the process table are separate structures; `Process::forget_thread` and `ProcessTable::forget_thread` are the missing half, and a regression test pins the misattribution. Restart is the first thing in the tree that reaps a process's thread and then spawns another, which is why it took until now to appear. **Verification:** 38/38 tests, 5/5 AArch64 boots deterministic, other four ports byte-identical. Two negative checks, both landing on "the second driver asked for Block and was told there is none": never sending the device back, and making the manager refuse to mark a reclaimed device available. **v0 deviations:** the death is a clean exit, not a crash — crash-restart with fault observation is proven separately on x86-64 (D49/D51) and what is new here is that the *device* survives; the supervisor is boot rather than a ring-3 service; the replacement is a different program from the one that died (the claim is that the device is re-bindable by whoever comes next, not that one binary can be relaunched); there is no restart *policy* — no budget, no backoff, no crash counting; and the device was returned by the supervisor rather than reclaimed automatically (**closed by D96**). | A restart policy (budget/backoff) reusing the x86-64 component manager's; and a ring-3 supervisor holding the authority instead of boot. |
| D96 | **A dead driver's devices come back on their own (M57).** D95 left the supervisor responsible for handing a device back by hand, which means a supervisor that forgets costs the machine a device permanently — the only handle to it dies with the process. `Executive::reclaim_devices` moves that into teardown: the kernel walks the dying process's handle table, takes every capability the device graph backs, and sends each to a nominated endpoint. **The supervisor in the boot check no longer mentions the block device at all** — it does not know which devices the driver held and does not need to, which is the whole point. Forgetting is not possible when you are not the one doing it. **Three decisions worth recording.** (1) Reclaim does **not** require `Rights::TRANSFER`. That right governs a *process* handing a capability on; this is the kernel taking one back from a process being destroyed, which no right of the dead process can permit or forbid — otherwise a driver granted a device without TRANSFER could strand it by dying, which is exactly backwards. (2) Each capability travels as a message with **no payload at all**. The kernel does not know, and must not know, what protocol the receiver speaks. (3) That forced a better discriminator than the flag D95 used: **a message carrying a capability to the manager *is* a return**, because a bind request never carries one and nothing else in the protocol hands the manager a device. A body can be forged by any sender; a transferred capability cannot — only something that held the device can hand it on. The `flags` bit and the kernel-side body encoder are both deleted, and the manager keys on the kernel's installed-handle report (D94) instead. **Verification:** 38/38 tests, lint and fmt clean, 3/3 AArch64 boots deterministic, other ports byte-identical. Negative check: making `reclaim_devices` take nothing → the manager never gets the device → the replacement driver is told there is none → exit 65. **v0 deviations:** reclaim is driven by the supervisor calling it during teardown rather than by the kernel noticing a death — a process that dies without anyone tearing it down still strands its devices, so this removes the need to remember *which* devices, not the need to tear down; the destination endpoint is passed per call rather than recorded per process at grant time, so the supervisor still has to know where a given process's devices should go; a full destination queue drops the capability, which is the honest bound on what reclaim can promise; and only MMIO-backed device objects are reclaimed, since they are the only capability class the graph currently backs. | Record the reclaim destination on the process at grant time, so the call site carries no policy; reclaim from the process-exit path itself so an untorn-down death is covered; and widen beyond device objects when a second reclaimable class exists. |
| D97 | **RISC-V 64 moves into the upper half (M58).** D86 shipped this port identity-mapped and named the split as its next milestone; this is that milestone, and it is the prerequisite for ring 3 here rather than a tidiness pass. Sv39 has a **single** translation root, so a process's tables have to carry the kernel too — which they can only do without colliding if the kernel lives somewhere no user program can be. The kernel image now links at `0xffff_ffc0_0000_0000 + phys`, physical memory is reached through a direct map at that same base, and `[0, 2^38)` is empty. **The interesting part is that the simplification D86 recorded disappears, and the AArch64 problem appears in a new form.** D86's argument was that RISC-V enters S-mode with `satp` in Bare mode, so there is no window in which the running address and the linked address differ, so no position-independent prologue is needed. Linking high inverts that exactly: with translation off, *no* linked address is valid. So this port now has a boot stub of its own — it zeroes `.bss`, fills one reserved `boot_root` page with 1 GiB gigapages mapping RAM **twice** (identity, so the stub can keep running; and at `DIRECT_MAP_BASE`, where the kernel is about to be), writes `satp`, and jumps high — all before the first Rust instruction. **It must be assembly, and the reason is worth keeping:** a first attempt did the table fill in Rust and hung with no output, because the table-setup helpers took `&mut dyn EarlyConsole` and `&mut dyn FrameSource`, and a trait object's vtable holds **link-time** (high) addresses. Any code path through a `dyn` reference is already unusable while running physically, however carefully the data it touches is addressed. **Three bugs the change surfaced, each a case of an address that used to be two things at once.** (1) `boot_memory_map` fed the frame allocator the linker symbols and the firmware's device-tree pointer as *physical* extents — true while identity-mapped, false now, and it showed up as an arithmetic overflow deep in the page-table walk rather than as anything resembling its cause. A memory map describes physical memory, so both are converted back explicitly. (2) The PLIC and the SiFive test finisher name themselves by physical address, and both ports share that code, so `karch-riscv-common` gains a **device access window** (`set_device_access_base`): zero on the 32-bit port, `DIRECT_MAP_BASE` on the 64-bit one, declared before the console because the panic path exits through the finisher. (3) The real tables are built while the stub's identity map is still what makes a freshly allocated table frame reachable — so `access_base` starts at zero and must move to the direct map at the instant those tables are activated, which is the line between "translation is a formality" and "the kernel lives somewhere". **Verified:** 9/9 conformance cases, exit 33, five consecutive boots byte-identical; 38/38 tests, lint and fmt clean; x86-64, AArch64 and ARM 32-bit artifacts byte-identical, and the RISC-V 32 kernel's boot output byte-identical across the shared-crate change (its binary differs by the window's atomic load, which is the honest cost of sharing). **The split asserts itself at boot rather than being assumed:** the kernel's own physical load address, read as a virtual address, must translate to *nothing*, and its high alias must still resolve — both directions, because a kernel that merely moved would pass a one-sided check. Negative check: leave RAM identity-mapped as well and the boot refuses with `paging: FATAL: the low half is not empty, the split is not real`, exit 65. **Still deviating:** no ring 3 on this port yet (`UserContextOps` remains deliberately unimplemented — a compile error, not a stub); the direct map covers all of RAM read-write with no guard region; the boot root's gigapages are never reclaimed (one page, inside `.bss`); `USER_ADDRESS_MAX` continues to report Sv39's architectural boundary, which is now also the layout's; and the 32-bit port stays identity-mapped, so its device range still sits in the nominal user half. | Ring 3 (`sscratch` as the per-hart kernel stack, and therefore per-thread state the switch must preserve — D81); per-process `satp` roots with ASIDs over the now-empty low half; the same split on RISC-V 32; reclaiming the boot root; a guard region between the direct map and the image. |
| D98 | **U-mode on RISC-V 64 — the third architecture with a privilege boundary (M59).** The port drops to the unprivileged level, serves a syscall, and contains a user fault instead of dying of one. This is what D97's higher-half split was for, and the checks say so directly: the kernel is not merely *elsewhere* now, it is **unreachable**. **`UserContextOps::init_user`** builds the same frame a kernel thread gets, differing only in which trampoline `ra` names and what `s0`-`s2` carry — the difference between a kernel thread and a user thread on this port is four CSR writes and an `sret`. The trap vector learns a privilege boundary: it swaps `sp` with `sscratch` on entry, records the interrupted stack pointer in the frame (the vector cannot leave it in `sp`, having just swapped it away), and writes `sepc` back on the way out. **The invariant that makes the vector's first branch exact is the interesting part: `sscratch` holds a kernel stack top exactly while U-mode is running, and zero at every other instant.** The vector zeroes it immediately after the swap and re-arms it only on the way back out, so a trap taken *inside* the kernel — including one taken while handling another trap — always sees zero and never swaps a live stack out from under itself. Testing `sstatus.SPP` instead would need a scratch register the vector does not have that early. **That invariant also answers D81's question in the negative, structurally.** `sscratch` is per-thread hardware state, so must the context switch preserve it, as AArch64 learned it must for `SP_EL0`? **No** — the value is *derived*, never carried: every path that leaves for U-mode (the fresh-thread trampoline, the vector's return) writes it from the stack pointer it is standing on, which is by construction the current thread's own kernel stack. There is no instant at which a stale value is readable, so there is nothing to save. The rule from D81 is unchanged; this register escapes it by never being state that outlives a transition. **`prepare_resume` implements half its contract and says why the other half is refused:** installing the address space is real (one root, one `satp`, so a switch is a write), but publishing the kernel stack here would *arm* the swap slot while the kernel is still running, and a trap taken between that and the `sret` would run on a stack the interrupted kernel code was already using. The exits to U-mode arm it themselves. **Proven at boot** (`umode:` verdicts, D58-gated, in dependency order): entering U-mode and returning at all — two syscalls, a value round-tripped through the kernel *and through the user stack* with a clobber in between, so neither a stale register nor an unmapped stack can pass; **W^X at the unprivileged level** — a store into the read-execute code page takes a store page fault, the address checked against the page rather than assumed; and **the privilege boundary** — a user load of the direct-map base takes a load page fault, because no kernel page carries `U`. The user program is position-independent by construction: it reads its own address with `auipc` and never refers to a kernel-linked symbol, which is the standing trap with in-kernel ring-3 blobs. **Three negative checks, each a separate patch-back:** dropping `.user()` from the **code** mapping → the program cannot even be fetched → check 6 → exit 65; dropping it from the **stack** mapping → same → exit 65 (this one only bites *because* the program was made to use its stack — before that the stack mapping was decoration, and the negative check is what proved it); and removing the handler's `frame.sepc += 4` → the `ecall` re-executes forever → **the boot hangs rather than failing**, which is recorded as what it is: the architecture does not advance `sepc`, deliberately, so a handler can restart an instruction, and a kernel that forgets gets a livelock rather than a verdict. **Verified:** 38/38 tests, lint/fmt clean, 9/9 conformance still green, exit 33, five boots byte-identical; x86-64, AArch64, ARM 32-bit and RISC-V 32 artifacts unchanged. The smoke script now greps for the U-mode marker as well as the alive marker — the exit status already gates a *failing* check, but not a check that stopped running. **v0 deviations:** U-mode runs in the **kernel's own address space**, with the user pages mapped into the (now empty) low half and every kernel page lacking `U` — a real boundary in one table, but **no per-process root** yet, so `prepare_resume`'s `satp` write is implemented and unexercised; a **hand-written blob**, not an ELF; a **two-call ABI** (`log`/`exit`) in the boot glue, **not** kcore's syscall substrate; **cooperative** — a user fault or exit abandons the thread mid-trap and its kernel stack with it, which is containment but not scheduling; `SUM` deliberately left clear, so the kernel cannot dereference a user pointer at all yet; and no FP state (`sstatus.FS` stays `Off`). So this port's caveat narrows from "no ring 3" to "minimal U-mode, not ring-3 parity". | Per-process Sv39 roots with ASIDs over the empty low half (`prepare_resume` is already waiting for them); kcore `Process`/`Thread`/`Scheduler` on this port, as AArch64 took in D75; `SUM`-gated user-pointer access for a real syscall ABI; an ELF loader; preemptive U-mode; FP context save. |
| D99 | **Per-process Sv39 roots on RISC-V 64 (M60).** Two processes, each with its own translation root, running the same program: the same virtual address reads different memory in each, and neither can reach the kernel that is mapped in both. This is what D97's empty low half was cleared for, and it makes `prepare_resume`'s `satp` write — implemented and unexercised in D98 — the thing that actually switches a process. **`KernelAddressSpace::new_user(alloc, asid)` copies the kernel's upper-half root entries by value**, the RISC-V twin of what x86-64 has done since D21 for the same reason: one root register, so "the kernel is mapped in every address space" is not a policy a kernel could choose against. A space without it faults on the instruction after `activate`, and the trap taken to report that faults too — which is exactly what the negative check produces: a **hang**, not a verdict, because there is no kernel left to print one. Copying by value rather than deep-copying is what makes sharing mean sharing: a later change to a kernel mapping is seen by every process. **ASIDs are implemented and, honestly, unproven.** A space with a non-zero ASID activates with a single *leading* `sfence.vma zero, asid` and no trailing fence — the fence does both jobs (retire this space's table writes, drop what a previous holder of the ASID left) and the space being left keeps its translations, which is the point of having ASIDs at all. It rests on kernel pages being mapped `global` by `build_kernel_space`, so they belong to no ASID and survive. The kernel space itself (ASID 0) keeps the conservative flush on both sides, and that is not laziness: the boot stub's gigapages are **global**, so an ASID-scoped fence would leave the stub's identity map cached and usable past the switch meant to retire it. **The negative check did not bite**: removing the leading fence entirely still boots clean, because QEMU's TCG flushes the whole TLB on every `satp` write. Recorded as unverified rather than as passing — the fence is there because the architecture requires it, not because anything in this repo would notice its absence (the same shape as D89's `li a1, 0` finding; hardware CI R1 territory). **Proven at boot** (`process:` verdicts, D58-gated): `0x3000_0000` reads A's word under one root and B's under the other — same address, different memory, with the *code frame deliberately shared* between them so the isolation on show is a property of the tables and not of the memory happening to differ; a page A maps and B does not faults in B **and reads correctly in A**, because "B faults" alone would also be satisfied by an address neither maps; and teardown of both spaces reclaims table frames while the kernel's own mappings survive. **The check that had to be tightened, and why it matters.** Teardown originally asserted `reclaimed >= 2`. The negative check that makes `free_tables` walk the whole root — freeing the *shared* kernel tables along with the process's own — **passed it**, returning 18 frames and exiting 33. Over-freeing is silent by construction: the kernel keeps working until something reuses a frame it still points at. The assertion is now the exact count, derived from the layout rather than observed (A spans two gigabyte slots: root + 2 + 4 = 7; B spans one: root + 1 + 3 = 5), and the negative check now fails at check 17. **Verified:** 38/38 tests, lint/fmt clean, 9/9 conformance, all U-mode verdicts intact, exit 33, five boots byte-identical; x86-64, AArch64, ARM 32-bit and RISC-V 32 artifacts byte-identical. **v0 deviations:** the spaces are built and switched by the **boot glue**, not by kcore — there is still no `Process`/`Thread`/`Scheduler` on this port, so a "process" here is a root plus a hand-run user thread; ASID allocation is two constants, with no recycling policy and no wrap handling; `prepare_resume` installs a root but cannot carry an ASID, since the trait hands it only a `PhysAddr`, so a resume through that path flushes more than it needs to; the kernel space keeps its user-half mappings from D98 (harmless, and a different ASID from every process, but it means the kernel space is not purely a kernel space); and nothing revokes or reference-counts the shared kernel-half entries, which is fine while they never change. | kcore `Process`/`Thread`/`Scheduler` on this port (D75's shape); an ASID allocator with recycling and wrap; giving `prepare_resume` the ASID so a resume can flush scoped; hardware validation of the ASID-scoped fence, which no emulator here distinguishes; removing the user-half mappings from the kernel space once processes own them. |
| D100 | **RISC-V 64 joins the kcore substrate (M61).** A real `kcore::Process` holding a `kcore::Thread`, scheduled by `kcore::Scheduler`, entered in U-mode through `prepare_resume`, making a syscall named by `kcore::syscall::SyscallNumber`, and exiting back to boot — the shape AArch64 took in D75. Everything before this on the port was a boot-glue harness that *resembled* the substrate; from here the port shares the substrate that every ring-3 feature on AArch64 was built on. Deliberately **not** routed through `kcore::dispatch` (D79), which needs an `Executive`: what is being proven is the layer beneath it. **The RISC-V-specific hazard this milestone had to solve, which AArch64 does not have.** `new_user` copies the kernel half **by value**, so a kernel mapping made *after* a process root exists is visible to that process only if it needed no new **root** entry. A thread's kernel stack is exactly such a mapping — `spawn_user` maps it into the kernel space after the process space was built. Put it in a fresh gigabyte slot and it is mapped in the kernel and absent from every process, and the first trap the thread takes faults **on its own kernel stack, with no stack to report the fault on**. So the stack sits in the gigabyte slot the direct map already populates, and the check *verifies* it by translating the stack's address from inside the process space rather than trusting the arithmetic. The negative check moves the stack to an unpopulated slot: check 5 fires, exit 65 — before the unreportable fault, which is the point of checking rather than discovering. **Second negative:** making `prepare_resume` ignore its `space_root` leaves the thread running under the kernel's `satp`, where the program's address is unmapped → the fault is recorded → check 10, exit 65. The scheduler really is what installs the address space. **Verified:** 38/38 tests, lint/fmt clean, all D97-D99 verdicts intact, exit 33, five boots byte-identical; x86-64, AArch64, ARM 32-bit and RISC-V 32 artifacts byte-identical. `KernelAddressSpace::from_root` is added for the borrowing kernel alias, matching AArch64's — it owns no tables and must never be torn down. **v0 deviations:** one thread, one process, run once — no preemption, no second runnable thread, so the scheduler's *choice* is untested here; the syscall hook decodes `SyscallNumber` directly rather than going through `kcore::dispatch`, so channels, ports, handles and capability device I/O are all still absent on this port; `prepare_resume` still cannot carry an ASID (the trait hands it only a `PhysAddr`), so a resume through it writes `satp` with ASID 0 and flushes globally — correct, and wasteful; the kernel-stack address rests on a platform assumption that is checked but not derived; and the process is built by the boot glue rather than by an ELF load. | Preemptive scheduling with two runnable U-mode threads; the `Executive` and `kcore::dispatch` on this port (channels, ports, capabilities); an ELF loader; an ASID-carrying resume; deriving the kernel-stack region from the memory map instead of assuming it. |
| D101 | **The Executive and channel IPC on RISC-V 64 (M62).** Two U-mode processes exchange a message over a channel: the client `call`s with a magic and blocks, the server `receive`s it, reports what arrived, `reply`s with a transform, and the client wakes with the reply in the buffer it sent from. Three things land at once, and only the first is about IPC — a message crosses an address-space boundary; the **scheduler chooses**, which a port with one runnable thread could not distinguish from a jump; and the syscall arrives through **`kcore::dispatch`** (D79), the same dispatcher the other ports call, so this port stops having its own idea of what a syscall is. What remains port-local is exactly the register ABI (`a7` names the call, `a0`-`a5` carry it, `a0` takes the result) and advancing `sepc`, which this architecture leaves to the handler. **`sstatus.SUM` — the thing RISC-V makes explicit that the other two hide.** `kcore::syscall::read_user` validates a user range against the caller's tracked mappings and then *dereferences it directly*. On RISC-V that faults by default: S-mode may not touch a `U` page unless `SUM` is set. x86-64's SMAP and AArch64's PAN are the same mechanism and neither port enables them, so setting `SUM` puts this port on the same footing rather than weakening it — but it is the only port where the decision had to be *made*. It is set once and left set, and the obvious narrowing is wrong for an interesting reason: a syscall that copies from user memory may then **block**, handing off to another thread inside the window. `sstatus` is per-hart, so a scoped `SUM` would be set while an unrelated thread ran and cleared on a return that no longer matched it.
