| D140 | **Something arbitrates (M100).** Every contract in this tree has declared power states and resume latencies since D128 — `block_driver.isl` names four device states and reports the resume latency of the deepest, `driver_lifecycle.isl` has had `Suspending`/`Suspended`/`Resuming` for as long — and **nothing weighed one component's requirement against another's**. `device-host` answered `SetPower` by recording the state it was told; `blk-client` sent `SET_POWER(IDLE)` and then `SET_POWER(ACTIVE)` because those two lines were next to each other in a transcript, which is a device changing state rather than a system deciding it should. `ManifestEntry.power_domain` was declared, delivered to every driver in `BindReply` v2, and set to `0` in all three entries. **The rule, and why it is one rule with qualifiers.** The highest vote wins: a vote is a *requirement* — the level below which the voter stops working — so averaging them, or taking the most recent, or preferring a class would break whichever voter needed most, silently. Everything after that is a constraint from above (thermal, policy) or below (the policy floor), and **a constraint that lowers the answer below what the winner asked for is a degradation that is reported**: `Resolution.clamp` is `Some` exactly when it happened, carrying what was asked for and who took it away, and `PowerVoteReply` carries all three across the wire. Raising to the floor is deliberately *not* a clamp — a voter that asked for less and got more is not degraded — and a floor above a ceiling withdraws the clamp, because nothing was taken away in the end. **Thermal readings travel in the same table as the votes**, distinguished by `VoterClass::THERMAL`, and that is a choice rather than a shortcut: a separate thermal argument means routing one message differently from every other, and a caller that routed it wrong would have a thermal reading treated as a demand — the machine driven *up* by being too hot. **`parent_floor` is the clause that makes it a graph.** A parent is at least the maximum of its children's resolved levels, over Phase 2's edges, so a bus cannot go down under a live device; `None` for a leaf, which is not `Some(Off)`. **Where the code lives is the boundary `docs/power/01` draws.** `//api/power` is `no_std`, `forbid(unsafe_code)` and host-tested — every ordered pair of levels, both ceilings binding at once, a full table refusing rather than dropping a vote, a replacement still fitting in a full table because lowering your own vote must never be blocked, and withdrawal proved distinct from voting `OFF` (one states an absence and lets the domain fall to the floor; the other is a requirement that competes). `userspace/power-manager` is the seventh EL0 program and holds almost none of it: the transport, the port select, and the lifecycle transitions. **A voter is identified by the endpoint it spoke on**, never by a field — a body can be forged by any sender, and a manager trusting a `voter` field would let one program withdraw another's vote. **Proven on hardware with three processes and no device flag.** `power_boot_aarch64` needs no machine option, because what is under test is whether one requirement can be weighed against another and that is not a property of any hardware: a driver votes retention and gets it, a user votes full-active and outranks it, a thermal zone takes it back to retention and the reply names `THERMAL` with `clamped_from = FULL_ACTIVE`. **The negative check is in-line rather than a second boot** — step 2's reply is full-active with nothing clamped and step 3's is retention with the clamp attributed, same processes and same domain, one extra message; nothing else about the machine differs between the two lines. Confirmed by inverting it: recast the thermal voter as a user voter and the same votes resolve to full-active, which is check 93. The three voters run one at a time (spawn, run to exit, spawn the next) so the transcript is a sequence rather than a race, while the manager stays parked on its port between them — which is also the point, since it is a resident service and not a script. **And the resolution happens to a device**: the manager drives it down, back up and down again, each through the intermediate state, and the kernel's edge table is what enforces that — had the manager skipped the resume, step 3's `Active -> Suspending` would have been declared from `Suspended` and refused. **v0 deviations, all recorded rather than glossed.** There is **no thermal sensor on any reference machine**, so the ceiling arrives over the same vote channel a sensor driver would use, from a process standing in for one — the interface is real, the measurement is not. The manager **narrates the device's lifecycle itself**, which in a full system belongs to whoever bound the device: the check's device has no driver, and narrating requires `Rights::MAP` by deliberate design (D128, so a process that has merely heard of a device cannot tell its story) — what bounds this manager is therefore the *node*, which has no register window, rather than the right. A **ring-3 service cannot emit a structured event**, so the clamp is carried in the reply and named in the verdict rather than in the event stream. Votes are per-domain and the policy is compiled in, because there is no configuration service; `allow_boost` is false because nothing here implements a boost state. And a **vote outlives a voter that exits without withdrawing** — withdrawal is explicit by design, and reclaiming on peer close needs the manager to observe endpoint closure. | Runtime idle and a wake capability, so a domain that falls to the floor can come back (M101); system suspend ordered by the dependency graph (M102); a thermal sensor class with a real reading; a ring-3 event path so a clamp is a record and not only a reply; withdrawal on peer close; CPU frequency governing and energy attribution (`docs/power/01`), neither of which has a producer on any port. |
| D141 | **A domain that goes quiet, and something that can wake it (M101).** D140 built the thing that arbitrates. What it could not do was let a domain that had fallen to the floor come back: nothing could wake a machine that had stopped, and no way existed to say which things were allowed to. **`Rights::WAKE` (bit 36), and why it is a bit rather than an implication.** Every driver holds a device and most of them have an interrupt, so if arming one as a wakeup source came with the device, the set of things able to wake this machine would be *the driver table* — which nobody chose and nobody can audit. `docs/power/01` asks for that set to be explicit and profile-policed, and a separate right is what makes it a decision somebody took. Added to `docs/security/01`'s Rights Catalog **first**, as that document requires, then to `rights.rs` and `handle_abi.isl`, which the existing bidirectional cross-check compares; a new test walks every right and refuses two sharing a bit. **The brokering happens where the right is granted**, not in a call anybody makes: a manager whose manifest says an entry is wake-capable hands out a capability carrying `WAKE`. There is no broker object in the path, which is the difference between a check auditable by reading the manifest and one auditable only by watching it run. **`WakeSource` (36) and `WakeHold` (37).** Arming is refused for a device with **no interrupt wired** rather than recorded: a wakeup source that cannot fire is indistinguishable from one that has not fired yet at every later point, and a machine that suspended trusting it would never come back — the one moment it can be caught is at arming. The arming lives on the graph node, so a device leaving takes its wake capability with it and the interrupt bridge asks the graph *by line number*, which is the direction an interrupt actually arrives from. `WakeHold` takes, releases and queries: the counter is readable under the same right that takes a hold because a caller reads it *in order to* decide whether to hold, and splitting them would put a syscall in the middle of the race. **A counter, not a flag** (`kcore::power`). The lost-wakeup race is the one failure a suspend path has that ordering cannot fix: an event delivered to a frozen process or an undrained port leaves the machine sleeping with the reason to stay awake already past, and a flag races the same way one layer down. The manager snapshots before entry and the commit refuses if the number moved — whether the event arrived before, during or after does not matter. So the bridge **counts before it signals**: delivery can wake a process that then races the entry, and counting first means the number has already moved by the time anything can observe the event at all. **A hold is a record, not an object.** `docs/power/01` calls wake holds capability-gated, time-limited and attributed; the gate is the right, the limit is a deadline in scheduler ticks (a liveness bound, like a DMA lease's — D135), and the attribution is the calling *process* rather than a field anybody can write. Making each hold its own object would buy transferability, which is exactly the property a suspend blocker must not have: a hold that can be handed on is one whose holder cannot be held responsible for it, which is the wakelock lesson this is written against. A wake takes a short grace hold for itself so an event arriving just after a resume is not swallowed by an immediate re-suspend; a full hold table **still counts the event**, because the count is what a commit compares. **`runtime_idle` makes `resume_latency_us` do something.** The class contracts have reported it since D128 and nothing read it — a number nobody consumes is a number nobody keeps accurate. A state that costs more to leave than a domain's users tolerate is not entered, and the refusal names both figures; the three not-idling answers are distinct because a domain that never idles from a latency budget and one whose timer is wrong look identical from outside, and only one is somebody's mistake. **The proof, with a real device nobody owns.** The PL031 on `qemu virt` — the same choice D104 made with the goldfish RTC on RISC-V, and for the same reason: a virtio device only interrupts for a request somebody made, so using one would mean idling with work outstanding, which is not what runtime idle is. **The manager touches no register.** Boot owns the clock, arms the alarm and clears it; the manager holds a capability to it with `WAKE`, a port, and the device whose lifecycle it narrates. It idles the domain, arms the source, parks, and is woken by the alarm on INTID 34 — after which the counter has moved by exactly one, the grace hold is there, and the device is back in service. **The negative check is a handle rather than a second boot**: a second capability to the *same device*, differing only in the right it carries, is refused by `WakeSource`. Confirmed by inverting it — grant that handle `WAKE` too and the check fails at 44. **The bug this found, which was mine from D140 and latent.** A ring-3 run leaves `TTBR0` holding the last process's space; `power_check` then freed that space, after which the live translation tables were frames the allocator had handed to somebody else. Nothing failed where it happened. What failed was the next thing to touch a low address — the interrupt controller, in a check further down the boot — as an EL1 translation fault with no console left to report it, because the console is a low address too. Every check that spawns processes calls `boot_low.activate()` before it observes or tears anything down; D140's did not, and now does. **v0 deviations.** The idle *timeout* is zero in the boot policy: it is exercised exhaustively in host tests, and a boot check spinning for scheduler ticks would be testing the timer rather than the idle path. The wake-hold handle is a rights carrier rather than a Power object with a table entry — what the kernel checks is the right, and the hold is attributed to the process; the suspend commit will need a real object and inventing one earlier would be an object nobody reads. Holds are not yet released when their holder dies (`release_wake_holds_of` exists and no departure path calls it). AArch64 only. | The suspend commit and the wake-counter snapshot comparison (M102); wake holds released on holder death; a Power object class; wake-capability as manifest data (`ManifestEntry` has no `wake_capable` yet, so boot decides which grant carries `WAKE`); CPU idle-state selection, frequency governing and energy attribution (`docs/power/01`), none of which has a producer on any port. |
| D142 | **The machine stops, leaves first (M102).** D140 arbitrated and D141 let a domain go quiet and come back. So the kernel enforces it: `Active -> Suspending` is refused while any child is bound and not `Suspended`, and `Suspended -> Resuming` is refused while the parent is bound and not `Active`. Phase 2's parent edges are what it reads; `Executive::declare_lifecycle` is the only place holding both the lifecycle record and the graph, which is why the *rule* is a free function in `crate::lifecycle` — testable against a list of states with no graph at all — and the *walk* is in the executive. **A neighbour with no recorded state does not block**, nor does one that is not `is_bound`: the table records devices a manager has told the kernel about, so a child with no lifecycle has no driver and nothing to suspend, and anything else would let hardware nobody is using stop the machine sleeping for ever. **The refusal is distinguishable and the others still are not.** D128 collapsed the three lifecycle refusals to one code deliberately — the recovery is the same in each case, and a caller that could tell "you are stale" from "that edge does not exist" could probe the table without holding anything. Nothing else is runnable, because user space is frozen, so the CPU reaches its idle loop, which *is* suspend-to-idle: `docs/power/01`'s baseline on every profile, needing no firmware. Ending the sleep is part of *counting* the wake rather than a later step: the parked thread is the machine being asleep, and a wake that moved the counter without unblocking it would leave a system awake by the numbers and stopped in fact. **The manager does not know the tree; it walks it.** It is handed the bus and nothing else, and `DeviceChild` — Phase 2's syscall, unchanged — answers what is behind it. The manager freezes nothing, because there is nothing else running to freeze. Key eviction before sleep (`docs/security/01`) has no key material to evict. Per-stage resume latency is not attributed (B22/B23 unbudgeted). AArch64 only. **And one mistake worth recording**: mid-milestone I ran `git checkout` on a file whose changes were uncommitted and destroyed them, then rewrote them from the transcript. The tree was dirty by design — this project commits on request — and a discard is not a revert. | Platform sleep through PSCI; freezing user space by job suspension; key eviction ordered before the commit; per-stage suspend and resume latency attribution; the ordering proven over an enumerated PCIe tree; the other four ports. |

| D143 | **What a device's data path costs, declared and checked (Phase 2 item 3).** Phase 2 built the binding tree and Phase 3 made its edges load-bearing for power ordering. What neither built is the third bullet of `docs/drivers/01` ("Bus Topology And Data Paths"): a transfer either crosses no extra process, because the controller gave it per-child queue separation, or it **relays** through the bus host — and where it relays, the class contract "declares its added latency and throughput cost" so that "a deep tree of relaying hubs is a declared cost, not a surprise". Nothing declared a cost, nothing accumulated one, and nothing checked one: `select` looked at a device and an entry and never at *where the device was*, so two identical endpoints — one on the root complex, one behind two hubs — bound identically and the driver was told nothing that distinguished them. Its budget was missed at run time, by an amount nobody computed, for a reason nothing recorded. **The rule is in `api/binding` in both halves, and that is the point.** Deciding *which* ancestors are hops is as much policy as adding their costs up, so `hop_for` is there beside `select`; a manager that made the first decision itself would put half the arithmetic where no test can reach it. `Hop` carries the doc's three cases as three variants — `Separated` is not a hop at all, `Relay` is one *even at zero declared latency* because hops count against the class budget in their own right, and `Undeclared` is an ancestor nothing claims. **`Undeclared` is deliberately not `Separated`**: treating a hub nobody described as free would let a device behind it bind as though direct-attached, and the arithmetic that was supposed to be visible would be a guess — the silent fallback `docs/lifecycle/04` forbids. Latency sums with `checked_add` (a path whose cost cannot be represented has not been shown to fit any budget, and saturating would report a number nobody computed as though somebody had); throughput takes the **min**, because a path is only as fast as its narrowest hop. **Three refusals, not one.** `BudgetExceeded` (8) is fixed by attaching the device closer, `ThroughputTooLow` (9) is not — a shorter path is no help when the remaining hop is the slow one — and `PathUndeclared` (10) is policy data that is missing rather than a cost found wanting. The path is checked **last**, after firmware: everything above it is about the driver, the installation or the device, and reporting a topology problem before an untrusted signature would send somebody to rewire a machine whose driver was never going to bind. A path failure **refuses rather than falling through** to a more tolerant entry, for the same reason a failed signature does. **The manager walks now.** `expand_bus` expanded exactly one level, which made a device two buses down invisible and left the manager with no notion of depth; it descends carrying the ancestors it passed through, and that chain *is* the data path — from the graph's own parent edges rather than a topology somebody described. A chain deeper than `MAX_RELAY_DEPTH` is refused outright rather than truncated, because a machine missing half its hardware is indistinguishable from a small one. An ancestor the **kernel** cannot identify is `Undeclared` and not probed: a bus is granted without `MAP` precisely so nobody reads its registers, so there is no second route of the kind a leaf device has. **`BindReply` version 3** carries `accumulated_latency_us`, `relay_hops` and `path_throughput_mbps` — told to every driver whether or not its entry declared a budget, because a number filled in only on the way to a refusal is one nobody can trust on the way to a bind. `DeviceClass::Bus` (3) is appended so the manifest can describe a hub at all; a PCI bridge classified as `Unknown` before, which `select` refuses by design, so the one question worth asking about a bus could not be asked. `blk-probe` checks what it is told against **its own copy** of the budget — duplicated on purpose, since a driver reading the number from where the manager read it would be checking nothing. **The real machine reports a truthful zero.** On `pci_bind` the manager is handed a `pcie-root-port`, which gives each function its own configuration and its own BARs; nothing relays, the manifest says so, and the endpoint binds at **0 hops** against an entry tolerating only 30 us. Declaring a cost there would have put a price in the verdict that nothing pays — and the root port is now registered *identified* rather than windowless-and-anonymous, without which the manager would (correctly) have refused the device it has been binding since D136. **Verified**: 55/55, exit 33 on all five ports, whole-graph lint clean — and confirmed by inverting it three ways, each changing exactly one thing. Raise the block entry's budget past the two-hub path and the far device binds; widen the far hub and the network device binds; give the unidentifiable hub an identity the manifest claims and the device behind it binds at one hop. Each fails the boot with a different number in the same report. **v0 deviations.** Throughput is **declared and checked but never measured** — nothing weighs a hub, and no admission-control path consumes the declaration yet, which is what `docs/kernel/07` will eventually do with it. The relaying hubs are **graph nodes with real parent edges rather than an enumerated PCIe switch** (the D142 precedent): no reference machine has a relaying hub on it, and attaching one would couple the proof to a topology that is not under test — the *non*-relaying half runs on real hardware. A refused device stays held, so a manager answers every later request for that class about the same device; the check works with that rather than around it, and asks a second manager. The manifest is still compiled in. AArch64 only. | Admission control consuming the declaration end to end; measured rather than declared hub costs; the accumulation proven over an enumerated PCIe switch; a manifest delivered as signed data; the other four ports. |

| D144 | **The relay path on the second port, and the honest account of the other three (D143 continued).** D143 landed the declared data-path cost on AArch64; the ask was the other four ports, and exactly one of them can carry it. **RISC-V 64 has the whole thing and needed no mechanism at all.** The arbiter is `api/binding`, the manifest and the accumulation are `userspace/device-manager`, the budget is checked by the same `blk-probe`, and all three already compiled for `riscv64` — what this adds is boot glue: a `relay_check` that registers the same chain of graph nodes, spawns a manager over each root and a probe against it, and compares two packed reports. The reports come back **byte-identical to AArch64's** (`0x3e80908000a0100` and `0x10a0000000a`), which is the strongest available statement that the arithmetic is not per-port: the same near device binds at one hop for ten microseconds on a gigabit path, the same far device is refused `BudgetExceeded` at 35 us against the same 30 us budget, the same network device is refused `ThroughputTooLow`, and the same unidentifiable hub yields `PathUndeclared`. **Verified by inverting it on this port too**, not by assuming D143's inversions carry: raise the block entry's budget past the two-hop path and the far device binds (check 62); give the unidentifiable hub an identity the manifest claims and the device behind it binds at one hop (check 63). **What the port taught.** The RISC-V teardown discipline is stricter than the glue D143 shipped: `Scheduler::reap` frees the scheduler slot while the dead process still claims the thread index, so `Process::forget_thread` has to follow every reap, and the kernel stacks are explicitly unmapped and their frames freed. Writing that here exposed that D143's AArch64 check removed processes and tore down their spaces **without reaping or forgetting the threads** — harmless in that boot because the next check installs a fresh `Executive`, but a stale scheduler entry pointing at a torn-down space is exactly the D115 failure waiting for a boot that reuses one. Fixed on both ports, and both now carry a `RelaySpawn` recording that a thread index and a process index are not the same number. Note also that `kernel_space.activate()` before teardown is the RISC-V analogue of AArch64's `boot_low.activate()` — the tables being freed are otherwise still the ones mapping this kernel. **The other three ports cannot run this, and it is not close.** x86-64 answers `MapDevice`, `DmaAlloc`, `DeviceChild`, `DeviceInfo`, `DriverLifecycle` and `ChannelReplyContinue` with `ENOSYS`: it has a roottask and no ring-3 driver framework, so there is no manager to accumulate a path and no graph to walk. RISC-V 32 and ARM32 have no `kcore::exec::Executive`, no `kcore::devmgr` and no ELF loader at all — they reached ring 3 and per-process address spaces (M67-71) and stop there. **Nothing was stubbed to make the count look like five.** Adding a relay check to a port with no device manager would mean building a manager for it first, which is the driver-framework port (D111's shape) and not this change; a check that skipped itself on three ports would report a pass for work nobody did. What those ports are missing is recorded as the next thing rather than papered over. **Verified**: 55/55, exit 33 on all five ports, whole-graph lint clean. **v0 deviations.** Two of five ports carry the driver framework, so two of five carry this; the exit criterion is the framework reaching the other three, not this milestone reaching them. RISC-V 64's manager is handed the device directly by `driver_rebind_check` rather than a bus, so this port has no real-hardware zero-hop half of the kind AArch64's `pci_bind` gives — the direct-attach answer is checked by the probe rather than printed about an enumerated root port. Everything D143 deferred is still deferred. | The driver framework on x86-64, RISC-V 32 and ARM32, after which this milestone is a boot check and not a port; the rest of D143's list. |

| D145 | **The driver framework runs on x86-64 (D111 continued).** D144 recorded that three ports could not carry the relay path and why: x86-64 answered `MapDevice`, `DmaAlloc`, `DeviceChild`, `DeviceInfo`, `DriverLifecycle` and `ChannelReplyContinue` with `ENOSYS`, so there was no manager to hand a device to and no driver to receive one. It has them now. A ring-3 manager holding a real PCI function's capability matches it against its binding manifest and transfers it to a ring-3 driver that asked for the class and held nothing; the driver maps its own register window and reads a word the kernel independently reads at the same physical address. **The claim under test is that the framework is common, so the evidence is what did not change.** `kernel/pci` is byte-for-byte the crate the other two ports enumerate with, and `userspace/device-manager` and `userspace/blk-probe` are the same *sources* — what this adds for them is a Bazel target and a linker script each. `api/binding` is untouched. **`ConfigSpace` is two methods over an ECAM-style byte offset, and that is why the legacy port pair fits underneath it**: ECAM's offset encoding is `bus:device:function:register` shifted, so `PortConfigSpace` decodes it back into the address `0xCF8` wants — no MCFG table parsed, no window found, no base hardcoded, and `-device virtio-blk-pci` on q35 gives this port the same classified device the others bind. **Extended configuration space is out of reach and says so rather than aliasing.** The address register has eight bits of register number, so an offset at or past 0x100 cannot be expressed; wrapping it into the first 256 bytes would answer a capability walk with the wrong register and look entirely successful, so the read returns the all-ones a missing function returns and `find_capability` — already bounded to the 256-byte header — never needs it. **The BAR window is checked against the memory map rather than chosen and hoped for**: firmware has already assigned BARs on x86, so this reassigns into a hole of its own, and `pci_window_is_clear` refuses to enumerate at all if that hole overlaps a usable entry of Limine's normalized map instead of placing a BAR over memory somebody is using. **The one thing `userspace/uabi` learned is a difference in kind, not spelling.** SYSCALL itself overwrites `rcx` and `r11` with the return RIP and RFLAGS, so the stub must declare them clobbered; the AArch64 and RISC-V stubs have no equivalent. **The bug that had to be fixed before any of it ran was in the entry stub, and it was a per-CPU cell holding a per-thread fact.** The stub parked the user RSP in the per-CPU block for the whole syscall. A syscall that blocks — a `receive` with no message, a `call` awaiting its reply — parks its thread mid-stub and lets another run; that thread's own syscall overwrote the cell, and the first resumed and `sysretq`ed onto the second thread's stack. **Nothing faults at the point of the mistake**: the value is a plausible address in another address space, and the failure surfaces as a wild jump much later. The cell now holds it for exactly two instructions, long enough to have somewhere to put it before there is a kernel stack to push onto, and the copy is taken onto the kernel stack — which is per-thread by construction and cannot be aliased. The window is safe because SFMASK clears IF on entry and this port takes no NMI. Ten qwords still precede the `call`, so the alignment pad went away rather than the count changing. **The check installing another demo's fault handler is what made every failure look identical.** `user_fault_handler` drives the single-process demos' `USER_PROCESS`/`USER_SCHEDULER` statics; a fault here found them stale, yielded to a scheduler this check never populated, and hung with nothing printed — so a boot timeout was the only symptom any defect could have, which is why the entry-stub fix was first reported as ineffective. `bind_user_fault_handler` exits the faulting process in *this* process table and blocks its thread, and the boot carries on and names the vector, CR2, RIP and thread. **That line found the real bug in a minute**: `vec=14 cr2=DEVICE_MMIO_VA+0x2000`. The check granted `first_bar()`, and the lowest-indexed BAR of a virtio-pci function is the MSI-X table — one page. The driver had been handed a page and asked to read into the third. It takes the largest memory BAR now and **refuses outright when even that cannot be read past its first page**, rather than moving the read to offset zero and claiming a window from one page of evidence; AArch64's code avoids `first_bar` explicitly, which is why the defect could only appear here. **Verified**: 55/55, exit 33 on all five ports, whole-graph lint clean, with the check armed and its two markers asserting the bind. **Two mistakes worth recording.** Everything staged was committed mid-milestone, which put twelve incomplete files on HEAD and turned the tier-3 boot red; `37a423b` parked it by dropping the image `--cfg`s and the markers, and `7f98eb8` re-armed them, so that middle commit's message no longer describes the tree. And I attributed an arch-battery failure to the kernel image growing 126 KB — five fresh runs across five KASLR bases showed zero failures, and the failing run was a stale artifact booted by hand against a mismatched path. **v0 deviations.** This port has the substrate and the bind and **nothing above them**: no crash-recovery ladder, no relay path, no DMA scoping (there is no IOMMU driver here — D119's SMMU work is AArch64's), and no ring-3 interrupts, because what would deliver them is still the PIC that D87 tracks replacing. The manifest is still compiled in. | The crash-recovery ladder and the relay path on this port; DMA scoping behind VT-d; interrupts once D87 replaces the PIC; extended configuration space through MCFG; the framework on RISC-V 32 and ARM32. |

| D146 | **Data the system verified before it read it (Phase 4, 0.4).** Phase 4 is firmware loading and protected memory, and its firmware half is specified as proving itself *through 0.4's store* — which was never built. Nothing in this tree delivered data as *verified* data: the binding manifest is a compiled-in Rust `static` (D130, restated by D143), the ring-3 programs are byte arrays a genrule embedded, and **the kernel trusted every one of them because it could not tell them from its own `.rodata`**. `docs/security/01` ("Boot Security") has required a verified initial system image and `docs/security/02` ("Anti-Rollback") has required firmware to carry a monotonically increasing security version number for as long as either document has existed; neither had a mechanism. **What "verified" means here, stated before anything else.** Measurement against a trust anchor, and **no asymmetric cryptography anywhere in this tree**. `docs/security/02` ("Trust Anchors And Signing Infrastructure") defines anchors as *"the public keys **and measurements** that verifiers treat as authoritative"*, so a measurement anchor is one of the two kinds it names rather than a stand-in for the other — but it establishes *what* the bytes are and never *who* produced them, and that is the milestone's headline deviation rather than a detail. Crypto agility is honoured so the other kind can arrive without a format change: `docs/security/02` forbids an algorithm implied by position, so the header carries an algorithm identifier, a key identifier (`anchor_id`) and a format version even though there is one of each today, and an algorithm outside the schema is **refused** rather than mapped onto the one that exists. **The anchor is checked in, and that is the mechanism, not an inconvenience.** It would have been far easier to have the build emit both the container and the digest it is checked against — and that would authenticate transport and nothing else, because a build that decides what it is trusted to produce authorizes whatever it happens to produce, the way a self-signed claim agrees with itself. With `kcore::store::TRUSTED_ANCHORS` in kernel source, changing what this kernel trusts is a source change somebody reviews, which is the reviewable-trust property of a signing ceremony reached by the only means available to a tree with no private key. The cost is real: **changing the store's contents fails the build until the constant is updated**, so `//store:anchor_test` fails first — in `bazel test`, printing the value to paste — rather than leaving a QEMU run that refuses to mount as the only symptom. **A hash tree two deep, and the split is about *when*.** Each entry carries its blob's digest; the anchor is the digest of header and directory together, so one 32-byte constant authenticates a container of any size. `mount` verifies the directory once and `open` measures the blob **every time**: a store proved intact at boot and read from for the rest of the boot is a store trusted for what it *was*, and nothing here is willing to say when "was" ended. **The format is ISL, because this is the case the rule is for.** Not a protocol — a host tool writes it and the kernel reads it — but a cross-component interface with two implementations and a long life, and a *stored* format is worse than a wire format in the way that matters: peers can be updated together, a container built before a system update and read after it cannot negotiate. `image_store.isl` is what both sides decode, with golden encodings for the 56-byte header and the 96-byte entry. **One SHA-256 now, where there were two (D13 retired).** Both copies were `Vec`-based and host-only; a hash that allocates cannot run where allocation is fallible and one that copies its input to append padding cannot measure an image at boot. `//api/hash` is `no_std`, allocation-free and streaming, and the two build tools take it — proved unchanged by the schema gate and the licence gate, which check the interface ids and the vendored-file pins it now computes. It is held to one property its predecessors never were: streaming in pieces equals the one-shot at **every** split of a three-block input, which is what caught a buffer-management bug in the first draft that every aligned case passed. **The negative half runs on every boot rather than being asserted once.** A verifier is only interesting when it says no, and a check that only ever saw a valid container would pass just as happily against a `mount` that returned success unconditionally — so the boot check gives the same code a copy with one byte changed in a blob, which must fail at `open` because the directory's digest of that blob is what disagrees, *and* a copy with one byte changed in the directory, which must fail at `mount` because that is what the anchor covers. Two different failures from two different tampers, and the untouched blob still opening, so the refusal is scoped to what changed. **Verified**: 59/59, exit 33 on all five ports, whole-graph lint clean, and the 32-bit build gate covers the format because kcore reaches it (every length is `u64` and every conversion to `usize` is checked). **Confirmed by inverting it twice, each changing one thing.** Change one byte of `TRUSTED_ANCHORS` and the boot exits 65 saying `store: FATAL: check failed (6)` — refused, and *said so*, rather than hanging or mounting anyway. Remove the digest comparison from `open` and the boot fails with the check's own code 101, the tampered blob having come out; without that inversion the in-line negative half would be a claim about code nobody had watched fail. **v0 deviations.** No asymmetric verification, as above; the anchor is a measurement and the container's *origin* is unproven. The store has **no consumer but the check** — its first real one is firmware loading, and D130's compiled-in manifest is not moved into it here. **Its contents are synthetic and named as such**: no device on any reference machine loads firmware, so `firmware.bin` is a deterministic pattern blob from a seed, generated rather than committed because every file in this tree carries an SPDX header and a binary cannot. There is **no rollback floor** — `svn` is carried by the format and enforced by nobody, because the minimum acceptable version belongs to whoever holds it and this tree has no monotonic counter or fuse to hold it in. The container is embedded in the kernel image rather than delivered as a separate artifact, so nothing yet exercises a store that arrives after the kernel. `Component::SECURITY` (8) is appended to the event schema, with `STORE_MOUNTED` (39) and `STORE_REFUSED` (40). AArch64 only; the mount is arch-neutral and the other four ports are a call each. | Firmware loading over this store, with a rollback floor and provenance; the binding manifest delivered as data, retiring D130; a public-key anchor behind the same algorithm identifier; a store that arrives as an artifact rather than as `.rodata`; the other four ports (done, D147). |

| D147 | **The store on all five ports, and the check became one implementation (D146 continued).** D146 landed the verified image store on AArch64; the ask was the other four, and **all four can carry it**, which is the first time that has been true of anything in this tree. Nothing here is architecture: the container is bytes in the kernel's own image, the reader is `no_std` and dependency-free, and no port needs a device, a bus or a process to mount one. **What the port changed is where the check lives.** D146 wrote the tamper half into AArch64's boot glue — about eighty lines that touch nothing architectural — and copying that four times would have been four chances for the interesting half to rot somewhere nobody looks. `kcore::store::self_check` is now the one implementation every port drives, on the `kcore::supervise` precedent (D127), and each port's glue is a scratch buffer, a call and a line. **That move is also what made the check testable without a boot**: `self_check_against` takes an anchor set, so kcore's host tests run the whole thing over a container built in the test — including the case that matters most, a **scratch buffer too small**, where the check must refuse rather than skip the tamper half and report success. That failure mode would have made every port's verdict a claim about nothing, and until the logic left boot glue nothing could reach it. **The five lines are byte-identical, digest included.** Same container, same 4856 bytes, same `firmware.bin` measuring to `0x8ad76964e917a4e5...` on x86-64, AArch64, RISC-V 64, RISC-V 32 and ARM 32 — which is the strongest available statement that neither the format nor the hash has a word-size or endianness assumption in it. **RISC-V 32 and ARM 32 are the load-bearing half of that.** `//kernel/width-conformance` has compiled the reader for a 32-bit target since D146; this *runs* it, over a container whose every offset and length is a `u64` and whose every conversion to `usize` is checked. A truncating conversion would not have failed to build — it would have produced a different answer, or none, on exactly two of the five. **Verified by inverting it on every port rather than assuming D146's inversions carry.** Change one byte of `TRUSTED_ANCHORS` and **all five** exit 65 with `store: FATAL: check failed (6)` — one constant reaching five kernels, which is what a shared anchor set is supposed to do. Remove the digest comparison from `open` and all five fail with 101, the tampered blob having come out. Each inversion changes one thing and produces the same number in five places. **Verified**: 59/59, exit 33 on all five ports, whole-graph lint clean, and every port's smoke script now asserts both markers — the mount and the refusal — so a port that stopped verifying fails its own boot rather than quietly passing. **v0 deviations.** Everything D146 deferred is still deferred: no asymmetric verification, no consumer but the check, synthetic contents, no rollback floor, and the container embedded in the kernel image rather than arriving as an artifact. The two 32-bit ports have no ring-3 framework, so what they prove is the reader and nothing above it. | Firmware loading over this store, with a rollback floor and provenance; the binding manifest delivered as data, retiring D130. |

| D148 | **Firmware, loaded by the framework and judged by the kernel (Phase 4 item 25).** `docs/drivers/01` ("Firmware Loading") lists six requirements and this tree had none of them. D146/D147 built the store the images have to arrive through and proved it on five ports; it has had **no consumer but its own boot check** since, and this is the consumer. **The shape is fixed by where trust already lives.** The kernel owns the store and the anchors, so a ring-3 manager given those anchors would move the root of trust into ring 3 — but `docs/drivers/01` says firmware loading is *mediated by the driver framework*, and the framework is that manager. So the split is exactly the trust boundary: **the manager holds the authority and asks, the kernel measures and decides**, and the image reaches the driver as an out-of-line grant (D131) it never had the right to fetch. **Two version numbers, because the spec asks two questions.** `docs/security/02` ("Anti-Rollback") makes the security version a monotonic counter compared against a floor so that an image is *"rejected even if correctly signed"* — a statement about vulnerability, made by the system. What a driver means by "the firmware I understand" is a statement about capability, made by the driver. With one number, "version constraints are checked" and "rollback is blocked" would be one comparison wearing two names, and the case worth catching — an image a driver is happy with that the system refuses anyway — could not be expressed at all. So `StoreEntry` gained `image_version` and the container went to **format version 2**, one milestone after it landed, which is the cheapest moment it will ever be: one consumer, one container, and the bump makes `api/image-store`'s "a reader that does not know a version refuses" rule fire for the first time — against version **1**, whose fields all sit where this reader still expects them, which is exactly what would make reading them look like it worked. **The floor is checked first and wins**, and that ordering is the rule rather than an implementation detail: reporting the driver's satisfaction first would answer a rollback with "your driver is fine with this", which is true and useless. **`Rights::FIRMWARE` (bit 38), catalog first** as `docs/security/01` requires. Not implied by holding a device, for the reason `wake` is not: firmware is code that runs on hardware outside this CPU's protection, so the set of components able to put it there must be auditable rather than being whatever the driver table contains. It belongs to what *mediates* loading, and **the manager narrows it away when it hands the device on** — which is what makes "the framework chooses the image" a rule the kernel enforces instead of a convention drivers observe. **`KError::PolicyRefused` (16)** is appended rather than folded into `AccessDenied`, which is about the caller: somebody told "access denied" for a rollback-blocked image would go looking at their rights, where nothing is wrong, and no rights change will ever produce a different image. *Which* policy refused travels in the report, on D128's argument — every policy refusal recovers the same way. **The driver measures what it was handed**, with `//api/hash`, and the check compares that against what the kernel measures from the store. It would have been far easier to have the driver read the digest the kernel reported, and it would have proved nothing: a driver echoing the sender's digest checks the sender against itself. This is the one comparison in the milestone neither side can satisfy by trusting the other. **Five claims, each a different outcome from one code path**: the image loads and both measurements agree; `firmware-old.bin` is refused `RollbackBlocked` **while measuring perfectly**; `firmware-v1.bin` is refused `VersionTooOld`, a different authority with a different fix; the driver's own `FirmwareLoad` is refused because the right stayed behind; and the update-compatibility rule runs over the store's real contents, filtered to the images *in use* — an image today's policy already refuses is not stranded by an update, because nothing is running it. **Verified**: 61/61, exit 33 on all five ports, whole-graph lint clean, and the boot asserts four markers because four of the five claims are refusals. **Confirmed by inverting it twice.** Drop the rollback floor below `firmware-old.bin`'s security version and that image *loads* — the manager's report turns the refusal into `0xf` — while the other refusal is untouched, which is the two-authorities claim demonstrated rather than asserted. Let `FIRMWARE` travel to the driver on the transfer and the driver's own load succeeds, reporting `0xff`. **One thing the port taught**: `BindReply` growing to 72 bytes broke three programs that sized their bind buffer with the literal 64, and the failures looked like decode bugs rather than a schema change. All of them take `BindReply::WIRE_SIZE` now. **v0 deviations.** **No device on any reference machine consumes firmware**, so the driver receives, measures and reports an image and nothing programs it into hardware; the images are synthetic pattern blobs generated from seeds. **The rollback floor is compiled in** — `docs/security/02` puts it in monotonic counters or fuses, and no machine here has either, so an attacker who can rewrite the kernel image can lower it. **Firmware crash telemetry has nothing to capture**, which the spec already qualifies with "where possible"; that is recorded as the reason rather than faked. **There is no OS update**, so the compatibility rule runs over real images against a hypothetical driver set. The image is still measured rather than signed (D146's deviation, unchanged). Exactly one manifest entry declares firmware, and it is product-specific — which is how firmware works, and also why every other check in the tree still binds with none. AArch64 only. | Firmware on the other two ports that have the framework; a public-key anchor behind the same algorithm identifier; a rollback floor in hardware; protected memory (Phase 4 item 27); the binding manifest delivered as data, retiring D130. |

| D149 | **Memory that is something in particular, and a device that may not have it (Phase 4 item 27).** `docs/drivers/01` ("DMA Safety") has six bullets and five were built; the sixth — *"protected memory cannot be mapped unless policy permits it"* — had never had a mechanism, because **nothing in this tree could say memory was anything**. Every object was the same kind of memory, so there was nothing for a policy to be about. Three documents already referred to the missing concept: `docs/kernel/01` lists "secure and protected memory pools", `docs/kernel/03` says protected pools suppress address and content fields in traces, and `docs/security/01` says "Protected media: protected memory path". **The doc change came first, as the catalog requires**, and it declares a *handling path* rather than a data class: several of the nine classes select the same treatment of memory — protected media and credentials both require that no device reach the bytes without authority — and the memory manager needs the treatment, not which of nine reasons produced it. Two paths, and further ones when they have handling rules; declaring nine would have been eight interfaces the kernel does nothing about, which is the mistake `DigestAlgorithm` having one value and the event schema's `Classification` having one class both avoid. **The authority is a right on the *device*.** Which hardware may be trusted with protected content — a decoder, a crypto engine — is a property of the platform, and a model that asked the buffer's owner instead would be asking the party least able to know. `Rights::PROTECTED_DMA` (bit 39) therefore sits where `FIRMWARE` sits, travels with the device capability, and narrows away on transfer, so the question is answered once rather than re-decided by every holder. **Classification only rises**, which is `docs/security/01`'s "the strongest applicable class governs" applied to memory — and it is the load-bearing half: without it anything holding a protected buffer could clear the class and hand the memory over, and every check above it would be decorative. Declassification is a policy act with its own authority and audit; `MemoryClassify` (40) is not it. **One rule, two callers.** `kcore::memory::attach_permitted` is a free function on the `record_dma_fault` precedent (D127): `DmaAttach` applies it to a handle's rights, and the boot check applies it to the rights the resource graph recorded — both asking the same question, where a rule written into the syscall alone would have left the second asking a different one that merely agreed. The check is placed **before any aperture is allocated**, so a refusal costs nothing; one that had already consumed address space would let a caller exhaust a device's translations with requests it was never allowed to make, and a host test says so. **The refusal is proven by a real program, with one variable.** `blk-client` moves a 512-byte sector out and back through a memory object, then classifies **that same object** and repeats the identical request — same buffer, same driver, same device, same sector — and it comes back refused. The driver reports the refusal from its own side, and the check's XOR sink expects exactly one per boot, so a second or none fails it. **And the hardware backs it.** A policy that returned an error while installing a translation anyway would pass every test above, so the SMMU check drives the device at the address the refused attach *would* have returned — **inside its own aperture**, an address the device is entitled to, unmapped because the rule stopped the mapping being made. That is what separates it from D119, where the refused address was outside the lease and the SMMU was refusing a device reaching somewhere it had no business at all. Two faults arrive on the unit's own interrupt, and the buffer the device *was* entitled to still works, so the refusal is scoped to what was classified. **Verified**: 61/61, exit 33 on all five ports, whole-graph lint clean. **Confirmed by inverting it.** Authorize the device for protected memory — boot's grant and the manager's transfer — and the client's protected request succeeds, failing the boot at check 185 with the sink no longer matching: the refusal was the right's doing. A second inversion, two lines rather than one, shows what monotonicity prevents: permit lowering and have the client declassify, and the request goes through. **One mistake worth recording**: reverting the first inversion with `git checkout` on a file whose milestone work was uncommitted destroyed it, and it had to be reapplied. That is the second time — D142 records the first — and the lesson is the same one: this tree commits on request, so the working tree is where the work lives and a discard is not a revert. **v0 deviations.** Two paths and no more, so a region can say it is protected and not that it holds biometric data; the trace suppression `docs/kernel/03` describes is **not** implemented — the class is carried and the tracer does not read it yet. Nothing allocates from a separate protected *pool*: the class is a property of an object rather than of the physical memory behind it, so a frame returning to the allocator carries nothing with it, which is right for this mechanism and is not the "secure and protected memory pools" `docs/kernel/01` will eventually want. Declassification has no authorized path at all, rather than a policy-gated one. AArch64 only, and only the SMMU port has the hardware half. | A protected pool the frames themselves come from; trace suppression reading the class; an authorized, audited declassification path; the classes Crypto and Multimedia turn out to need; the other ports. |

| D150 | **A driver that speaks first (Phase 5, the network class).** The class rollout's first step, and it was chosen because the network contract already existed in full (`network_driver.isl`, 288 lines) and nothing implemented it. What it forced was a hole in the kernel: `SyscallNumber::ChannelSend = 12` had been declared in the ABI since the channel facility landed and **had no dispatch arm**. Every message this system had ever moved was a call, a reply, or a reply-receive — always an answer to somebody's request — which suits a device a client reads from and cannot describe one where *a frame arrives because a machine on the other side of the wire sent one*. The executive already had `send` (async enqueue, wake a parked receiver, signal the destination endpoint's object); only the syscall was missing, and it is the same shape as `channel_call` minus the block: `Rights::WRITE`, handles and memory objects carried on the request path, and a full queue answered `WouldBlock` **to the sender** rather than a frame dropped quietly. Now: *"a ring-3 driver bound a NIC by class and served the network contract to a client holding no device at all; the NIC interrupted the driver and the driver SENT the frame, no call outstanding; the gateway answered 52:55:0a:00:02:02; taking the link down was announced the same way, a transmit while it was down came back LINK_DOWN rather than an I/O error, and the block class's conformance suite judged all of it — same seven rules, second class, every one reached and held."* **`TRANSFERRED` ownership stopped being a word in a comment.** `NetFrameEvent` gained `buffer: transfer handle<Object, {READ, MAP}>` — narrower than the block class's out-of-line buffer on purpose, because a client receiving a frame reads it and must not be able to pass it on — and the driver creates the object, attaches it to the NIC, **never maps it**, detaches it and sends it away, holding nothing afterwards. The order is the mode: detach first (an object the device can still reach is one it may write into after somebody else owns it), then send, then replenish, and the driver is genuinely without a receive buffer in between. Two smaller findings made that possible. The frame had to start at its buffer's first byte or the class contract would have grown a virtio detail every client must know, so `Net::post_rx_split` posts the transport header and the frame as a **two-descriptor chain** — header into a page the driver keeps, frame into the page it gives away — and the mock in `//kernel/virtio` was taught to follow the chain rather than agree with a driver that got it wrong. And the events needed **their own channel**: a pushed event and a reply share one endpoint's queue, so a client's `ChannelCall` would happily dequeue an event as its answer. The driver's single `PortWait` selects over the device interrupt *and* the client's endpoint, which is what makes the send unsolicited rather than merely asynchronous — it is not sitting in anybody's call when it decides to send. Link state is read only from a device that offered `VIRTIO_NET_F_STATUS`, because the field does not exist otherwise and a driver taking an interface out of service over uninitialised config space would be acting on noise; a device that does not offer it has a link that is up by definition. **Inverted four ways, each reported rather than asserted**: the driver keeping its handle instead of transferring (the client sees no granted buffer, `0x77/0x100`); the `ChannelSend` arm removed (the run stalls with nothing exited, check 453); `IO_ERROR` instead of `LINK_DOWN` while the link is down (bit 49 clears — the distinction this class draws that the block class's `NO_MEDIUM` does not); and advertising `PROMISCUOUS` without implementing it (bit 48 clears, so the conformance suite is load-bearing rather than decorative). **Deferred, and recorded rather than faked**: a *device-originated* link change is not exercised — QEMU's user netdev link is always up, and only the transitions the driver causes are proven; driving `set_link` over QMP from the boot script is the honest way to add it. One buffer per frame, no multi-buffer receive and no full-MTU queue interface, which the contract already defers. And x86-64 carries no network driver until the PCI bus driver lands, when a ring-3 virtio-pci transport stops being a detour. | `bazel test //...` green including `virtio_net_boot_aarch64_test` and `ring3_host_boot_aarch64_test` with the `net-class` markers; the class conformance suite reports *complete* against the network class |

| D151 | **PCI enumeration left the kernel (Phase 5, the bus driver).** The kernel walked ECAM at boot: it descended through bridges, handed out bus numbers, sized and placed every BAR, and wrote what it found into the resource graph. Everything downstream — bind-by-class, `DeviceInfo`, D124's manager-bound PCI device — rested on a table built by touching hardware in the most privileged code on the machine. `DeviceChild` let a bus controller *read* the edges below it, and every one of those edges had been put there by the kernel; **nothing in this tree let a program outside it say a device exists.** Now: *"a ring-3 program held the host bridge and nothing else, walked it with the same enumerator the kernel uses, placed the BARs and declared the functions it found; it offered them to the device manager as capabilities rather than as claims, the manager took hardware it had never seen, and a driver bound one by class, mapped its OWN configuration space and read 1af4:1042 out of it — which is what the kernel's independent walk found in the same register."* **The walk is not rewritten.** `tessera_pci` was already `no_std`, `forbid(unsafe_code)`, with config access behind a `read32`/`write32` trait — the same seam `tessera-virtio` used to move a driver out of the kernel — so the bridge descent, the multi-function rules and the read-modify-restore that sizes a BAR all now run unprivileged, unchanged, on the code the kernel was running a moment before. What is new is `DeviceDeclare` (41) and **containment, which is the whole safety argument**: the kernel checks that a declared function's configuration slot lies inside the window the controller's own capability covers and that its register window lies inside what the bridge forwards, and it computes the slot from the BDF rather than taking an address on trust. Without both, a controller declares a device whose "registers" are the kernel's page tables and then maps them — and a bus driver is exactly the component to assume hostile, because it is the one that touches every unknown device before anything has classified it. What is *not* checked is the identity, and saying so is better than a check that consists of believing it twice: the controller read it out of configuration space, which is the only place it exists. **`Rights::CONFIGURE` stopped being a bit nobody had used.** `MapConfig` (42) maps exactly one function's 4 KiB slot, on a right separate from the one that maps its registers, because they are different authorities over the same device — configuration space is where bus mastering is turned on, where a BAR can be moved out from under whoever placed it, and where MSI is armed. The grant is a **manifest** decision (`grants_configure`) and not a consequence of the transport, gated by whether the device has a configuration window at all — which only a declaration creates, so a device the kernel registered itself is never asked for a right that would gate nothing. That gating was found the hard way: making it a pure manifest decision broke every existing check, because a manager holding a device without `CONFIGURE` cannot hand one out and rights only ever narrow. Two more findings. A **bus window is bounded differently from a driver's**: D114's argument for enumerating in the kernel was that a driver gets a page against 256 MiB, which is an argument about drivers — a controller's whole job is to reach every function's slot, so `MAX_BUS_WINDOW_BYTES` is eight buses and a machine with more hands over more window deliberately. And the offer path: the manager's arriving-capability handler read every capability as a *return*, so a device it had never seen killed it; a return is re-verified and marked degraded, and a device nobody has ever driven is not degraded. The discriminator was already there — an unheld slot means something is outstanding. **Both machines, and the difference is the evidence.** x86-64 reaches configuration space through the `0xCF8`/`0xCFC` pair, so a ring-3 controller could not use it at all; the port gained one config read — `PCIEXBAR` at `0:0.0` offset `0x60` — and refuses rather than guesses when the chipset says the window is off. The kernel then walks through ports and the controller through memory, and the two agree about the same function, which neither can do by echoing the other. **Inverted three ways, each reported rather than asserted**: a register window declared outside what the bridge forwards is refused and creates nothing (`0x82/8`); `grants_configure` turned off leaves the driver's `MapConfig` denied (`0x5b/8`), so the right gates something; and a declared device id one bit off the hardware's fails at the comparison (check 497) with the driver's own config read on one side and the kernel's port-based walk on the other. **Deferred, and recorded rather than faked**: declared functions carry **no interrupt** — a PCI function's INTx line comes from the device tree's `interrupt-map`, which nothing parses, and MSI/MSI-X belongs with NVMe; the controller enumerates and **exits** rather than staying resident for hotplug, which still has its own in-kernel check; and the kernel keeps its own PCI walk for the checks that prove other mechanisms (MSI, MSI-X, hotplug, virtio-pci). What this retires is the driver framework's *dependence* on it. | `bazel test //...` green including `smoke_boot_test` (q35) and `pci_bind_boot_aarch64_test` with the `pci-bus` markers; the ring-3 walk and the kernel's agree about the same function on both machines |

| D152 | **Memory that says where it has to be (Phase 5.3, item 29 — the contiguity contract).** `docs/hardware/04` says a memory object may be created with placement constraints — physical contiguity, a minimum alignment, an addressing limit — *satisfied at allocation or the create fails with a resource error, never silently weakened*; and it states the IOMMU-first rule: where the consuming device sits behind an IOMMU the broker satisfies a contiguity requirement with an **IOVA-contiguous mapping over scattered physical pages**, the request must declare device-visible contiguity rather than physical, and physical contiguity is honoured only for devices the resource graph records as needing it. **None of it was expressible.** `MemoryCreateArgs` carried a length and nothing else, so there was no constraint to satisfy and none to refuse. What already existed was the mechanism the rule depends on: `attach_translations` maps an object's frames to consecutive IOVAs, so device-visible contiguity over scattered pages is what `DmaAttach` had been quietly producing all along — undeclared, unchecked, and with nothing proving the pages were ever scattered. Now `MemoryCreateArgs` v2 carries `MemoryConstraint` bits, an alignment and an addressing limit; `kcore::memory::Placement` is checked against the frames that came back and **refuses**, freeing what it drew, rather than handing back memory that does not meet the constraint its owner stated. **The two contiguities are different bits and that is the point** — one is a constraint on the mapping and free behind an IOMMU, the other is a run of memory nothing can defragment — and a schema with a single "contiguous" flag would have left the rule unstatable, because there would be no way to tell an over-ask from a real requirement. The broker enforces it at `DmaAttach`: a physically-contiguous object offered to a device behind an IOMMU is `PolicyRefused` and recorded as `DMA_CONTIGUITY_REFUSED` (45), while the same request for hardware the graph records as needing one — no scatter-gather, nothing translating — is honoured. Both halves are tested, because "physical contiguity is never available" is a different and wrong policy. Two smaller decisions, both stated rather than assumed: the check **checks and does not search**, since a placing allocator belongs to the memory manager and a refusal a caller can retry is a smaller lie than a silent downgrade; and an addressing limit is measured against a frame's **last byte**, because a page that starts below a 32-bit controller's ceiling and ends above it is still memory it cannot address. **Inverted**: weakening the allocator to ignore an alignment it cannot meet fails `an_alignment_that_cannot_be_met_is_refused_not_rounded`, so strict binding is a property the tests hold rather than a sentence in a doc. **Not built, and recorded rather than faked**: named heaps and reclaimable carveouts — the constraint field carries the concept and nothing allocates from a heap, which would make a heap name a field that lies. And the NVMe driver that is this contract's first real consumer — multi-queue, a vector per queue, one PRP entry over a contiguous IOVA — is the next step; what landed here is the contract it will ask against. | `bazel test //...` green with the placement tests and both halves of the broker's rule; `MemoryCreateArgs` v2 carried through every ring-3 caller |

| D153 | **The block class over a second transport, with a vector per queue (Phase 5.3, NVMe).** The class contract had exactly one implementation, which makes "a contract belongs to the class and not to the transport" a claim rather than a finding. Now: *"a ring-3 driver brought an NVMe controller up and served the BLOCK CLASS over it — the same contract the virtio driver serves, judged by the same client program byte for byte, and the class conformance suite came back complete. Nothing in the schema changed to let a second transport in."* `blk-client` does not know which controller answered it. **`kernel/nvme`** is the transport core — bring-up, the admin and I/O queue pair protocol, the command encodings, and completion parsing behind a `Registers` trait, `no_std` and `forbid(unsafe_code)`, host-tested against a mock — the third crate to use that seam and the third driver to move out of the kernel unchanged because of it. The **phase tag** is what a test earns there: nothing in a completion entry says it is new, one bit does, and a reader that ignored it would replay the previous pass on every wrap, which looks exactly like a controller completing work twice. **A vector per queue is the milestone's own claim.** Each I/O queue is created with its own MSI-X vector, boot routes each to its own port, and the driver submits on a queue and waits where *that* queue's interrupts land — it never reads the other ring to find out what finished. Reads go on one queue and writes on the other, so the contract's ordinary traffic exercises both rather than a side test doing it once. That forced two things the graph did not have. A device could hold **one interrupt line**, which every device driven here had; a controller with a vector per queue needs all of them recorded or the second is one nothing can re-arm, so `IrqComplete` now re-enables every line a device has — a driver that took a completion cannot name the line it arrived on, since the *port* identifies the queue. And an interrupt **route** was one per device, so a multi-queue controller would have kept delivering on every line but the first into ports whose holder was gone — the exact hole routing was introduced to close, reopened by a device having more than one interrupt. Routes are now per line and the sweep takes all of them; a line the graph never recorded cannot be routed, and routing one twice replaces where it goes rather than leaving two records for a sweep to end one of. **Four bugs, each found by running the check rather than by reading it.** A second storage transport broke every check that went looking for "the block device" *by class* — NVMe is mass storage too, so the virtio-pci check found it and declared a fatal error about a controller that never claimed to have virtio capabilities; those checks ask for class *and* vendor now. The controller was registered with a window and no identity, so the manager fell back to probing its registers for the virtio magic and read a capability register instead. Every queue base must be **page-aligned** — packing two rings into one page is ordinary where a ring is just memory, and here it makes the controller refuse to enable with a fatal status that names no reason. And the one that cost the most: adding a route for a *named* line as a passthrough to the graph recorded the route and left the **port unbound**, so the interrupt arrived at the GIC, was masked and signalled, and reached nothing — the driver parked forever on a completion its port had never been told about. The comment on the original said why that must not happen: recording and binding are one operation, and the same code has to own both halves. `device_route_irq` is now written in terms of the named-line one, so there is a single path, and a test signals each line and checks something arrives. **Inverted**: both queues given the same vector leaves the driver unable to say which completed and the check fails; the unbound route fails the test that now exists for it. **Deferred, and recorded rather than faked**: no PRP *lists* — one entry per request, which is what a page-sized transfer needs and what a contiguous device address makes sufficient; the driver's queues come from `DmaAlloc` rather than through an IOMMU, so D152's device-visible contiguity is proven in host tests and not yet on this path; and x86-64 carries no NVMe driver until it has ring-3 interrupt delivery at all. | `bazel test //...` green including the new `nvme_boot_aarch64_test`; the block class conformance suite reports *complete* against a second transport |

| D154 | **A controller whose children are devices, and a clock that is asked for (Phase 5.4, MMC/SD).** Three things met here, and two of them hold. *"A ring-3 driver identified the card in an SD host controller, DECLARED it into the resource graph as a device behind that controller — one the kernel never enumerated, holding no registers of its own because every transfer goes through the controller — and served the block class over it to the same client program that judges virtio and NVMe, conformance suite and all. Its bus clock was asked for rather than written."* **The clock class exists now.** `docs/drivers/04` has specified it since it was written and none of it was built: `clock_controller.isl` is the third class contract, and `api/clock` holds the parts that are arithmetic rather than registers — reference counting, where a clock runs while any consumer wants it and stops when the last lets go, and rate selection, where a request outside the declared range is **refused and never clamped** because a consumer handed the nearest thing the hardware could manage cannot tell it from what it asked for. A shared clock's rate will not move under another holder. The range's ends are inclusive, since a controller declaring 400 kHz and refusing exactly 400 kHz would refuse the rate every SD card identifies at. **The third class needed no new conformance rule**, which is the finding rather than a convenience: two classes agreeing on a shape can be a coincidence of both moving bytes through queues, and this one moves nothing at all. **`DeviceDeclare` stopped being PCI-shaped.** A bus declaring no configuration window says its children own none, and the capability that comes back carries identity and lifecycle and no memory — the honest description of a card. The case is a *narrowing*: a bus that forwards nothing cannot declare a child with a register window, or the no-window path would be a way to describe a bus with nothing to contain a window in and then put one there. A device with nothing to map holds **no window** rather than one of length zero, because `Some((0, 0))` would have sent `MapDevice` to map physical page zero. `DeviceClass::Sd` is its own class rather than `Bus`, since a manifest that could not tell an SD host from a PCIe bridge would offer a bridge driver a card slot; and the manifest gained `grants_derive`, which only the SD entry sets — a driver holding it can put nodes in the graph, which is authority over what everything else will bind against. **`kernel/sdhci` is the fourth transport core on the seam** the other three established, and the first whose device can leave: presence is a question it answers, `NoCard` is its own outcome and not a timeout, and its mock's card **can be taken out**. The divider rounds down and never up — overshooting a card's identification rate is how a driver works on the card it was written against and not the next one — and reports the rate it actually produced. **Two bugs the boot found.** A card is addressed in blocks or in bytes depending on its capacity, and the two agree at sector zero and nowhere else: the first read passed and the second came back shifted by one byte, because a block number had been handed to a card that wanted an offset. And the check's own splice: a `str.index` matched inside `virtio_pci_regions` and deleted several functions, which is why the repair rebuilt the file from `HEAD` plus the extracted blocks rather than reaching for `git checkout` — the third time a discard would have destroyed uncommitted work in this tree. **What does not hold, and is recorded rather than faked: card-detect hotplug.** QEMU's `sd-bus` refuses `device_del` with *"does not support hotplugging"*, and its `sdhci-pci` reports a card present and answers the whole identification sequence with **no `sd-card` device attached at all** — so neither a removal nor an empty slot can be produced on this emulator. The `NO_MEDIUM` path is written and reachable, and the transport's half of it is exercised against a mock whose card is pulled; what is missing is the end-to-end boot, and it needs an emulator that can model an empty slot. Also not built: the reset controller and the regulator `docs/drivers/04` specifies beside the clock class, because a card's bring-up here needs neither and a class nothing drives is a contract nobody has checked. Single-block PIO, no ADMA, no SDIO. | `bazel test //...` green including the new `sd_boot_aarch64_test`; the block class conformance suite reports *complete* against a card behind a controller |

| D155 | **A bus whose devices have no registers (Phase 5.5, USB).** Every driver in this tree maps a window and writes to it. A USB device has nothing to map, so this is the first **relaying** bus host `docs/drivers/01` describes: *"a ring-3 host bound an xHCI controller, walked its ports and a hub, and DECLARED every device it found into the resource graph: hubs as buses with devices behind them, so the graph is three levels deep and the relay cost on a device two levels down is a sum of two. Its devices have NO REGISTERS, so two class drivers served the block and input contracts over bytes this host moved for them. The disk was judged by the same client program that judges virtio, NVMe and SD, byte for byte, and an idle keyboard answered NO_REPORT rather than failing. One attached device was REFUSED."* `Hop::Relay` has been in the binding rules since D124 with nothing real to count — PCIe gives every function its own queues, so every device here has been `Separated` — and the manifest's USB entry is the first relay cost that describes the machine rather than a fixture. **`kernel/xhci` is the fifth transport core on the seam** the other four established: nothing is at a fixed offset (the mock's registers sit where no real controller puts them, so a regression to constants fails rather than passing by luck), a producer ring wraps **through a Link TRB that toggles the cycle**, and nothing in an event says it is new — the same shape as NVMe's phase tag, and the mock's event ring wraps for exactly that reason. **`api/usb` parses what a device says about itself**, which is untrusted hotplug input in the strict sense: a length byte of zero is the only way a flat descriptor chain contains a cycle and costs an attacker one byte; a length past the buffer reads the previous device's descriptors; a count that disagrees with what arrived asks a driver to open a pipe that is not there. Each is a typed error, a bound reached is refused rather than clipped, and the fuzz obligation is met as the ISL parser's is (D12) — a seeded generator, not libfuzzer. **The allowlist is the first policy here that refuses something that works**, and a refused device is **declared and not offered**: visible in the graph with a class code no manifest entry claims, because a refusal nobody can see is not a control. **`input_device.isl` is the fourth class contract** and the first whose data comes from a person, so `NO_REPORT` is a value rather than an error — an idle keyboard is a working keyboard. The conformance suite judged it with no new rule. `usb-storage` is the **fourth transport under `tessera.driver.block`** and the first that owns nothing at all. **Five defects, each found by running it.** A device declared by a bus that forwards nothing was a leaf, so the `DERIVE` travelling down to it had nothing to exercise and a tree of relaying hosts was flat however deep the machine was; it is now itself such a bus, inheriting the same bound at every depth. The manager granted `DERIVE` without `TRANSFER`, so a bus driver could declare a device and not hand it on — something in the graph nobody would ever be given. The manager could not tell a device coming *back* from one arriving for the first time once a controller bound its own device and declared children behind it, and was marking brand-new hardware `Degraded`; arrivals are matched by identity now. **Two drivers blocked on one service channel got each other's replies** — the documented single-outstanding-caller limit, never reached because no topology here had two drivers binding at once; `ChannelRecvAny` (43) is the answer, and it registers on every endpoint rather than polling, because a server that never blocks is a server no other thread runs behind under a cooperative scheduler.
