And two endpoints shared one transfer ring, which the specification allows nobody to do: a ring is named in an endpoint's own context and rung by its own doorbell. **Deferred, and recorded rather than faked**: isochronous transfers, which need a periodic schedule with bandwidth reserved at configuration time and are declared-and-refused rather than silently absent; a brief transfer that does not complete leaves its TRB queued, because stopping an endpoint and re-setting its dequeue pointer is not built, so a client polling a silent device past a ring's worth of times would overwrite pending work; the rest of `docs/drivers/02`'s USB security list (locked-screen attach policy, user consent, spoofing detection, USB4 DMA protection); no SuperSpeed handling, no streams, no USB4. | `bazel test //...` green including the new `usb_boot_aarch64_test`; the block class conformance suite reports *complete* against a device reached through two processes, and the input class against a fourth contract |

| D156 | **An interrupt no interrupt controller can see (Phase 5.6, GPIO).** A PL061 has eight lines and one interrupt output, so which line fired is in a status register and known only to whoever read it. *"A ring-3 driver bound a PL061 that is on NEITHER PCI NOR VIRTIO-MMIO: nothing enumerated it, so the manager asked the part itself and read the answer out of its PrimeCell identification registers. The driver then handed each watching client a capability to ONE LINE. A button was pressed from OUTSIDE the machine, the client holding line 3 woke, and the client holding line 5 did not."* The quiet client is the load-bearing half: a mechanism that broadcast, or a driver reading the raw status instead of the masked one, would wake both and neither client could tell. **`Rights::SIGNAL` gets the first thing it gates**, having been in the catalog from the start appearing only in a name table. `PortSignal` (44) raises a software edge on **the port you hold** — not on a source number, which any holder could use to wake somebody else's client — and only on a source that port is already bound to, so what a holder may raise was decided when the port was made. Waiting and waking are separate authorities: the client is given READ and the driver SIGNAL, because a client that could signal its own port could report an edge that never happened. **`kernel/pl061` is the sixth transport core** on the established seam, and the mistake it exists to prevent is the data register's mask-in-the-address: `GPIODATA` occupies 256 bytes and bits 9:2 of the offset are a pin mask, so a single-pin write through offset zero writes *every* pin — and the pin it meant to change does change, which is what lets the bug survive a test. The mock models the masking exactly so the clobber is assertable. A trigger is one value across three registers, because both-edges means nothing on a level-sensed line. **`gpio_controller.isl` is the fifth class contract**, and the first whose reply hands over a capability: `WatchLine` returns the line's interrupt object, which is why there is no polling method and — deliberately — no line-changed event, since an event would be a second, weaker path reaching every holder of the contract rather than the client that was granted the line. The conformance suite gained a table and no rule; a PL061 genuinely drives, genuinely interrupts and genuinely has no bias or drive strength, so one transcript reaches both halves of the optional-method rule without contriving anything. **`api/pinctrl`** is the policy half `docs/drivers/04` asks for — one pin one owner, a mux applied whole or not at all, and declarations scoped to a board revision with the walk *reporting what it skipped*, because a silent skip is indistinguishable from a table with nothing in it for this board. And **the manager gained a third way to identify a device**: a platform device has no configuration space and no magic word, so the existing ask-the-device path now reads the PrimeCell registers when the magic is absent — asked of the part rather than answered from the device tree, because the tree is a description somebody wrote. The GPIO manifest entry is the first that matches on a **bus**, which is what distinguishes a device with no vendor id of its own. **Two bugs the boot found.** The driver polled its clients before they had spoken and parked on its interrupt for good — under a cooperative scheduler the clients cannot run until it blocks, so blocking on the channels is what lets them ask at all. And the client read the port event's source at offset zero, which is the record's `size`. **Deferred, and recorded rather than faked**: a wait that spans a port *and* a channel, which is what a driver with clients and an interrupt actually needs — the channel half landed in D155 and this driver waits on its clients until each has asked for a line and on the machine afterwards, so a request arriving while it is parked waits for the next edge; `HandleDuplicate` is declared and undispatched, so boot installs two handles per line port (one to signal with, one to hand over) where a driver would mint the narrowed grant itself; bias and drive strength are answered `NOT_SUPPORTED` because a PL061 has neither; pin muxing has no hardware on this machine, so `api/pinctrl` is host-tested policy with no controller behind it; and only line 3 has an external stimulus here, so the demultiplex is proven negatively. | `bazel test //...` green including the new `gpio_boot_aarch64_test`, which arms, is pressed over QMP, and wakes exactly one of two clients |

| D157 | **Enumeration left the kernel for a third bus (Phase 5.7, platform).** 5.6 drove a platform device and boot found it: the kernel read the device tree, recorded a window and an interrupt, and only then did anything unprivileged get involved — the arrangement PCI was in before 5.2. Now: *"NOTHING PRIVILEGED LOOKED AT THIS DEVICE. A ring-3 bus controller read the machine's own description — the device tree, mapped as its bus capability's window exactly as a PCI controller maps ECAM — and declared what it found."* **The device tree is that bus's configuration space**, and not by analogy: it is granted as the capability's window and mapped with the same `MapDevice` call, because from the kernel's side there is no difference between a window that describes devices and a window that is one. It is parsed by the unchanged `tessera-devicetree` — `no_std`, `forbid(unsafe_code)`, already carrying a no-panic test over arbitrary bytes, because a description is firmware's word — and the crate gained the many-device form of a walk it only had in the singular, where **a buffer too small reports the number found rather than the number written**: a clamped count would make a machine with more devices than room look like a smaller machine. **Two things had to exist first.** A declared device could not have an interrupt, so `DeviceDeclareArgs` version 2 carries one — without it a bus can describe everything about a device except how it interrupts, which leaves enumeration in ring 3 and the one fact that makes a device drivable in the kernel. And a bus must not claim any line it likes, so `BusWindow` gained the range it may declare from and a declaration outside it is refused: the interrupt-shaped version of the containment check the register window has always had, and inverting the two separately fails different tests, which is what says neither stands in for the other. **Zero lines is the default**, so every bus that predates the field keeps exactly the authority it had — a PCI bridge forwards memory and its functions interrupt by message, through a different door. A declared device now also **inherits its bus's kind** rather than being recorded as PCI unconditionally: what transport a driver must speak is a binding input, and a device-tree node recorded as PCI would be offered to drivers written for a bus it is not on. **What the controller declares is bounded twice and neither bound is its own**, and it asks `DeviceInfo` for both rather than learning them by refusal — so a device beyond this bus's reach is *counted* (thirty-two virtio transports a megabyte above the forwarded window) rather than dropped in silence. The console is withheld by a table entry rather than by an absence in the code, and counted for the same reason. **The kernel's one remaining part is the route**, and it takes it from the graph: boot asks the bus what is behind it, takes the child whose class code says GPIO, and routes whatever line the graph records — it never learns what a PL061 is, where it lives, or which SPI it uses. **What moved, and is worth naming**: 5.6's verdict said the *manager* asked the part itself, and it no longer does — a declared device arrives with an identity from the controller that found it, so the manager classifies from the graph as it does for PCI, and the **driver** checks the part's own PrimeCell registers before writing a word to it. A description is a claim, and the thing that acts on it is the thing that verifies it. **Deferred, and recorded rather than faked**: a driver still cannot ask for its own interrupt route, which is what boot stands in for; the declared trigger is carried and not yet applied to the GIC; no ACPI, so x86-64 has no part of this; and the compatible-string table is compiled in for the reason the manager's manifest is. Also honest about an inversion that did *not* behave as predicted: declaring the console does not lose the verdict lines, because nothing binds a UART driver — what catches it is the declared-count check, and the reason to withhold it is what would happen once such a driver exists. | `bazel test //...` green; `gpio_boot_aarch64_test` passes over a device nothing privileged enumerated, and both inversions (a bus given no lines, a bus that declares the console) fail it |

| D158 | **A device that is never finished (Phase 5.8, sound).** Every device this tree drives answers a request and stops. A playback stream is a standing obligation with a deadline: *"a ring-3 driver brought a virtio-sound device up and served the AUDIO CLASS over it, and the conformance suite came back complete on a sixth contract. A stream with periods still queued REPORTED NO GAP; a second stream, primed the same way and then abandoned, DRAINED AND THE DRIVER REPORTED THE UNDERRUN."* **`UNDERRUN` is an outcome, not a fault** — the device plays silence and carries on, nothing fails, and a client told `OK` would have no way to know a gap was heard. It is the audio analogue of `NO_MEDIUM` and `NO_REPORT`, and the only one of the three the **driver** observes rather than the device: no register anywhere records it, so the fact is "the queue was empty when the device asked for the next period". **`kernel/virtio` gained a `snd` module** beside `Blk` and `Net`, host-tested, and the mistake it exists to prevent is that a transmit buffer's **status is written after the samples** — the used ring's length therefore covers the status, and taking it for bytes played reads eight where a period is a thousand. Two smaller ones: a control status succeeds at 0x8000 rather than zero, which is the reverse of the usual convention; and the four queues are not interchangeable the way NVMe's pairs are, so the encoders return which queue a message belongs on. **`audio_output.isl` is the sixth class contract**, and the suite judged it with a table and no rule. `Describe` reports the period and how many the device holds because a client that guessed would either starve it or add latency nobody chose; `accepted` is authoritative because a period crosses inline and a client assuming the whole write landed would drift ahead and find out as late audio rather than as an error; and `Status` is required so that zero underruns is a claim rather than an absence. **Three bugs the boot found.** Two streams share the transmit queue and the used ring does not say which one a buffer belonged to — the first version attributed completions to whichever stream had one outstanding, which would have credited a fed stream with a starved stream's period and reported the gap against the wrong sound; completions are matched by descriptor head now. Configuring a stream also started it, so a client could not prime the ring before the device began playing — starting is its own method, and a stream started empty gaps before the first sample is heard. And a queue sized to the periods rather than to the *descriptors* would have reused a descriptor still in flight, because a chain is two. **What is NOT proven, and it is weaker than the milestone intended.** The check cannot show a stream being *kept* fed. This emulated device consumes faster than a client supplying one period per syscall round trip, so neither stream stays ahead of it — feeding the "abandoned" stream during the observation window still produces an underrun, which means the fed-versus-starved contrast the plan called for does not hold. What the two streams do show is narrower and true: an underrun is observed and reported when the queue empties, and is not reported while there is something in it. The contrast needs periods granted from the client's own memory rather than copied through sixty-four-byte messages — the out-of-line mechanism the block class has and this contract does not, which is the change that closes this. **Also deferred**: capture, because against a null backend it returns silence whether or not the driver is correct — a test that cannot fail; jacks and channel maps beyond their counts; one format and one rate, with mixing and rate conversion belonging to a service above this contract. | `bazel test //...` green including the new `snd_boot_aarch64_test`; inverting the driver's underrun observation fails it |

| D159 | **A device whose work is checked from outside the machine (Phase 5.9, display).** Every check in this tree until now believes the guest, and is right to: a driver reports it read a sector and nothing else could have produced that value. A display is the first device whose output is not a value the guest can report — it is on the glass, and **a driver that created the resource, attached the backing, set the scanout and drew nothing reports exactly what a working one does**. So the boot check asks the guest for the smaller half and the harness asks QEMU for the framebuffer: *"a client DREW EVERY PIXEL of a 64x64 pattern through the contract and asked for it to be SHOWN"*, and `tools/qemu/gpu_boot_aarch64.sh` reads back all 4096 pixels with QMP `screendump`. The pattern makes red rise with the column and green with the row, so a wrong stride, transposed axes, a wrong origin and a wrong byte order each produce a *different* wrong picture; a flat colour would come back identical under all four. **Two mechanisms are new.** `RESOURCE_ATTACH_BACKING` is the first time anything here hands a device a **described** region — a list of pages, because `DmaAlloc` grants them one at a time and nothing promises they are adjacent — and every command names a **rectangle within a resource**, which is the one piece of arithmetic a device does not check for you: a flush past the resource's edge reads whatever follows the backing and puts it on the screen, so `kernel/virtio`'s `gpu` module refuses it, overflow included, host-tested. **`display_output.isl` is the seventh class contract**, judged by the same seven rules and a table. Its two distinctive errors are `NO_SCANOUT` — nothing attached is a state, not a fault, the display analogue of `NO_MEDIUM` and `UNDERRUN` — and `OUT_OF_BOUNDS`, **refused rather than clipped**, because a client whose request was quietly trimmed would see a picture it did not compose and have nothing to check. And **nothing is visible until a flush**: `Blit` and `Flush` are separate methods so a client can build a frame before showing any of it, and so that the driver never shows anything on a client's behalf. **Both inversions were run, not asserted.** With the flush made a no-op the guest still reports complete success and exits 33, and the screendump comes back black — 4096 of 4096 pixels wrong, which is the entire reason the screendump is there. With the pattern written one pixel wider than the stride, the origin and the whole first row stay correct and 3969 of 4096 differ — a check that had looked only at the corner it starts from would have passed. **Deferred, not faked**: 2D only, no virgl and no 3D contexts; the `CURSOR` feature bit is declared and clear, because a cursor plane is a second scanout and nothing here has two; one display, one resource; no mode setting beyond using the mode the device reports; and pixels cross **inline** sixty-four bytes at a time, as audio's periods do and for the same reason — granting the client's own memory is the out-of-line change D158 already records, and a real frame at this rate would be a syscall per sixteen pixels. **What is NOT proven**: that this scales to a display of the size the device offers. The picture is 64x64 deliberately — a framebuffer for the reported mode is megabytes and a thousand separate page grants — so what the screendump establishes is that pixels a client sent reach the glass unaltered, not that a full-resolution frame can be delivered at any rate. | `bazel test //...` green including the new `gpu_boot_aarch64_test`; suppressing the flush, or writing at the wrong stride, fails it while the guest still reports success |

| D160 | **A device whose right answer was decided somewhere else (Phase 5.10, crypto).** The last class this phase adds, and the first that handles a secret. A cipher's output cannot be inspected: bytes encrypted with the wrong key, the wrong mode or not encrypted at all are indistinguishable from correct ones, and everything a driver *reports* — the session was made, the operation completed — is reported identically by all of them. So the check is a **known-answer test** against NIST SP 800-38A F.2.1: *"a client encrypted NIST SP 800-38A's vector and got back THE CIPHERTEXT THE STANDARD PUBLISHES, decrypted it back to the plaintext, and saw a one-bit change of key CHANGE THE ANSWER."* The key, IV, plaintext and ciphertext are reproduced from the standard and nothing here computed any of them — a value a wrong implementation cannot agree with by accident. **`crypto_service.isl` is the eighth class contract**, judged by the same seven rules and a table, and it is shaped by one fact: substituting an algorithm is the only mistake in this tree with no symptom. So `CryptoAlgorithm::NONE` is zero, and a blank field therefore names nothing rather than the first algorithm in the list; every operation repeats its session's algorithm so a disagreement is detectable; and `NOT_SUPPORTED` is the *correct* answer to an algorithm this machine does not have rather than a shortfall — the suite counts that refusal as a pass, which needed no new rule. `Reset` destroys every session and zeroizes every key, because a reset that left one installed would leave a secret alive in a device the next client to bind can reach. **A key crosses exactly once**, into a session; no reply, error or trace point has anywhere to put key material, and `tools/qemu/crypto_boot_aarch64.sh` greps the whole serial log for the key in six renderings — before it checks the exit code, because a leaked key matters as much on a boot that failed. **`kernel/virtio` gained a `crypto` module**: the control queue is **after** the data queues, the reverse of every other virtio device here, and a driver carrying the usual assumption over would create its sessions on the queue it then sends operations to; a `Session` carries its algorithm so the protocol's two copies of it are one value; destroying a session is answered in one byte where creating one is answered in sixteen; `src_data_len` counts the data alone because the IV is its own region; and the destination length is written from the source's. The driver **refuses AES-ECB although the device offers it** — equal blocks encrypt to equal ciphertext, which leaks the shape of what it protects. **All three inversions were run.** Reconciling a disagreeing algorithm instead of refusing it: caught, and only by the disagreement claim. Sending a zero IV instead of the client's: the ciphertext stops matching the standard and **the round trip still succeeds** — a decrypt-what-you-encrypted test on its own would have passed a driver that ignored the IV entirely, which is exactly why the published vector is the check. A driver that traces its key to the debug sink: caught by the log grep, and by nothing else in the machine. **What is NOT proven.** Nothing here is constant-time, and no claim is made: the cipher is the device's and this code frames requests, which is not secret-dependent, but the framing has never been measured. The key crosses **inline**, which is honest about what it is — bytes in a page the kernel transported; the successor is a capability to a key minted by a key service, which needs a kernel object, a rights entry and a service that do not exist, and is a milestone rather than a corner of a driver one. The ECB refusal is a **compiled constant**, where `docs/security/02` wants policy to enable and forbid algorithms centrally without recompiling dependents — the mechanism is right and its owner is missing. **Also deferred**: hash, MAC, AEAD, algorithm chaining and RSA; one data queue; and 64 bytes an operation, so this is a correctness path and not a throughput one. | `bazel test //...` green including the new `crypto_boot_aarch64_test`; reconciling a mismatched algorithm, zeroing the IV, or tracing the key each fail it |
| D161 | **A runner that will not certify what it did not check (Phase 6.1).** `docs/drivers/01` ("Certification") lists ten checks and one consequence — certified drivers are distributed through signed update channels. Most of the ten already have a mechanism in this tree: removal is scripted over QMP, a translation fault is harvested and ends a lease, power votes are arbitrated and clamped, a driver host is restarted and re-bound. **What none of them produced is a verdict about a driver**, and a channel needs one. `api/certification` is the rules crate — `no_std`, dependency-free, host-tested and run unchanged in ring 3, the arrangement `api/class-conformance` already proved — and `certification.isl` is the record, so a verdict outlives the run that made it. **The one rule everything else is shaped by: a check nobody ran must never look like a check that passed.** That is not hypothetical. The checks here are shell scripts registered by hand in a `BUILD.bazel` file; delete a registration and every remaining test still passes, because from the inside a check that was removed and a check that never existed are the same absence. So `Outcome::NotRun` is a first-class answer, `is_certified()` requires every check to have *run* as well as passed, `missing()` names the ones that did not — which is what tells an unfit driver from an absent rig — and recording a pass after a failure keeps the failure, so a rig that re-ran a flaky check until it went green certifies what it observed. `Certificate`'s fields are private because `passed ⊆ ran` is the invariant worth protecting: both masks arrive independently from the wire, so a record claiming a check that never ran is representable whatever the encoder meant, and nothing downstream could tell it from a real one. **Eleven checks, not ten** — that document names class conformance separately, as an element of a class contract, and it is a different question from ABI conformance; certifying on either alone certifies half a driver. The two are declared twice, in Rust and in ISL, and cross-checked by two exhaustive `match` functions, so drift stops the build rather than a test. `userspace/certifier` runs the two checks a peer can make and **refuses to certify**, which is the first boot check here whose success is a refusal. Its ABI check is the one form a peer can make: every reply is self-describing, and the size the driver declared is compared against the type the reader decoded — not what the host goldens ask, and not vacuous, because `DestroySession` answers with a control reply where a data request went. **All three inversions were run.** Making `is_certified()` read `passed == ran`: caught, 1343. The certifier recording a check it never ran: caught, 1344 — the failure the whole facility exists for. The ABI check trusting the method rather than the declaration: caught, 1341. **What is NOT proven.** Nine of the eleven checks, which is what the machine says out loud. The `driver` field is 32 bits and the manifest's driver signature is 64, so boot passes the low half — survivable exactly because that field was never the identity; the artifact's measurement is, which is why the record carries a digest, and nothing measures one yet. A certificate has **no validity window**, because nothing in this tree has a trustworthy wall clock to stamp one with; `docs/architecture/02`'s declared support windows need that clock first. And the subject comes from boot rather than from the certifier, because a client cannot observe who answers its channel. | `bazel test //...` green including the new `certification_boot_aarch64_test`; certifying on two checks, claiming an unrun check, or trusting the method over the declaration each fail it |
| D162 | **The records a driver emitted, read back (Phase 6.2, trace schema).** The second of the eleven certification checks to become a verdict, and the first that no peer could have produced. `api/trace-schema` validates what `kcore::event` emitted against what `kernel_event.isl` says it should be. **The clauses are exactly the ones the decoder cannot make.** A generated decoder refuses a record whose `kind`, `severity`, `component` or `classification` is outside its enum, and says nothing at all about any field that is not an enum — so a record can decode perfectly with its timestamp zero, its causal id zero, and payload slots the schema never described holding whatever the emitter put there. Such a record renders without complaint and joins to nothing. The validator therefore takes **plain integers**, for the reason `api/class-conformance` takes a `u32` status: a type that could only hold values already inside the enums could never hold the evidence. **`CATALOG` is the schema's prose made checkable** — one entry per kind, saying how many of the four payload slots the schema gives a meaning to and which component the kind is filed under. The arity is the load-bearing part: a slot the schema does not describe is an undeclared payload, shown by no renderer and classified by no reviewer, which is the one place in a structured-logging facility where a value travels without anybody agreeing it should. A slot documented *reserved* counts as declared-to-be-nothing and must be zero. The component clause is `kernel_event.isl`'s own argument about the component it added last: a security decision filed where nobody reads for security decisions is not lost and not readable either. **The vantage is the point.** The certifier holds one channel and no view of the kernel's ring, so this outcome is one it could never have observed — and a runner that recorded only what a single vantage can see would be capped at that vantage forever. So the certificate of record is assembled in boot from the certifier's two checks and this third, which is the seam every remaining check arrives through. Read with `tail` rather than `drain`, because the ring is drained once per boot by `device_events_check` and judging the records at the cost of destroying them is the worse trade; scoped to the driver's process, because the machine's whole ring answers a question about a driver nobody is certifying. **All four inversions were run**: a value in an undeclared slot (caught, naming `DeviceDmaUnscoped`), an envelope with no timestamp and no causal id (caught), records filed under the wrong component (caught), and a run that examined no records at all (caught by its own claim — the verdict line carries the count, because "the records were well formed" is unfalsifiable from a log that does not say how many there were). **What is NOT proven.** Seven records, from one driver, on one boot: the catalog's other forty-odd kinds are transcribed assertions no emitter here exercises, and a disagreement between the table and an emitter is a finding waiting to be had — `DEVICE_RESET` already looks like one, since its schema calls `arg3` reserved and `Executive::device_reset` emits a policy value there. Nothing validates the *meaning* of a declared slot, only that undeclared ones are empty. And `Classification` has one value, so nothing here exercises redaction; the clause that would is absent rather than passing. | `bazel test //...` green including `certification_boot_aarch64_test`, now over three checks; an undeclared slot, an empty envelope, a wrong component or an empty run each fail it |
| D163 | **A KASLR draw that mapped over something (x86-64 fix).** `smoke_boot_test` failed roughly one boot in forty, which is the shape of bug a suite does not catch and a person stops believing. `choose_direct_map_base` picked one randomized PML4 slot out of eighty as though the direct map occupied one slot; on `q35` the boot memory map reaches a terabyte — device ranges, not RAM — so the map covers **two**, and a base one slot below a fixed region reached into it. Two fixed ranges were also missing from the reserved list entirely: the architecture-conformance scratch range at slot 352, whose own comment claimed to be *"clear of the direct map"* with nothing enforcing it, and the PCI far-read window at slot 320. Each accounted for two draws in eighty; the second was found only because fixing the first left a residue. **The fix is three things**: the chooser knows the span in slots, the reserved list is written as the named addresses rather than as bare slot numbers so a missing one is something a reader can look for, and `direct_map_choice_is_sound` walks **every one of the eighty draws** at each boot instead of only the draw taken — turning a 2.5% boot failure into a deterministic one. That check measures the span in *bytes* from `max_phys` and compares addresses, deliberately not sharing the chooser's slot arithmetic: the first version did share it, agreed with whatever the chooser assumed, and passed the broken chooser cleanly. **What is NOT proven: that the list is complete.** Inverted by hand — removing the far-read window leaves the self-check passing on every boot and the machine flaking once in forty again, which is exactly the historical failure. The check proves the chooser respects the list; nothing proves the list names every fixed higher-half address, and the mitigation is that the entries are now constants a reader can compare against the file. Verified over 40 consecutive boots after the fix (0 failures, 41 distinct bases across runs) against 2–3 failures per 40 before it. | 40 consecutive `smoke_boot_test` boots clean; assuming a one-slot span fails every boot with a named message rather than one in forty |
| D164 | **Fuzzing, generated from the schema (Phase 6.3).** `docs/lifecycle/02` ("Tier 2") asks for *"structure-aware fuzz targets generated from ISL … parsers and binary interfaces have mandatory fuzz targets that fail CI if absent"*. All three clauses are built. **Why generated rather than byte-level.** A decoder for a frozen struct refuses nearly every random byte string and refuses it *at the first strict-enum field*, so a byte fuzzer spends its whole budget rediscovering the layout the compiler already holds and reaches the decoder behind the guard almost never. `islc --fuzz` emits what it cannot construct: field offsets and widths, the legal domains, and **a seed record that decodes**. **The domains are what make an oracle**, which is the difference between this and a crash-hunt — a permitted value must still decode or the decoder is narrower than the type it implements; an undeclared enum value or an undeclared `bits` bit must be refused; anything that decodes must re-encode to the same bytes, because two byte strings naming one value is how a signature over bytes and a check over values come apart; a short buffer must be refused. 26 schemas, **122 frozen structs, ~244,000 inputs**. Runs are `(target, seed, iteration)` and nothing else, so a finding reproduces from three numbers; the seed is fixed rather than drawn, because a fuzz test that explored somewhere new every CI run would fail on unrelated changes and pass on the retry. **The absence gate** is the one tier-0 gate that complains about something not there, so it derives its expected population from the schema directory rather than from the fuzz suite — a suite missing a target looks exactly like one that never had it. Demonstrated: with one target renamed away, `bazel test //api/isl:all` reports **59 of 59 passing** while the gate names what is gone. **`Check::Fuzz` is the first outcome from a vantage that is not the machine at all.** A test result is not a build input, so a kernel compiled with "the fuzzing passed" would state something its own build never checked; instead `fuzz_all` links every target, exits non-zero on a finding, and a genrule runs it to produce the record the kernel links — so **a kernel that fuzzed badly is a kernel that did not build**, and the evidence is the artifact existing. Boot still checks the record is *substantive* (targets exist, more inputs than targets, and a two-sided run), because a stubbed record would compile, link and claim a run over nothing. **Inversions run**: strict enums accepting undeclared values (caught at iteration 0), `bits` fields accepting undeclared bits (caught at iteration 1), a seed the schema refuses (caught before any mutation), a deleted target (suite green, gate red), a new schema with no target, a listed parser nobody fuzzes, a decoder bug (the kernel fails to build), and a stubbed evidence record (boot fails 1349). **What is NOT proven.** The hand-written parsers get a far weaker oracle — return rather than panic — because the compiler knows nothing about a parser it did not write; that weakness is the argument for generating one wherever a schema can. Their list is the gate's soft spot: nothing can derive it, and a parser nobody adds to it is a parser nobody notices. There is no corpus persistence and no coverage feedback, so this is the pre-merge bounded run and not the continuous fleet that document puts post-merge. And a real finding is recorded rather than fixed: **all eight class contracts declare a `bits` feature type and then use `features: uint64`**, so a driver's `Describe` can advertise a bit nobody declared and no decoder objects — typing those fields would tighten eight contracts' accept behaviour and is a decision of its own. | `bazel test //...` green with 27 new tests; a decoder that accepts an undeclared value fails the kernel *build*, and a deleted target fails the gate while the suite stays green |
| D165 | **What a driver actually holds (Phase 6.4, security policy).** The fifth check, and the second whose vantage is boot rather than the certifier. Both halves existed and had never met: `api/binding`'s manifest declares what a driver bound by an entry may reach, the kernel decides what handles it ends up with, and **nothing compared them — nothing could**. Every accessor on a handle table starts from a handle the caller already has, so it can only confirm what the asker already knew; a capability a driver was given by mistake is one nobody thinks to look up. `HandleTable::audit` is that read, and `api/policy-compliance` is the comparison. **The framing that shapes the rule**: a capability system's failures are rarely refusals, which are loud and immediate. They are *grants nobody meant to make* — a handle installed with one bit more than it needed, a capability arriving by transfer carrying rights the receiver was never entitled to. None of those errors; the system works, with more authority in the wrong place than anybody chose. So the rule is **subset, not equality**: holding less than the manifest allows is a driver that did not need what it was offered, and holding more is the failure whatever route it took. The baseline is deliberately narrow — read, write, map, signal, wait, duplicate, transfer — and what it excludes is the point: `ADMIN`, `REVOKE`, `FIRMWARE`, `WAKE` and `PROTECTED_DMA` are authority over something other than this driver's own device. Rights are held as a plain `u64` for the reason class-conformance holds a status as `u32`: a bit no catalog defines is exactly what this reports. Boot reads three capabilities out of the driver's own table, and the verdict line says three, because "held only what it was allowed" is unfalsifiable from a log that does not say how many were looked at. **What is NOT proven, and an inversion proves it is not.** The manifest entry is **restated in boot** (`CERTIFIED_POLICY`) because the real one lives in a ring-3 manager and there is no protocol for the manager to report the entry it matched. So the check compares a driver against a policy *this file* asserts, and a third inversion — loosening the restated policy to grant `DERIVE` the real entry never grants — **passes**, because the driver does not hold `DERIVE` and a subset test is blind to a ceiling nobody raised into. A too-permissive policy is therefore undetectable here; the fix is a manager that reports its matched entry, which is a milestone rather than a corner of this one. Also unproven: the security domain is carried and compared against nothing, and one driver on one boot is the whole sample. | `bazel test //...` green; `certification_boot_aarch64_test` over five checks, and a driver handed one undeclared right, or a process audited as holding nothing, each fail it at 1349 |
| D166 | **A check that fails, and says why (Phase 6.5, DMA).** The sixth check, and the first the certificate records as *failed* rather than passed or unrun. **The question turned out to be prior to the one the plan named.** Every SMMU check here already proves the hardware refuses what it should; none asks whether *this driver's* memory goes through an aperture at all — and a driver handed a physical address has no fault to handle, because there is nothing for a fault to be raised against. So `Check::DmaFault` reads the driver's own records for `DEVICE_DMA_SCOPED` against `DEVICE_DMA_UNSCOPED`, which `kernel_event.isl` already keeps apart precisely so a scoped grant is a positive record rather than the absence of a warning. It requires at least one *scoped* grant: reading "nothing came back unscoped" off a driver that asked for no DMA is the empty claim again. **On this rig the answer is no, and the machine says so.** Wiring the device behind the SMMU was attempted and abandoned on evidence: `register_stream` succeeds, translations install, and the device then stalls with **zero translation faults** — on bus zero and behind a `pcie-root-port` alike — so QEMU's SMMU is not translating for a virtio-crypto function and the driver's IOVAs are read as physical addresses. That is the emulator's device model, not this kernel: the same rig's `smmu_boot` shows ring-3 scoped DMA working for the `edu` device. **Recording it as a failure is the point.** A certificate that collapsed "failed" into "not run" would lose the only thing telling an unfit driver from an absent rig, and this is the case that makes the distinction earn its keep — five passed, one failed and named its reason, five never asked. The boot check asserts that exact shape, so a future change that quietly makes this pass or quietly stops asking both fail. **Inversions run**: counting an unscoped grant as containment, and letting a driver that made no grants pass by vacuity — each caught at 1349. **What is NOT proven.** That any driver's DMA is contained end to end on this rig; that the driver *reports* a fault rather than retrying, which was the plan's wording and needs a device that can be made to fault while a ring-3 driver holds it. Also uncovered: `LEASE_LEN` is two pages and the crypto driver wants three, so even with a working IOMMU the default lease would refuse its third buffer — found during the attempt, left unchanged because nothing uses it yet, and the first thing to fix when a translating device exists. | `certification_boot_aarch64_test` requires six checks with exactly one failure; counting unscoped as contained, or passing on no grants at all, each fail it |
| D167 | **The guest's half of hotplug, on the tick (Phase 6.7, step one).** A prerequisite rather than a check: **a device could not leave this machine while ring 3 was running.** A hot-pluggable slot does not lose its device — the port raises an eject request and *waits* for the guest to answer through the slot control register, because the software using the device is the only thing that knows whether it is mid-transfer. `hotplug_check` answers in a boot loop with no thread alive, which is the one situation a driver is never in; during `Scheduler::run` the boot CPU is inside the scheduler and nothing polls. Measured rather than assumed: `device_del` on a device behind a root port, driven mid-boot over QMP, left the machine finishing cleanly at exit 33 with the device still attached. Two further facts came from the same runs: `pcie.0` refuses hotplug outright (*"Bus 'pcie.0' does not support hotplugging"*), so there is no surprise-removal path to fall back on, and **the removal path above the slot already exists end to end** — the kernel wakes a parked driver with `IRQ_PORT_SIGNAL_REMOVED`, distinct from an interrupt, and `userspace/device-host` answers `BlockError::Removed` to *both* clients because either may be the one blocked in a call. That code had never run: nothing in the tree referenced its report value, because nothing could make a removal happen while it was serving. **The fix is one hook.** The periodic tick is the only thing that fires during a run, so the slot watch lives there: answer the eject once, then watch config space, because acknowledging is a request to de-energize and what makes a device *gone* is that it stops answering. It holds no references — `tessera_pci::Host` and `EcamWindow` are five numbers between them, so the hook rebuilds them from atomics rather than pointing into a boot frame a later check reuses. Safe against the dispatch hook because AArch64 masks interrupts on exception entry, so a tick cannot preempt it. Proven with a bridge in a slot that **no driver holds**, so a failure is about the mechanism and not about a driver. **Two bugs found by doing it.** The hook first incremented `OBSERVED_TICKS`, which belongs to `timer_check` and is never reset while the architecture's own counter is reset by `start_periodic` — so that check found its threshold already crossed before its timer had ticked once. Then restoring `on_tick` afterwards did the same thing more slowly, through every later ring-3 run; the state to restore was *no hook*, and there is no way to unregister one, so a no-op hook is what goes back. **Inversions run**: never answering the eject is caught at 1452, and installing no watching hook at all is caught at 1451 — the claim that the tick looked *while ring 3 was still running*, read before the post-run settle can add to it. Both took 208 seconds first, which is why the settle bound came down from 20,000 ticks to 2,000: a bound sized so nothing can hit it makes every negative result expensive. **What is NOT proven.** That a *driver's own* device can be pulled and its client told `REMOVED` — the victim here is held by nobody. That is step two, and with this hook and `device-host`'s existing answer it is now mostly wiring. | `certification_boot_aarch64_test` drives `device_del` over QMP after an armed marker and requires the tick to have looked during the run; never answering the slot, or not watching, each fail it |
| D168 | **A driver held to its own word about power (Phase 6.9).** The seventh check, and the second a peer can make without leaving the channel. A different question from `api/power`, which arbitrates *between* voters: this is prior to that one — when the answer reaches a single driver, does the driver do what it says? **Checkable from outside because it holds a driver to its own `Describe` reply**, the same leverage `api/class-conformance` uses for optional methods: advertising a state is a claim it can be reached, and not advertising one is a claim it cannot. Neither half needs a wattmeter. Four rules: an advertised state is reached; an unadvertised one is refused; every reply names a state the driver actually claimed; and **a refusal does not move the device**. The last is the one nothing else in the tree catches, and it is `docs/lifecycle/04`'s "No Silent Fallback" in the direction people forget — not a degradation reported as success, but a *change reported as an error*, which is invisible to every client that reads the status and stops reading. The certifier asks for all four states the class names rather than only the advertised ones, because half the rules are about what a driver does with a state it does not have, and a run that asked only for what it advertised could never reach them; it ends by asking for `Active` again, so the driver is left where the contract says it starts. The `settled` state is tracked from the contract's defined initial state rather than from the first reply, so a driver whose very first answer moves without permission is caught. **Inversions run, and the first one is worth recording because it did not fire.** Making the driver refuse and move to `Idle` **passed** — at that point in the run the device was already in `Idle`, so the inversion moved nothing and the rule was never tested. Re-run moving to `Active` instead: caught at 1347. An unadvertised state quietly accepted: also caught at 1347, and it breaks two rules at once because a driver that accepts `Off` then reports a state it never advertised. **What is NOT proven.** Nothing here observes power: the device may report `Idle` and draw exactly what it drew before, and no rule here would know. Resume latency, the `resume_latency_us` every class contract carries, is read by nobody. And this is one driver whose advertised set is two of four states — a driver advertising all four would exercise the reachability rule far harder and the refusal rules not at all. | `certification_boot_aarch64_test` over seven checks; a refusal that moves the device to a state it was not in, or an unadvertised state quietly accepted, each fail it at 1347 |
| D169 | **A resume proved by working afterwards (Phase 6.8).** The eighth check, and the third a peer can make from inside the channel. **Nothing about a resume can be checked by looking at the resume.** A driver that brings its device back and one that returns `Ok` over a dead device reply identically — same status, same reported state — so the reply carries no information about the thing it is claiming. The only distinguishing act is *doing something with the device afterwards* and getting the answer it gave before. So the check encrypts, suspends to `Idle`, resumes to `Active`, and encrypts the identical input again, requiring the identical ciphertext. **The same session across the round trip, deliberately.** A driver that rebuilt its state from nothing would serve a *fresh* session perfectly and answer a surviving one with `NO_SESSION`, and only the second is a resume — a suspend is not allowed to quietly cost a client the state it was holding. That the answer is comparable at all is a property of the subject rather than of the check: a cipher over a fixed session gives a fixed answer, which is why the crypto driver is a better suspend/resume subject than a block device whose next read would succeed either way. **Both inversions run**: a suspend that clears its sessions, and a resume that comes back with a zeroed IV — the device is alive, answers `Ok`, and gives different bytes. Each is caught at 1349. The second is the interesting one, because every status in the exchange is a success and only the *content* disagrees. **What is NOT proven.** That the device was ever actually powered down — `SetPower(Idle)` on this driver sets a field, and nothing here or anywhere measures a rail. B22 and B23, the suspend-entry and resume-to-first-frame budgets, are not measured; this check is about correctness across the transition and says nothing about how long it took. And the round trip is one level deep: `Standby` and `Off` are refused by this driver, so the deepest state anything here suspends to is the shallowest one there is. | `certification_boot_aarch64_test` over eight checks; a suspend that loses its sessions, or a resume that returns the wrong bytes, each fail it at 1349 |
| D170 | **Telling a caller its server died (Phase 6.6, the mechanism).** Not a check: the thing a crash-recovery check needs in order to have anything to ask. **A process blocked in a synchronous call to a driver that dies was never woken.** It stays `Blocked` for the rest of the boot, waiting for a reply nothing will ever send, and no amount of restarting the driver reaches it — which is the plan's own sentence, *"restart alone is not recovery if the client is still waiting"*, describing the tree as it stood. The asymmetry was visible: `remove_one_device` already wakes a driver parked on the interrupt of a departed device *"knowing which of the two happened"*, and nothing did the equivalent for a channel. **`Executive::close_endpoint` already existed, and was correct** — it closes a side, raises peer-closed, and wakes a caller awaiting a reply or a receiver parked on it. Its only caller was a unit test. So this is wiring, not invention: `close_endpoints_of` walks the channels whose object a dying process held, read from the process's own handle table (`HandleTable::audit`, D165) rather than from a list kept alongside, because a channel given late or arriving by transfer is exactly the one a separate list forgets. **The scope was found by breaking the tree twice.** Closing every endpoint on every thread exit took **18 of 24 boot checks** down: a ring-3 program reaching the end of its work is the ordinary case, its peers are still running, and closing their channels is a teardown nobody asked for. Restricting to the *fault* path left **4 timeouts**, all built on driver restart — because a device manager sits in a receive on the very channel the replacement driver will be bound over, and telling it the peer is gone ends the conversation recovery depends on. The distinction that survives both is the one that was always the point: **close only where somebody is awaiting a reply** (`pending_caller`), never where a peer is merely parked in a receive. A caller mid-call is waiting for *this* process specifically; a receiver is waiting for work, and work can come from a replacement. **What is NOT proven.** No boot check exercises it. The wiring runs on every ring-3 fault in the tree and closes nothing, because no check has a caller mid-call when a driver dies — which is precisely the check 6.6 still needs, and it needs a second spawn inside `certification_check` because a crash that hangs the certifier destroys the transcript the other eight checks depend on. **Also recorded here, measured while choosing what to do next: the performance gate of 6.10 is not achievable on this rig.** Five identical boots of B7 context-switch gave p50 of 1888, 1904, 1888, 2960 and 2736 ns — a **57% spread on an unchanged binary**. `docs/architecture/03` gates a regression at 5% and puts the budget suite on dedicated bare-metal runners; on TCG a 5% gate would fail more often than it passed. `Check::PerfRegression` stays `NotRun` with that measurement as its reason, rather than becoming a green check that measures scheduler noise. | `bazel test //...` green with four new kcore tests; closing every endpoint instead of only the awaited ones fails 18 boot checks, and closing on clean exits as well as faults fails 4 more |
| D171 | **The check that makes D170 live (Phase 6.6).** The ninth check, and the first with a run of its own. It has to be separate: a client left without an answer destroys the transcript the other eight are built on, and `crash_recovery_check` builds a fresh executive anyway, so it runs first and hands its verdict in. **The crash is placed where it has to be.** The driver's startup bit makes it die *after taking a request and before answering it* — not after binding, because a call to an already-dead server is refused at the door (`channel_call` checks for a closed peer before it parks), which would exercise the wrong path entirely. What has to be provoked is the other case: a caller **already parked**, waiting for a reply from a process about to stop existing. **The evidence is that the client reported at all.** A thread still blocked writes nothing, so the report count distinguishes a client that came back from one that is still waiting — there is no third outcome to confuse it with, and the run simply ends with the thread parked. Two further conditions: the client's report must be a channel-call failure rather than a success (a client that came back claiming success would be worse than one that hung), and the driver must actually have faulted, or the run proved nothing and says so at 1540 rather than passing on a crash that never happened. **Both inversions run.** Removing `close_endpoints_of_current` from the fault path — the world as it stood before D170 — fails the check: the client parks, the driver dies, and nothing connects them. A driver told not to crash fails at 1540. **What is NOT proven.** The client here discovers its server died and stops; it does not retry, and nothing rebinds it to the replacement, so this is half of recovery — the half that was missing. `driver_rebind_check` proves the other half, that a fresh driver is bound to the same transport, and no single check yet spans both. The crash is a page fault at address zero, which is one way to die; a driver that hangs rather than faults is not covered by anything here, and a hang has no event to hook. | `certification_boot_aarch64_test` over nine checks; removing the wake from the fault path, or telling the driver not to crash, each fail it |
| D172 | **The signed update channel (Phase 6.11).** `docs/drivers/01` ends on *"certified drivers can be distributed through signed update channels"*, and this is the half that **consumes** a verdict. Everything earlier in the phase produced certificates and nothing read one; a certificate nobody checks is a record rather than a decision. **The crate parses the manifest itself**, and that is the whole reason it has a parser: a caller that decoded the bytes and handed the fields over would leave a seam between what was *signed* and what was *judged*, which is where every interesting attack on a signed artifact lives. Here the bytes the signature covers are the bytes the rules read. Four conditions, each a different conversation to have, which is why the refusals are distinct values rather than one "no": the manifest is signed by the channel's anchor; the driver is certified in the full sense — every check *ran* and passed; the certificate is about *this* driver, contract version included; and the certificate **names the bytes being shipped**, which is where `certification.isl`'s statement that an unmeasured certificate is "evidence about no bytes, and a channel is the thing that must refuse it" finally has a refuser. The rollback floor sits outside all of that: a correctly signed, fully certified image below the floor is still refused, because `docs/security/02` makes that the system's decision and not the producer's — and it is checked *after* certification, so a driver that is both uncertified and stale hears the reason raising the floor would not fix. **The channel joins the fuzz gate's hand-written parser list**, since it reads a manifest offered by whoever is distributing drivers, before its signature has been believed — a length field trusted too early is the mistake a signed format is supposed to make impossible. **Three inversions run, and the third had to be run three times.** Admitting on a certificate that merely exists fails three tests; accepting one that names no bytes fails its own. Weakening the signature to cover only the header **passed twice**: first because every test that reached the signature used a manifest with *no entries*, so header-only and full-length were indistinguishable; then because the edit rewrote the assertion along with the code. The fix was to make the signed range observable — `signed_len` is public and tested on its own — which is the only way a tree that can verify but cannot sign can prove what a signature covers. **What is NOT proven.** Nothing here signs, so no test exercises a manifest that genuinely verifies; the positive path is `admit` alone, with the signature step resting on `api/ed25519`'s vectors. There is no key custody: an anchor is a byte array a caller supplies, and where it comes from, how it rotates and what revokes it are untouched. And no boot check consumes a channel — this is the rule, not yet a consumer. | `bazel test //...` green with eleven new tests; admitting an uncertified driver, an unmeasured certificate, or a signature over less than the manifest each fail it |
| D173 | **A signer, so the channel's positive path exists (Phase 6.11, second half).** D172 recorded that nothing in the tree signed, which meant `api/update-channel` **could have refused everything and passed every test it had** — no test could produce a manifest that genuinely verifies, and a mutation test had nothing to mutate, since changing a byte of a manifest that never verified proves nothing about one that did. `tools/ed25519-signer` produces one. **It lives under `tools/` and that is the enforcement**: signing holds a secret, verification does not, and the second is what makes a hand-written implementation defensible — so the crate that holds a key is one nothing in `kernel/` or `userspace/` may depend on. `api/ed25519` stays verify-only and its header's claim stands unchanged; the one thing it now exposes is `base_mul`, a *public* group operation, so the curve arithmetic exists once rather than twice. One implementation with one set of vectors behind it is worth more than two of anything. **The reduction is deliberately the slow algorithm.** Reference implementations reduce mod `L` with a hand-derived 21-bit limb schedule that is fast and very easy to get subtly wrong; this does shift-and-subtract long division, runs once per signature on a build machine, and optimises for being visibly correct. **Anchored by reproducing RFC 8032's signatures byte for byte** — Ed25519 draws no nonce, so there is exactly one right answer and "it verifies" is the weaker claim. That passed on the first run, which is the evidence the scalar arithmetic is right. **The mutation test that was impossible now runs**: every byte of a genuinely signed manifest, flipped one at a time, must break the signature — which is what finally proves the signed range covers the entries and not just the header, the property that took three attempts to test at all. **Inversions run**: a signature covering only the header fails three round-trip tests, and a signer that skips RFC 8032's clamping fails the published vectors. **What is NOT proven.** There is no key custody of any kind. The anchor is a byte array a caller supplies and the secret is an argument; where a key comes from, how it rotates, what revokes it, and whether it ever leaves an HSM are all untouched — `docs/security/02`'s signing infrastructure is a milestone this does not begin. Nothing here is constant-time, which for a build machine trusted with the key by the act of holding it is a statement about the machine rather than about this code. And no boot check consumes a channel: the rule and its fixture exist, and no machine yet installs anything through one. | `bazel test //...` green with two new test targets; a signature covering less than the manifest, or a signer that skips clamping, each fail |
| D174 | **The driver SDK's first two pieces (Phase 6.12).** `docs/drivers/01` ("Developer Experience") lists nine; the ISL compiler existed, and `userspace/sdk` adds the **host template** and the **simulator hooks**, which are the two everything else on that list is written against. **The boilerplate was measured, not guessed**: across the ring-3 programs here `channel_args` is written out nineteen times, `exit_reporting` twenty-one, `map_device` eleven and the bind handshake twelve — each a near-copy with its own invented failure codes, and none of it driver logic. **The seam is the operation, not the syscall**, and that is the design decision the crate turns on: a trait of syscalls would leave a simulator obliged to speak `ChannelMsgArgs` and a driver author still obliged to know what one is. With `Platform`'s methods being *call*, *receive*, *respond*, *map* and *allocate*, a driver is generic over where it runs, and what executes on a developer's machine is **the driver itself rather than something like it** — which is what makes `docs/lifecycle/02`'s "a class contract is testable before any hardware exists" true rather than aspirational. **The simulator is scripted rather than modelled**, because the interesting cases are not what a working device does: a `Script` can refuse a binding, refuse a mapping, or hand back a client that leaves mid-conversation, so `docs/drivers/01`'s fault injection arrives as the same mechanism instead of a second one. And it **hands back two different addresses for one page** deliberately — a simulator that returned the requested VA as the device address would pass a driver that conflates them and that a machine with an IOMMU refuses, which is the class of bug a simulator should catch rather than hide. **Inversions run**: blurring the two addresses fails three tests including the example driver's own check; treating a departed client as a failure fails two, which is the D170 case a driver would otherwise report as broken every time it was merely finished; and accepting a bind reply too short to hold what it claims fails the refusal test. **What is NOT proven, and it is the main thing.** *No shipped driver uses this.* The consumer is an example driver in the crate's own tests — real in the sense that it names no syscall, no kernel type and no handle it was not given, and small in every other sense. The SDK's real claim, that a driver can be written from it alone, is only established for a driver that does nothing hard; converting one of the six real drivers is what would test it, and **whatever that conversion could not express would be the finding**. Also deferred with no consumer yet, rather than stubbed: the trace viewer, the DMA test harness beyond what the simulator already gives, the power-state emulator, firmware packaging, and the certification runner as a command. Nothing here is a safety boundary — a driver is still confined by the kernel, and what this removes is the need to *read* one. | `bazel test //...` green with seven new tests; a simulator that blurs a page's two addresses, a serve loop that fails on a departed client, or a bind that accepts a short reply each fail it |
| D175 | **A real driver written against the SDK (Phase 6.12, the test of D174).** D174 recorded that the SDK's claim was unproven because no shipped driver used it, and that *whatever a conversion could not express would be the finding*. `blk-driver` is converted: it reads the disk on a real RISC-V machine through `userspace/sdk`, 111 lines removed against 66 added, and it now names **no syscall number and no ISL argument struct**. What remains is virtio, address arithmetic, and volatile access to a window the kernel mapped — the parts genuinely its own. **Three findings.** The SDK could not express this driver at all: it was built around bind-and-serve, and this one waits on an interrupt and serves nothing, so `Platform` gained `wait_for_interrupt` and `interrupt_complete` — taking a handle and returning a source, because a driver never names an interrupt line. A driver can be *given* its device rather than binding for one, which `bind` cannot represent since it assumes a manager and a class contract; naming that case keeps it from reading as a driver that forgot to bind. And `map_device` returned the address that was *asked for* rather than the kernel's answer — they agree today, so the boot test passed either way, and a driver resting on that agreement breaks the first time they do not, which is the exact assumption a platform layer exists to prevent. **The inversions found something better than a bug.** Conflating a page's two addresses fails the boot test. Replacing `ReplyContinue` with plain `Reply` — the mistake this tree has made twice (D85, D91) — **passed**, and so did a stale `ChannelMsgArgs` version, because `blk-driver` never calls, receives or responds. **The machine platform's entire channel half is written and unrun**: the smallest driver was the easiest to convert and exercises the least. Converting a *serving* driver — crypto, gpu or snd — is what would close it, and until then the SDK is proven for the device half only. **Also recorded, and not from here**: `snd_boot_aarch64_test` failed three times in five full-matrix runs and passed six for six in isolation. The aarch64 kernel image is byte-identical with and without this change and `blk-driver` is not among its dependencies, so the flake predates this and appears only under concurrent load — an owner and a cause are both still missing. | `virtio_blk_boot_riscv64_test` passes with the driver on the SDK; conflating a page's two addresses fails it, while breaking the reply form does not, which is the gap this entry records |
| D176 | **A check that asserted a real-time guarantee nobody makes (snd flake fix).** `snd_boot_aarch64_test` failed three times in five when the boot matrix ran its other twenty-three QEMUs alongside, and six for six in isolation — the shape that invites blaming the harness. It was not the harness. The machine booted fully and the check failed on its own claim: the fed stream reported **six periods played and one gap**, and the claim required *zero* gaps. **That is a real-time property, asserted on a host that promises nothing about when a ring-3 program runs.** The client supplies one period per syscall round trip and the emulated device consumes in real time; on an idle machine the client wins, and on a busy one it does not. Nothing about the driver differed between the passing and failing runs. **The check already knew.** Its own verdict line says *"NOT proven here: that a stream can be KEPT fed. This emulated device consumes faster than a client supplying one period per syscall round trip"* — while the pass condition required exactly that. Two sentences in one check, contradicting each other, and only one of them was enforced. **The fix is to claim what the machine can guarantee**: the fed stream played the periods it was given. The gap count stays in the report's low half, where it was always carried, so a reader can still tell a stream that hiccupped from one that ran dry — and the contrast between an attentive client and a negligent one is the *starved* stream, which needs no timing to be true and was always the load-bearing half. **Verified**: six consecutive full-matrix runs clean, against three failures in five before. **Inversions run**: a driver whose streams never play fails at 942, and a driver blind to underruns fails at 943 — so both halves still catch a driver that is actually wrong. **What is NOT proven, and now says so honestly**: that a stream can be kept fed. It never was; the difference is that the check no longer claims it. Doing so needs the out-of-line grant this contract does not have, so a client can hand over a buffer rather than a period per round trip. | six consecutive `bazel test //tools/qemu:all` runs clean; a stream that plays nothing, or a driver that cannot see an underrun, each still fail |

| D177 | **A serving driver on the SDK, and the invented reply the simulator had been written to match (Phase 6.12).** D175 recorded that the SDK's channel half — `call`, `receive`, `respond` — was written and unrun, because the one driver on it waited on interrupts and never spoke to a client. `userspace/crypto-driver` now binds, maps its registers, takes its DMA pages, serves its class contract and exits through `userspace/sdk`, and **names no syscall, no argument-struct version and no field offset**: 273 lines became 221, and what is left is what crypto means. Converting it found the bug the first driver could not: `bind` returned a device handle and a register layout, and the `BindReply` a manager actually sends carries **neither**. It survived a full suite because the simulator had been written to match the invention — a simulator built from the template rather than from the protocol confirms the template, which is the failure mode a simulator is supposed to be immune to. The fix inverts the dependency: `bind` carries opaque bytes and reads none of them, and what the manager's answer means stays the driver's business. Two smaller gaps closed with it: `device_info` had no platform method at all, and no path re-read the bytes a syscall wrote, so `tessera_uabi::refresh_kernel_filled` is the run-time-length sibling of `read_kernel_filled` and the SDK is still `deny(unsafe_code)`. **Not proven: that the re-read is load-bearing.** Removing it passes both boots — `syscall2`'s inline asm clobbers memory, so the compiler reloads anyway. It stays because that is an artefact of one asm block's constraints and not a promise, and every other kernel-filled read in this tree is written the same way. | `crypto_boot` and `certification_boot` pass; a stale `ChannelMsgArgs` version and a plain `ChannelReply` in place of `ChannelReplyContinue` each fail both boots — the second reporting 0, the D85/D91 signature of a server blocked on its own client |

| D178 | **The verdict leaves the machine, and a runner that cannot invent one reads it back (Phase 6.12).** D161 built the rules and D171 built the channel that refuses an uncertified entry, and the two were joined only in code: `api/update-channel` takes `ran` and `passed` from a manifest entry, and nothing carried those numbers from the machine that observed them to the manifest that claims them. **A person typed them in.** Every property the channel checks rested on two integers a human supplied about a run nobody could re-examine — which is the same failure the phase opened on, one layer out. So boot now measures the driver's ELF, encodes the `certification.isl` record it had been building and discarding, and prints it as one line of hex; `certification_bindings` had existed since 6.1 with no producer and no consumer. `tools/certify` is the ninth thing `docs/drivers/01` asks a driver SDK for, and it reads that line: `ran` and `passed` are copied out of the record and never computed, there is no flag that overrides them, and a boot that printed nothing exits differently from a driver that failed — a tool that could be argued into certifying is a tool whose refusals mean nothing, and a rig that stopped asking must not arrive as an unfit driver. **The end-to-end result is a true negative from a real run**: this machine's crypto driver fails the DMA check and never ran two others, so the runner refuses it a channel and names all three. `Check::name` is new, because a refusal that named only a bit could not tell those two kinds apart. **Not proven: that the digest is of the right bytes.** Its presence is load-bearing — a certificate measuring nothing is refused, by boot's own test — but nothing downstream can tell one blob's hash from another's, and only a channel that ships the artifact beside the certificate would. | `certification_boot` passes end to end; the same record with every check moved into `passed` certifies, so the refusal comes from the observation and not from the tool; a boot emitting `DigestAlgorithm::NONE` is refused for measuring no artifact rather than for its checks |

| D179 | **The DMA test harness, and a driver that no longer forms its own pointer (Phase 6.12).** `docs/drivers/01` lists a DMA test harness beside the simulator hooks, and the simulator could not have had one: its `dma_alloc` handed back the address the driver asked for, and a driver that wrote through that address on a host would write through a number nobody mapped. **A driver that used DMA could not run against the model at all** — only one that allocated pages and left them alone, which is no driver. The fix is to what a driver is allowed to do, not to the model: `Platform::with_dma` lends a scoped slice, so the `from_raw_parts_mut` every driver had a private copy of lives once in `tessera_uabi::with_dma_page` and the model can hand back memory it owns instead. `userspace/sdk/src/dma.rs` then answers the three questions a return value cannot: what a device reading at an address would have found, whether any page still holds a given secret, and how many pages were granted or reached without being granted — a stray is counted rather than conjured, because a harness that invented a page would model a bug as working. `Script::dma_grants` replaces `allows_dma`, so the fault injection is a count and not a flag: **a driver whose error path only runs on its first allocation has an error path that has not run.** Two claims that had only ever been written down now run on a host with no emulator: that a driver publishes the *device's* address (on a machine with no IOMMU the wrong one works, so the machine cannot tell — the model makes the two differ on purpose), and that a driver handed a key does not leave it in a page, which `userspace/crypto-driver`'s header and `docs/security/02` both assert. The careless and the careful driver return the identical value; only the harness separates them, and both run as tests. **Not proven: that the real crypto driver clears its key.** It is an AArch64 `no_main` binary and cannot run on a host, so what the harness watches is the discipline and not that driver. Splitting it into a library so its own serve path runs here is the next step and a separate concern. | 124 tests pass; the model blurring the two addresses of a page fails both SDK tests, and a machine `with_dma` lending `device_address` instead of `va` fails the crypto boot |

| D180 | **A model that aliased two pages, found by reviewing the reviewer (Phase 6.12).** D179 built the DMA harness on the sentence that a model returning one number for a page's two addresses "would pass a driver that conflates them and that a machine with an IOMMU refuses". The transform it shipped was `0x4000_0000 | (va & 0xffff)` — different from `va`, which is what every test asserted, and **not injective**, which nothing asked. Every address in `tessera_uabi::layout` is megabyte-aligned, so the MMIO window, the DMA page and the queue rings all fold to `0x4000_0000`: a driver granting two of them got two pages under one device address, and `seen_by_device` answered about whichever was granted first. **The harness would have confirmed that a device could read a page it was never told about** — the same failure the entry was written to prevent, one level down, and invisible because the only multi-page test used addresses a page apart. Two more of the same kind came out of the same read. `grant` accepted a `va` it had already handed out, which a machine's `DmaAlloc` cannot do, leaving the second page addressable by a device and unreachable by the driver — a state no machine can be in, so a test reaching it would be testing nothing. And `holds` clamped its search range with `saturating_sub` and then indexed past the page, so **asking whether a page holds a secret larger than a page panicked the test that asked** — reachable from a harness's own public surface, and the answer is `false` by arithmetic rather than by fallback, since a page cannot contain more than a page. All three are the model's, not a driver's, which is the argument for the entry: a harness is believed by the tests written against it, and nothing was checking the harness. Bit 62 now separates the two address spaces, and no port's user half reaches it — AArch64's is 2^48, x86-64's 2^47, Sv39's 2^38, the 32-bit ports' 2^32 — so the mask is the identity on every address a driver can hold. **What is NOT proven, and is unchanged:** no shipped driver's DMA discipline is watched here, because the two that use the SDK are `no_main` binaries for other architectures; the harness watches the discipline and not them. | 124 tests pass; each fix reverted on its own fails its own test — the low-16 fold fails `pages_a_megabyte_apart_are_told_apart`, an unchecked `grant` fails `one_address_is_granted_once`, and an unguarded `holds` panics at `range end index 4097 out of range for slice of length 4096` |
| D181 | **The answer for a card that left, tested where a card can leave (Phase 6.12).** The SD boot check's header named a companion script that runs the same driver against an empty slot, and called the pair what makes `NO_MEDIUM` attributable to the card. That script was never committed, and running the machine it describes shows why it could not be: QEMU's `sdhci-pci` reports card-present in an empty slot, so the kernel's present-state probe reads true with no `sd-card` attached and the with-card verdict comes back unchanged. **The driver's medium-gone path was therefore executed by nothing** — the transport core's `NoCard` was covered by a mock, but `sd-host`'s translation of it into the block class's `NO_MEDIUM` sat in a `no_main` AArch64 binary no host test could call, and the tree was green either way. Closed at the tier that can run rather than papered over: the translation moved into `sd_host_medium`, generic over the transport's `Registers` trait so the driver supplies real MMIO and a test supplies a model; and the mock moved out of `kernel/sdhci`'s test module into `//kernel/sdhci-mock`, so the tier that turns an empty slot into `NoCard` and the tier that turns `NoCard` into `NO_MEDIUM` are held to **one** model — a second mock could disagree with the first about when a card is gone and both suites would pass while describing different hardware. `present` and `irq` became public for it, which is register-bit constants and not behaviour. **What is NOT proven:** the join — that the driver reached from ring 3, over a channel, through the kernel's mapping of the controller, answers the same way. That needs a card that can leave, and no emulator here has one; the boot check now says so instead of implying a script that would have. | 132 tests pass; 5 inversions, each failing the test that names its claim — dropping `NoCard` from the read path fails `a_card_that_goes_mid_request_is_no_medium_and_not_an_io_error`, a `check_presence` that acts on nothing fails `a_card_taken_out_between_requests_answers_no_medium`, making every failure `NO_MEDIUM` fails `a_controller_error_is_an_io_error_and_the_card_is_still_there`, restoring `ready` on re-insertion fails `a_card_put_back_is_not_served_until_it_is_identified_again`, and issuing a command with no card fails `a_request_with_no_card_issues_no_command`; one inversion of the shared mock — a card that leaves without clearing the presence bit — fails tests in **both** tiers, which is the one-model claim rather than an assertion of it; `sd_boot_aarch64_test` passes uncached |
