| D182 | **The package-coverage gate is blind in one direction under Bazel.** `//:all_srcs` is the hand-written list every tier-0 gate reads through, so a package missing from it is never inspected and every gate still reports success. `//tools/checks:packages_test` closes that, except for the half Bazel cannot answer from inside a test: the runfiles tree a test is handed *is* `//:all_srcs`, so an omitted package contributes no files and cannot be seen. The stale-label and per-package-filegroup checks have teeth under Bazel; the omitted-package check has teeth only under `cargo test -p tessera-checks`, which walks the repository itself. | A CI step enumerates packages independently (`bazel query 'filter(":srcs$", …)'` against a `find`), making the check hermetic rather than inner-loop-only. Lands with CI. |
| D183 | **The ports and the kernel binaries have never been linted, behind a comment saying they were.** `.bazelrc` claimed the `--config=lint` aspects "propagate through the kernel platform transition, covering crates that only build for bare metal". They do not: `platform_binary`/`platform_library` expose the transitioned target through their own `binary`/`library` attribute, and an aspect propagates along the attributes *it* declares (`deps`, `crate`), so it stops at the wrapper. Measured, the host config emits 231 clippy outputs across 64 packages and **none** for `karch-x86_64`, `karch-aarch64`, `karch-riscv64`, `karch-riscv32`, `karch-arm32`, `karch-arm-common`, `karch-riscv-common`, `width-conformance`, or any of the five kernel binaries — that is, for essentially every line of `unsafe` in the tree. The `--config=lint-<arch>` configs added beside it reach them, and report **505 findings**: `kernel/kernel` 170, `kernel-aarch64` 232, `kernel-riscv64` 90, `kernel-riscv32` 3, `kernel-arm32` 3, `karch-x86_64` 6, `karch-aarch64` 1 (each figure is the target and its dependencies under that platform). All are style-class lints — `deref_addrof`, `identity_op`, `unnecessary_cast`, `collapsible_if`, `needless_lifetimes` — none is a soundness finding, and none is fixed here: the 498 in the kernel binaries live in the five `main.rs` files, most of which the boot-check extraction is about to move, and fixing a lint in code that is being moved is work done twice. **What is NOT proven:** that the count stays at 505, because nothing yet fails a build when it grows. | The port crates' 7 findings fixed and the `--config=lint-<arch>` runs made part of CI; the kernel binaries' 498 fixed after the architecture-independent boot checks move out of the per-port `main.rs` files, at which point the remaining set is small enough to hold at zero. |
| D184 | **The boot checks asserted prose, and one of them asserted the wrong branch.** Twenty-four scripts grepped 136 substrings of the kernel's verdict lines — whole sentences, in several cases — which made the prose a test contract: no line could be shortened without breaking a test, and rewording one broke it silently. Each property is now a claim key (`claim <group>.<name>`, one per line, from `kcore::verdict::claims`), 113 of them, and the scripts match those instead. Five markers were matching more than one place in a source file; four of those are now pinned to the verdict that owns them, and the fifth was a real defect — `gpio_boot`'s wait-for-armed matched both the arming notice *and* the timed-out "nobody pressed" line, so the QMP button press was racing a marker that also appears on the failure path. The check now waits on the arming notice alone and runs in 2.6 s rather than 6.7 s. `pcie_boot_riscv64.sh` deliberately keeps its prose marker: it asserts a function count and a vendor id, which a static key would drop. **What is NOT proven:** that the claim set is complete — a property nobody wrote a key for is still a property nobody checks. | The verdict lines shortened to ≤150 characters (their prose moving into code comments), which the claim keys are what make possible; and the remaining prose marker replaced when that line is rewritten to carry the count explicitly. |

| D185 | **The verdict lines were prose on the wire, and now they are not.** Seventy-six format strings exceeded 150 characters, the longest 4,573 — nine paragraphs of argument printed to a serial console on one line. Each is now a message plus its values, with the prose moved verbatim into a comment above the emission, which is where `docs/lifecycle/04` says it belongs; five diagnostics too wide for one line became two, three or four separate messages rather than one wrapped one. `kprint!`/`kprintln!` now carry the envelope `docs/observability/01` mandates — `[<ticks> <module>] `, the module from `module_path!()` at the call site so it cannot drift, the tick from the same clock the event ring uses, read without blocking so the panic path cannot deadlock on it. The panic report became one enveloped line instead of two. Two checks hold the rule: a tier-0 gate on the format strings, and every boot check asserting it against the serial log the machine actually printed — the second is the one that matters, and it caught five lines the first could not, because a literal within budget still overruns once its values expand. The encoded certificate is exempt and says so: it is a fixed-size wire record `//tools/certify` reads back, not a message. **What is NOT proven:** that the envelope's tick field is a *time* — it is a counter delta from the first line, which orders lines within one boot and means nothing across two. | The tick rendered as elapsed microseconds, once the porting layer's `counter_hz` reaches the console; and the remaining prose in comments reduced rather than merely relocated. |

| D186 | **Test code lived inside the code it tests, and two fixtures shipped with it.** 20,888 lines across 71 source files were `#[cfg(test)] mod tests { … }` bodies — 66% of `kcore::dispatch.rs` alone, which read as a 9,000-line file and was a 3,000-line one. Each is now `#[cfg(test)] #[path = "tests/<module>.rs"] mod tests;` beside a sibling file, the convention nine device crates already used; the `#[path]` form keeps the test module a child of the module it tests, so `super::` still reaches private items and no visibility had to widen. Per-crate test counts are identical across all 24 crates (850 total), which is the only check that catches a moved test that quietly stops being compiled. Two fixtures came out of production code: `kcore::firmware`'s `set_test_store` into that module's test file, and the SDK's simulator into `//userspace/sdk-sim`, visible to test targets alone — the shape `karch-mock` and `sdhci-mock` already had. `//tools/checks:doubles_test` holds it: no `rust_binary`, `tessera_kernel_binary` or `tessera_user_binary` may depend on a package whose name ends `-mock` or `-sim`, matched by suffix so a mock added tomorrow is covered without anybody remembering. **What is NOT proven:** that the simulator was ever reaching a shipped image — it was not; `--gc-sections` dropped it, and the driver binaries carry no simulator symbols either side of this change. What it was reaching is the SDK's public surface, which is what a consumer builds against. | The two remaining `-mock` crates checked for the same suffix discipline when a third arrives; and `kcore::dispatch` split along its syscall families, now that the file is small enough to see them. |

| D187 | **Five copy-pasted build shapes became five rules.** `api/isl/BUILD.bazel` was 1,964 lines of thirty-one hand-written {genrule, bindings crate, conformance test} triples plus twenty-six fuzz families — 422 now, behind `isl_bindings`. The pipeline that turns a built ELF into a Rust byte array was written out thirty-two times; it is `tessera_embedded_elf` now. The kernel rule and the ring-3 rule each carried their own architecture table, agreeing about which platform an architecture builds for only because nobody had edited one of them; there is one table, in `build/rules/arch.bzl`, and `//tools/checks:flags_test` fails if a rule grows another. Thirty-one ring-3 linker scripts became two plus three shared ones. Twenty-four QEMU checks became `tessera_boot_check` calls. **Each was verified against the graph rather than by reading it**: `bazel query --output=build` before and after gives 176 identical ISL targets, 142 identical userspace/store targets, and 26 identical QEMU targets, with `rustc` command lines identical across all five kernels and three ring-3 binaries, and every one of the 63 user binaries byte-identical. **What is NOT proven:** nothing about the two scripts left standing — `roottask.ld` and `blk-driver.ld` differ from the shared layouts in `.got`, `.bss` and `COMMON` placement, and the byte comparison is what found that out after a comment-stripping heuristic had called them duplicates. | The remaining two folded in when their differences are understood to be incidental rather than assumed to be; and `//tools/qemu`'s twenty-four scripts sharing their QEMU invocation, which this leaves untouched. |

| D188 | **The SDK seam is the interface for three of twelve ring-3 drivers, and what the other nine need is now known rather than guessed.** `usb-hid` is converted: its syscall numbers, its hand-rolled `ChannelMsgArgs` encoding, its `patch_args`/`kernel_u32` volatile helpers and its receive/reply loop are gone, and 509 lines of it are USB and HID. The `usb` boot check passes and its serial output is line-for-line identical to before, bar the kernel image growing three pages, which is embedded content and not behaviour — a size comparison of the driver binary alone came back equal, so what those three pages are is not established. **A correction to what was recorded earlier:** `crypto-driver` was described as linking the SDK without going through it. It does go through it — it names no syscall at all — and its 1,059 lines are virtio-crypto logic, not boilerplate. The measure that matters is syscall constants per driver, not line count. Four more convert against the `Platform` trait as it stands (`gpu`, `snd`, `nvme`, `usb-storage`: every syscall they name has a method). Five need it to grow first, and by exactly this much: `gpio` a port signal; `sd-host` and `usb-host` a device declare; `net` a channel send, a DMA attach/detach and a memory create; `device-host` a DMA attach. **What is NOT proven:** that growing the trait for those five is the right shape rather than the first one that fits — a method added to satisfy one caller is how a platform seam becomes a syscall table with different names. | The four that fit converted; then each new `Platform` method added in its own change, with the driver that needed it as the argument for its shape. |

| D189 | **The first check that is not any architecture's.** `//kernel/boot-checks` holds checks written against `kcore` alone, as `//kernel/arch-conformance` holds the ones written against the porting layer alone. `device_events_check` — 174 lines reading the driver framework's own event records — was carried twice, by AArch64 and RISC-V 64, at 99% similarity; it is one copy now, and the two ports' `main.rs` lose 177 and 186 lines. A check here **reports and returns rather than exiting**: ending a run is the harness's business and the mechanism is the port's (Arm semihosting, a RISC-V test finisher, an x86 debug-exit device), so a check that called one could only ever live on one port. **The 1% was three things, and one was real:** a comment reworded, a local named `wire_ok` on one side and `envelope_ok` on the other, and — the real one — AArch64's failure line reporting `reclaimed` and `irq_revoked` where RISC-V 64's did not. Reconciled by reporting both everywhere, which changes a line that only prints when the check fails and costs RISC-V 64 one page of image. **What is NOT proven:** that this is a large share of the duplication. Measured across the five kernels, functions sharing a name total about 9,000 lines, but only **390** of those are copies alike enough (≥0.90) to unify without reading the divergence first; the rest share a name and not much else. The earlier claim that this extraction would remove most of D183's kernel-binary lint findings was wrong — the per-architecture counts are unchanged at 233 and 90. | The remaining ≥0.90 group — `load_user_elf`, `user_trap`, and the `arm32`/`riscv32`/`riscv64` user-entry family — moved the same way; then the 0.70–0.90 band, one function at a time, with each divergence read and stated rather than merged. |

| D190 | **Two more copies unified, and one that only looked like a copy.** `load_user_elf` — reserve writable, copy, narrow to what the segment declared, publish executable pages to the instruction cache — was carried by AArch64 and RISC-V 64 at 96%. It is `kcore::elf::load_into` now, generic over the porting layer, beside the parser it uses; the single real difference between the two copies was the `Machine` they parsed for, which is a parameter. `map_user_word` was carried by three ports at what the similarity table called 100%, and **it was not one function**: two took a `u32` and the third a `u64`, so the three wrote four bytes into the page or eight. It is `boot_checks::map_user_bytes` now, taking the bytes and leaving the width to the caller that knows its register size. `user_transform` diverges the same way and is *not* shared — a rotate over a `u32` and a rotate over a `u64` are two functions with one name — and each port's copy now says so. **What is NOT proven, and was asserted three times before it was checked:** that a high similarity score means two functions are the same. The score in D189's table was the best-matching *pair*, and on `kernel_sections`, `map_user_word` and `user_transform` the best pair agreed while another differed by register width. Compilation caught two of the three; the third was caught by reading. A similarity number is a place to look, not a reason to merge. | The 0.70–0.90 band taken one function at a time, each divergence read before the copies are joined rather than after the compiler objects. |

| D191 | **CI exists, and is a script rather than a provider's file.** `README.md` and `CONTRIBUTING.md` both referred to "the same gate CI runs" while no CI configuration was tracked anywhere: every gate this tree has built was opt-in and local. `tools/ci/presubmit.sh` is now what both a developer and a workflow run — tiers 0–2, rustfmt and clippy over the graph, the per-architecture lint held to its baseline, and one architecture's boot, in 14 seconds warm against `docs/lifecycle/02`'s thirty-minute budget. `tools/ci/continuous.sh` runs the full tier-3 matrix post-merge. The logic is in the scripts because `docs/lifecycle/02` ("Developer Experience") asks that the local presubmit be the same execution as CI pre-merge, and that only stays true without anybody checking if both run one file. **It found three failures on its first run** — two rustfmt diffs dating from D186's SDK split, and unused imports in D189's new crate — all of which `bazel build //... --config=lint` had been reporting since, and which a verification habit of `grep -cE '^error'` never saw, because rustfmt prints `ERROR:` and `Diff in` and clippy's summary line is the only one that starts lower-case. **What is NOT proven:** most of what `docs/lifecycle/02` asks of CI. Tier 4 needs hardware (D34) and the fuzzing fleet needs infrastructure (D12), so neither runs; the release-candidate lane — tier 5, the reproducible-rebuild check, the ABI diff, the budget report — does not exist; and the workers are GitHub-hosted rather than hermetic and network-isolated, so build inputs are pinned but the machine is not. | The lanes above as their subjects land; hermetic workers with the remote cache `.bazelrc` already reserves a place for. |

| D192 | **Two more drivers converted, and the sweep stopped there by direction.** `usb-storage` (776 → 703 lines) and `gpu-driver` (849 → 747) name no syscall now: their `SYS_*` constants, their hand-rolled `ChannelMsgArgs` encoding, the `patch_args`/`kernel_u32` volatile helpers that read what the kernel wrote into an args block, and their receive/reply-continue loops are the SDK's, and what is left is USB mass storage and virtio-gpu. `gpu-driver` also stops forming a pointer to its own DMA pages — `with_page` calls `Machine::with_dma`, which is what makes a driver's use of a page something `//userspace/sdk`'s harness (D179, D180) can watch at all. One thing did **not** transfer mechanically: the SDK's `Dma` names the two addresses `va` and `device_address`, where the driver's local type called the second one `phys`, and the SDK deliberately derives no `Default`, because a zeroed page is not a page. The rename is the point of the seam and the array of not-yet-allocated framebuffer pages now says what it means by an empty entry. **The remaining nine drivers are not converted**, and this is a decision rather than a pause: the seam is demonstrated on five of twelve (`crypto`, `usb-hid`, `usb-storage`, `gpu`, plus what `sdk-sim` covers), which is enough to hold the shape, and D188's list of what the other nine would need from `Platform` stands as the record of the work not done. **What is NOT proven:** that the two converted drivers' serial output is unchanged in full. `gpu_boot_aarch64` passes and its claim keys match, which is the contract D184 made testable; a line-by-line diff of the kind run for `usb` was not run here. | Each remaining driver converted when it is touched for its own reasons, rather than in a sweep — and `Platform` grown one method per change, with the driver that needed it as the argument for its shape. |

| D193 | **The 0.70–0.90 band, read one function at a time — one joined, one refused, one split in half.** `irq_complete` looked like a 39-line pair and was not one: AArch64 re-armed **every** line of the device, RISC-V 64 re-armed the first. Everything above the controller write is now `kcore::dispatch::resolve_irq_lines` — the authority check and the lines themselves — and each port keeps only its GIC or PLIC write. That closes a latent defect rather than tidying two copies: no RISC-V 64 device declares an extra line today, so nothing was observably wrong, but `MAX_EXTRA_IRQS` is `kcore`'s and not a port's, so the first multi-queue controller that port grew would have had a queue go quiet with nothing saying so. The line buffer is now `&mut [u32; MAX_IRQ_LINES]`, because `intids_of_object` fills a caller's buffer and *stops when it ends* — for a short buffer that is a silently dropped line, and a fixed-size parameter is a caller that cannot be given one. `process_space_check` is the opposite verdict: **three functions with one name, and they stay three.** The narrative is shared, but the user program is entered by each port's assembly, its trap reported through each port's statics, and the teardown frame count fixed by each port's page-table geometry — a joined version takes every one of those as a closure and is a scaffold, not a check. Reading them did find a real defect: ARM 32's teardown asserted `> before`, which is exactly the "at least" form RISC-V 64's own comment records as having let a deliberately broken `free_tables` sail through. It asserts `== 11` now, derived from the layout (every address in the second gigabyte: one root, one level-2, one level-3 per 2 MiB region touched — 6 for A, 5 for B) and *then* confirmed against the machine, in that order. `driver_giveup_check` split: the spawn, the hook and the address-space dance stay per port; step 7 — the failure policy, the quarantine, the closing lifecycle record and the two assertions — is `boot_checks::apply_giveup_policy` and `driver_giveup_verdict`, because it is the shared supervisor's policy and two ports deciding it separately is two ports that can end the same ladder differently. **Four doc comments were attached to the wrong function**, found by a doc block extractor walking backwards into a second one: `mmio_of_object`'s had drifted 1,500 lines up onto `device_set_mmio_irq`, a stale near-duplicate sat on `device_add_mmio_irq`, `driver_rebind_check`'s was orphaned when a struct was inserted between them, and — **mine** — D189's extraction moved `device_events_check` to `//kernel/boot-checks` and left its doc behind in the AArch64 port, glued onto the next function. **What is NOT proven:** that these four are all of them. The same signature — a doc paragraph opening straight after a completed sentence with no `///` separator — matches 107 places tree-wide, and spot-reading says most are ordinary style rather than drift. Separating them is the deferred comment sweep (F32/F33), not this. | The 107 candidates read and the real ones fixed; and a tier-0 gate for the class, if one can be written that does not cry wolf 100 times. |

| D194 | **The kernel stopped naming userspace, and a probe caught three false verification readings.** Five kernel BUILD files carried 31 dependency edges on `//userspace/*` image crates, and three `main.rs` files carried a `#[cfg(has_x)] fn y_elf()` / `#[cfg(not) fn y_elf()` pair per program — 31 pairs, differing only in a symbol. Which programs a machine boots with is composition, not kernel: the kernel is what userspace is built *against*, and a kernel package naming `//userspace/gpu-driver` points the dependency the wrong way. The list is `//components` now, one `tessera_image_components` call per machine, and each kernel names one label. The accessors are generated from that same list, so the list and the code cannot disagree. **Six cfg flags became one** — `has_ring3_host`, `has_nvme`, `has_ring3_driver`, `has_root_task`, `has_device_manager` and `has_blk_probe` existed *solely* to gate those accessors, which is checkable rather than asserted: 48 + 6 + 8 uses across the three ports, and every one of them was an accessor. The image crates' visibility moved from the kernel packages to `//components` alone, which is a tightening. **It is deliberately not called a manifest**: `docs/architecture/01`'s component manifest is a runtime object carrying capabilities, restart policy and budgets, and putting two different things under one word is how a word stops meaning anything. **One reference was not an accessor**, and it mattered: the certificate encoder hashed `crypto_driver_image::CRYPTO_DRIVER_ELF` directly. Because it named the crate, a build without that crate did not compile — loud, but only by accident; routing it through the composition makes such a build possible, and `sha256(&[])` is a digest of nothing wearing a certificate that says it measured a driver. It is refused now. **What is NOT proven, and what three readings claimed before a probe checked:** the kernels are **not** byte-identical. The three that changed compile against a different crate graph and differ; the two untouched ones are identical. Before that was established, "ALL 7 BYTE-IDENTICAL" was printed three times against **stale artifacts** — `bazel-bin/kernel/kernel-aarch64/kernel-aarch64_bin` is not what the boot check consumes (it consumes `kernel-aarch64_image_bin`, under a transitioned config), so it was never rebuilt and its hash never moved. What settled it was a probe: change a string the boot prints, confirm the hash changes, and only then trust the comparison. What *is* established is the invariant that matters — booting both AArch64 images under the same QEMU gives 74 serial lines differing in one place, the context-switch perf line, which is timing. Also unproven: that the cargo fallback compiles, because no bare-metal Rust target is installed on this machine. | The remaining two ports folded in when they grow ring-3 programs; and a helper for artifact comparison that takes its paths from `bazel cquery` rather than from `bazel-bin`, so the stale reading above is not available to make again. |

| D195 | **The kernel's sizing became a surface, and it is not ISL.** Twenty-seven `pub const MAX_…` declarations sat in the sixteen `kcore` modules whose tables they size. Each was right where it was, and together they were a configuration surface nobody could see: no list of what a machine can be tuned to, no bound saying which values are sane, and no way to build the same kernel two sizes without editing source. They are `config/kernel.config` now — the number and the reasoning that fixed it, moved together — and each module re-exports its own, so `kcore::process::MAX_PROCESSES` still resolves and no call site changed. **The plan said ISL and the plan was wrong.** `docs/api/03`'s schema language defines *wire* boundaries and has no construct for a defaulted, ranged scalar; adding one would change a language whose job is ABI compatibility, for values that never cross a wire. `//tools/kconfig` reads this format and nothing else does. **A refusal, never a clamp:** a profile asking for a value outside a tunable's declared range does not build, because clamping would produce a kernel sized differently from the one that was asked for and nothing downstream could tell. Both build paths run the same program over the same declaration — Bazel's `//config:values` genrule and `kernel/kcore/build.rs` — bridged on `kconfig_bazel` exactly as the ISL bindings are on `isl_bazel` (D24/D54), so a host unit test and a release image cannot be sized differently. The one difference between the two outputs is `#![no_std]`, which is a named `Form` on the emitter rather than a file that gets post-processed. `//tools/checks:config_test` holds three ways this can quietly stop being true: a tunable nothing re-exports, a number that drifts back into a module, and a profile that has stopped resolving — the last mattering because `kconfig` only refuses the profile something is *built with*, and a profile nobody builds is one nobody finds out about until they need it. **Two defects were found by inverting rather than by reading.** The redeclaration scan was passing a *path string* to the matcher instead of the file's contents, so it matched nothing and the gate reported clean on a tree that violated it — caught only because the inversion that should have failed did not. And rustfmt sorts a contiguous run of `pub use` and leaves doc comments where they sit, which silently attached each number's reasoning to a different number; the re-exports are separated by blank lines so they are not one group. **What is NOT proven:** that any profile other than `default` produces a working kernel. `minimal` demonstrates that the surface is real — it emits different constants and its out-of-range values are refused — and its own comment says the boot checks are not expected to pass under it, because the AArch64 machine runs 24 ring-3 programs and it has no room for them. Nothing builds a kernel with it. Also: the kernels are **not** byte-identical (`kcore` links a crate it did not before); the values are unchanged, and booting both AArch64 images gives 74 serial lines differing only in the context-switch perf line, which is timing. | A profile that is built and booted, so "configurable" is proven by a second working machine rather than by a refusal; and the remaining sizing — the ports' own constants, which are per-machine rather than per-kernel — brought in when a second machine needs them to differ. |

Owner for all: mjchandra@gmail.com.
